## K: keel–KiwiRail

### keel
 
 <i>n.</i> a ship

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Mr. Clegg told me he'd been a cook on a boat, but now he couldn't get a keel. <i>(1946,That Summer,55</i>)

### keen
 
 <i>v.</i> to mourn, tangi (Maori not irish)

>  <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,47</i>)

### keeny-seeny
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> They were unable to assess our games because around puberty amnesia had settled over their memories, expunging all knowledge of marbles, hopscotch, skipping, kingaseeny, shanghais and cigarette substitutes.<i>ibid.</i> 41 Bullrush involved as many of the school as possible, all at one end of the paddock, one person in the middle. At the cry of 'bullrush' we all ran and he had to catch one of us. it was also called Charlie across the water and kingaseeny. <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,39</i>)

>  <b>LOCKE</b> if he caught a runner, the King would pat his head three times and say, "King of Seni, one two three, you're the only man for me. King, king, king!" <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,80</i>)

>  Beyond that ceremony came trials at "Kingaseenie" which were essentially tests for discovering speed, swerving and collaring material for the football team. <i>(Aug 15, 1928,NZ Free Lance,35</i>)

>  <b>LORETZ</b> We made bows from supple-jacks, arrows from toi-toi, played king-a-seeny.. and did tricks on the horizontal bar. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,6</i>)

>  Kerikeri School banned it [sc. bullrush] (they called it king asini) three years ago. Too many children were getting their clothes ripped, too many were getting hurt. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,NZ Education Review,15</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Others wanted "Prisoners' Base" or "Chivvy," "Brewer." "Egg-in-the-hat," "Duck Stone." "Red Rover." or "Kingy-seeny."... "Kingy-seeney" it was to them and they did not care whether that had a meaning or not.<i>ibid.</i> 115 Hard knocks they revelled in, and this rough and tumble Kingy-seeny gave them these galore. <i>ibid.</i> 125 "King-seeny, one, two, three / You're the very man for me." <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,84</i>) [NOTES: Check spelling]

>  <b>CONDON</b> A popular game was what we in Hurleyville called Keeney-Seeney. it has a variety of other names. One person would call a boy's name out of the whole group and try to catch him and pat three times on his back before he got to the other end. if caught he would form part of the catching team. if not caught the whole school would go to the other end - any caught would form part of the catching team. The game would end with almost the whole school forming the catchers and trying to catch a rampaging All Black type. At times a rather rough game. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,25</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> He liked nearly all the boys [at primary school in early 1930s] ... They played cock-fighting and keeny-seeny, and stuffed grass down the necks of girls who gave cheek or got in the road. <i>(1965,Power of Joy,133</i>)

### keep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary keeper.

>  <b>DUPPA</b> .. Sheep should be passed on to isolated Hill and kept there until shearing if the keep will stand it. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,136</i>) [NOTES: in St Leonards Station Journal Principal Work]

### keep (the) boundary
 
 <i>phr.</i> to patrol a (usu. unfenced) station boundary (See [cf. boundary keeper](bla-bri.md#boundary-keeper),[boundary-keeping](bla-bri.md#boundary-keeping))

>  Left Ch.Ch. i had next door to an offer of 300[pounds] p.a. from Government to keep the boundary of Canterbury and Otago but i could not accept ... <i>(Jan 29, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,120,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Wire fences ... save the cost of a shepherd to `keep the boundary.' <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,203</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... i did not think you would have got into such a scrape when i sent you to keep the boundary. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,170</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> keep (a, the) boundary to patrol an often unfenced boundary of a sheep-station. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,103,1954 ed.</i>)

>  Had dinner and started after a [147] mob of sheep that had broken away from the man that was keeping a boundary. <i>(Oct 6, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,146,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... Gilbert decided to send Mike ... , as he had the bullock dray already loaded ... to take over to the out-station, and both ... thought that Mike could be trusted to keep the boundary in the mean time; ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,144</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> was one man's duty to `keep boundary'; that is, to sit at the mouth of the creek and keep all the sheep from going back - a lazy man's job, and one not sought. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,15</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The open places [on the run] were guarded by shepherds, `keeping boundary,' who as well as they were able, prevented the sheep from straying. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,103,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The chief station work in those days [c1847] consisted of milking, keeping boundary on the sheep and hunting for lost cattle. <i>(1975,Early Canterbury Runs,9/267</i>)

### keep a dog and bark oneself
 
 <i>phr.</i> to employ somebody and yet do the work oneself

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>... to keep a dog and bark yourself</i> [a 20th cent. phrase] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

### keep boundary
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to watch that stock do not wander across a boundary, or away from a specified grazing locality.

>  <b>CHUDLEiGH</b> Had dinner and started after a mob of sheep that had broken away from the man who was keeping a boundary. <i>(Oct 6, 1864,Chudleigh Diary</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> if the fog came after a couple of days mustering on a big block, we might keep boundary for a day, but if the next day was still foggy, we generally chucked it ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,121</i>)

>  <b>MCKENZiE</b> Alexander kept boundary of the sheep while John took three pack-horses laden with fencing materials to the fence line. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,113</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Next morning - Christmas morning - we mustered over to Crimea Creek, leaving one man to keep boundary at Cat Creek. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,18</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> When quite young, the girls of the family used to 'keep boundary'; they were posted at different points on the run and kept the sheep from wandering over the boundaries. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,180</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The next day the head of the Alma was mustered and two men kept the boundary. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,72</i>)

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> The Sheep inspector asked George if he knew of any steady man who knew scab on sheep, as he wanted such a man to keep the provincial boundary between Hawke's Bay and Wellington. <i>(Jul 7, 1872,Balfour Diaries</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The head shepherd swung the billy; after a brew he would climb on to the top and keep boundary in case sheep were drifting back. <i>(1949,High Country Days,166</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> This lonely life had an appeal, because some men kept boundary for as long as thirty years. <i>(1965,High Endeavour,46</i>)

### keep off the grass
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> keep off the grass: go carefully. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>)

### keep off the grass and let my wife see the races
 
 <i>phr.</i> an obsolesc. catchphrase said to a person blocking one's view. ca 1880 W. H. Orsman.

### keep out
 
<b>1</b> <i>exclam.</i> (Rural) a command to a heading dog that needs to recast its run.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Keep out: command to a heading dog to recast it wider around its sheep. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,46</i>)

### keep the billy boiling
 
 <i>phr.</i> to keep the fire warm; `hold the fort'; keep things going [ca1940 Havelock and (occasionally) Wellington 1951 Wellington, phrase used in hotel by man leaving a pub-school temporarily: `Keep the billy boiling; I'll be back in a couple of shakes.] (See [cf. gen. English keep the pot boiling](../G#gen.-english-keep-the-pot-boiling))

### keep the fire warm
 
 <i>phr.</i> Said when one is temporarily leaving a person or group in the senses `keep things going, i'll be back; hold the fort; keep the billy (pot) boiling'. [ca1950 Wellington]

### keep the road
 
 <i>phr.</i> to keep watch on the road (ahead).

>  ... he had to go ahead to keep the road. <i>(Aug 6, 1866,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: Times [Maungatapu Murders]]

### keep your wool on
 
 <i>exclam.</i> keep your hair on

>  <b>MASON</b> TAHi, <i>disgruntled</i>: Keep your wool on. ... All i want's a good feed .... [sic] <i>(1969,Awatea,45,1978 ed.</i>)

### keeper
 
 <i>n.</i> on a dog-collar.

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> But he tied her [a dog] up one night and omitted to put the surplus end of the strap in the keeper; and she succeeded in undoing the buckle ... <i>(1970,Tat,55</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal that survives a culling draft and remains on a property.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The keepers were then taken to the woolshed to be crutched. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,110</i>)

>  Thinking about a hot and hearty breakfast (though probably without heart care approval) is a great help when trying to encourage another group of young keepers to stay on the teat instead of blundering around the shed sucking on everything else. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,96</i>)

>  i'm supposed to train 10 new keepers this morning ... <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,97</i>)

### keeper cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cattle beast which temporarily looks after a calf for another member of the herd.

>  <b>HALL</b> early in the morning some cows will leave their calves with a keeper cow. Maybe half a dozen calves will stay with one cow, while the mothers will go off to graze .. Usually a cow will go to retrieve a calf from a keeper cow as soon as it hears a dog barking ... <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,70</i>)

### keeping
 
 <i>adj.</i> of fruit

>  Prime Baking and Keeping Apples. <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,4</i>)

### keeping-lamb
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe lamb that is retained by a farmer to breed from [rather than sold for slaughter as a fat lamb.] (See [carryover](c-chi.md#carryover); [holdover](hea-hom.md#holdover))

>  The keeping-lambs are crutched, branded, dipped and placed out. <i>(Jul 20, 1920,NZ Journal of Agriculture,8</i>)

### keeps
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRAME</b> i played ringie and holie. i played funs and keeps. i had pee-wees and bully taws and changers that weren't made of glass mind you. And i won nearly every game because the kids were so dazzled by the look of my stinkers. ... They came for miles to play marbles with me ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Treasure']

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> it is important to distinguish these `Action-Terms' of marbles from the innumerable merely descriptive marble terms which have no effect on the course of the action - terms such as Ringer, in, Smug, Dead Stick, Skun, Skinned, Sported off, Scat, Cannons, Dubs, Kills, Funs, Keeps, Pink. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/770</i>)

>  <i> keeps, for adv</i>. for good, especially in marbles and cricket. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>KEiTH & MAiN</b> [Photo caption] Marbles became `in' or `out' capriciously but someone always knew the rules of the various games - `holey,' `liney' and, seen here, `ringey' - which could be played for `funs' or `keeps.' <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### keg
 
 <i>n.</i> a hollow-barrelled tree

>  KEG Hollow barrelled tree or log. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a barrel of beer, or its contents; thence, a container of beer. [ca 1945-48 (Undergraduates) The keg's cut, =`the booze is finished'. There's a good drop of keg going tonight]

>  `Kegging,' i.e., that beastly habit of boozing out of a keg, is peculiarly a New Zealand habit; ... <i>(May 14, 1910,NZ Truth,255/5/6</i>)

>  The big fight is on about the drink. The prohibitionists are circulating petitions for a poll to abolish kegs, casks, demijohns, square-faces, pewters, and all other appurtenances of `joy-juice'. <i>(Sep 2, 1918,Quick March,15</i>)

>  i thought it was the keg. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,15</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> When the bottles are empty/ When the keg runs sour/ ... When the tote goes broke/ <i>(1950,imaginary islands,26</i>) [NOTES: (`Secular Litany')]

>  Often his clique meets at a beer party. Book-titles apart, his party is not so different from the Saturday night boozeroo in the Sydenham side-street with the keg on the kitchen-sink. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/224</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

>  Every time some New Zealander starts to tell me (usually round the keg in the kitchen) what sports-loving ... types he and his countrymen are ... <i>(Aug 1952,Here & Now,43</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Kiwi led father to the keg [at the party]. <i>ibid.</i> 108 Sandy ... sometimes took her off to his wild proletarian parties in slummy Freeman's Bay, where kegs flowed and fights flared. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,26,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOGAN</b> We were waiting at the turn-off/ For the Pack horse with the keg,/ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,11</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> Just keep Harry away from the keg, that's all. <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,252</i>)

>  i had a schottische with the tart from the Butcher's,/ Got stuck for a waltz with the constable's wife;/ i had a beer from the keg on the cream-truck,/ And the cop had one, too, you can bet your life. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),259</i>) [NOTES: (Cape `Down the Hall on Saturday Night)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The clubhouse after the game. The tickets punched for the beer, the hot sausages, the keg, ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,174</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> The flagon-parties, the crates of half-gees on the sink, men in singlets queued up at the keg; ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,110</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Plenty of garrulous half-shot but willing hands, generous with advice and counter-advice, had all the wool stacked safely in the store by 1 am, when they turned to even more willingly and cut the keg. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,14</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>keg</i> beer; from a barrel of beer. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,65</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> And balancing a jar on the back of his bike, Plodder pedalled into the clearing ... Plodder took the cork out of the keg. [Time set in 1930s, in invercargill dry area.] <i>(1978,Travelling Man,45</i>)

>  You can't see the keg... This big joker is standing in front of it. <i>(Sep 27, 1943,NZEF Times,iii/117 : 6</i>)

>  This then either goes on the bar or towards something like a keg. <i>(Sep 10, 2003,Capital Times,17</i>)

>  He put the keg in the pushchair and let young hopeful walk. <i>(Jun 21, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),2</i>)

### keg party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  So-called keg parties became popular. Lads would pool their money and send one person to a nearby licensed district to buy as much booze as the money would cover. Then they would proceed to drink the lot in one sitting. <i>(Dec 2005,NZ Geographic,72</i>)

>  The people who had rented his basement flat in Auckland took advantage of his absence to hold noisy keg parties. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,17</i>)

### keg race
 
 <i>n.</i> a university students' drinking contest

>  Firstly, if more people had been moderate in their drinking and went to the Ball for a formal evening instead of a Keg Race, then the alcohol would have lasted past 12pm. <i>(Aug 11, 1986,Salient,15</i>)

### keg-drinker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Some of the old keg-drinkers would find that sort of pansy <i>(Jul 24, 1944,NZEF Times,iV/161 : 4</i>)

### kegged
 
 <i>adj.</i>

### kegger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The prosecution of `keggers' in and around invercargill has led to the adoption of the saying, `No rest for the beery.' it suits a `dry' area. <i>(Aug 12, 1911,NZ Truth,320/1/5</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> The police kept a tolerant, if suspicious, eye on the "keggers". <i>(1967,ink on My Fingers,174</i>)

### kegging
 
 <i>n.</i> keg-drinking

>  `Kegging,' i.e., that beastly habit of boozing out of a keg, is peculiarly a New Zealand habit; <i>(May 14, 1910,NZ Truth,255/5/6</i>)

### keha
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, keha = semen http://www.learningmedia.co.nz/nz/online/ngata/m2edictionary]

>  One takes the meaning offspring or keha as the first meaning and father or pa follows. <i>(2005,North & South,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a flea

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 137. <i>Pulex.</i> Keha, or flea. Polack 1. c. i., p.321. Tuiau. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. ... The natives say that fleas were introduced by the Europeans, and for that reason call them sometimes `he pakeha nohinohi', the little stranger. - <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/291</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Adam and White) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/291]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> Netta warmed to Shirley immediately, especially when she heard her refer to pakeha customers as kehas (fleas). They made numerous jokes: "Will you be quick with the Keha's oysters, please?' `Look out for the keha with the moustache - he bites.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,218</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> wild turnip

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ke|ha, s. A turnip (exotic). <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,162</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Not a spot on the earth lay waste; many parts teeming with the keha [e,a-acute] or wild turnip whose yellow flower on stalks of six feet in height, covered the distance, as far as the eye could discern, and emitted a pleasing fragrance. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/134</i>)

### kehe
 
 <i>n.</i> marblefish (See [also keke](kee-kiw.md#keke); [cf. ngehe](new-no.md#ngehe))

>  <b>KOHERE</b> We can catch kehe whenever we choose. [fn] Kehe, granite trout. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,127</i>)

>  HAPLODACTYLUS MEANDRATUS ... Native name - Kehei. <i>(1875,TrNZi,7/241</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> GRANiTE TROUT (<i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i>). ... [36] it is called by the Natives kehei. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> Sometimes kehe are caught, seized with the hands, and lifted quickly out of the water. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,63</i>)

>  The luxuriant growth of the kohuwai seaweed brought the well-conditioned kehe up the rocky channels of the favoured reefs. <i>ibid.</i> 599 Poles of manuka are also used to make the framework of the trap-net for the kehe fish. <i>(1926,TrNZi,58/598</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net... The kehe, nanua, and kopipiro, are believed by the Maori to live on vegetable matter,... <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  <b>FiRTH</b> The <i>kehe</i> ... could be taken almost all the year round, as also the crayfish.. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,67</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> However, a scoop net termed <i>kupenga koko kehe</i> was used in rocky channels on the East Coast to catch <i>kehe (Haplodactylus meandratus) ... </i> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,215</i>)

### kehua
 
 <i>n.</i> a ghost or evil presence [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HYDE</b> i met one little nun who quite definitely believed in the "kehu" - an agile sort of Maori banshee, who is seen perching on the gateposts immediately before a death. <i>(1934,Journalese,169</i>)

>  And in the evening the kehua keen in the trees, or is it the dying westerly wind? <i>(Feb 5, 2002,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  ... this haunting spirit, or phantom, <i>(Kehua,)</i> which haunted its former place of residence, when in the body, ... differed widely from the sensible intellectual spirit <i>(Wairua)</i>, which had departed to the <i>reinga</i> and which was not feared. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/42</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> if one met a <i>kehua</i> (ghost) you had only to offer it some cooked food and it would instantly disappear, for ghosts, like all supernatural beings, dislike cooked food. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,426</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> ... there is a good deal of what present-day folk call superstition among the older natives in respect to the <i>kehua</i>, or Maori ghost. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,98</i>)

>  <i>kehua</i>, ghost, particularly baneful if it is one of yourself: Germ. doppelganger. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: (J.C.Andersen)]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kehua</i>: ghost, spirit. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>JENKiN</b> To the Maori ...the soul or <i>wairua</i> of a man was believed to depart to the spirit world on the death of the body ...sometimes the spirit did return, world on the death of the body ...sometimes the spirit did return, and these ghosts or <i>kehua</i> were greatly loved. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,96</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kehua <i>ghost </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Kehua trap [title] <i>(1982,Moeraki Conversations,16</i>) [NOTES: The Silences Between]

>  <b>HULME</b> Kehua = ghosts <i>(1983,Bone People, The,469 [p] 445</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> (Marama, Kaikoura) So Toua and Poua would say to us, take a fairy cake, crumble it and throw it over your head, to keep all the kehuas and spirits off. <i>(1985,i'm Ninety-five,101</i>)

>  <b>MiTCALFE</b> `You sure you're Winston, you look more like a kehua to me!. [fn] kehua: ghost <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,48</i>)

>  No one told me that the dusties collected the rubbish around the back. Early. And no one told the dusties that a white robed flailer was not a kehua, a ghost. <i>(Nov 8, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> i am not a person who sees kehua. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,65</i>)

>  Each car is tooting to the old pub as they pass it... i toot, as i pass, to mihi to the old kehua that haunts the hallways of that old place at night. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,70</i>)

>  Then he is filled with remorse and haunted by kehua. <i>(May 12, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  Making the film was not important to her and she suspected that kehua (ghosts) lived in the camera. <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),104</i>)

>  The girl at the further end was a "Kehu" - that is, a Maori albino, meaning by this term a person with auburn hair, about the colour of a dull chestnut. <i>(Dec 16, 1897,Otago Witness,29</i>)

### kei te pai
 
 <i>phr.</i> it's all right

>  "Kei te pai, dear," she said when we rang to apologise. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 11</i>)

### Keith Hay
 
 <i>n.</i> firm producing kit-set and tailor-made houses

>  <b>SHAW</b> A rough Northland farm, macrocarpa around a Keith Hay home, 400 acres of slopes, ... <i>ibid.</i> 61 For the few weeks of spring's lambing Barry brought his family up from the brick and tile house ... and moved into the Keith Hay. <i>(1983,Longfin,57</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> That's them then, the Ohern family, with an inset photo for Terry, who's over in Derby Street playing the Keith Hay-home-maker with wife Tessa, thin as an X-ray plate, even after two plump daughters. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,25</i>)

### keke
 
 <i>n.</i> marblefish (See [also kehe](kee-kiw.md#kehe))

>  APLODACTYLUS MEANDRATUS (Richardson). <i>Marble-fish; Keke</i>. Secured in deep water in the vicinity of kelp, and is sometimes taken in mullet-nets. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/115</i>)

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Haplodactylus meandratus </i>Richardson. MARBLEFiSH; KEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,37,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GRANiTE TROUT or MARBLE FiSH (KEKE) <i>Dactylosargus arctidens maeandratus</i> Richardson The common names of this fish are derived from the resembalance of the skin to marble or granite. The ground colour is black, mottled or waved with slaty-grey. At one time this fish was sold in Auckland and Wellington as Maori Chief but i do not know why this should be, as the true Maori Chief is quite different and not at all like the Granite Trout. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,248,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE MARBLE FiSH <i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i> (Richardson) ... <i>Maori name: KEKE ... The Marble Fish, or Granite trout as it is sometimes called, derives its name from the colour, which is black, mottled with slaty grey, resembling the markings of granite or marble. </i> <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,112</i>)

>  Marblefish or granite trout .. .. keke .. .. <i>Apodactylus meandratus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/708</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> MARBLEFiSH ... <i>Other names: Aplodactylus meandratus</i>; granite trout; keke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,246</i>)

>  <b>NATURSCH</b> Family APLODACTYLiDAE The marble-fish or keke, <i>Haplodactylus meandratus</i>, grows to thirty inches, and is found throughout New Zealand. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> MARBLEFiSH [<i>Aplodactylus meandratus</i>] ... Another name is marbletrout, while the Maori keke is still in use in some northern areas. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>MARBLEFiSH (Keke) Aplodactylus arctidens</i> ... The body colour ... is a uniform olive brown, finely mottled with white on the body and fins. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,240,1984 ed.</i>)

### kekeno
 
 <i>n.</i> a seal, esp. the NZ fur seal [Maori]; also formerly called sea bear

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> Phoca, or seal Cakenno <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/329</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Kek|eno, s. A seal fish. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,162</i>)

>  seal - ki'kkano [kekeno] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 285 (|= macron precedes) seal, ke|kkano [kekeno] c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112 seal - ki'kkano [kekeno]]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Kekino is their native name. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/183]

>  <b>HUNT</b> [The Morioris] would venture down ... to barter their kekune or seal-skins for such articles as might attract their fancy. <i>(1866,Travels in NZ,34</i>)

>  ...a seal or kekeno colony now provides a stench for the cape to live up to. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/58</i>)

>  <b>STARK</b> seal - kikkano [kekeno] <i>(1986,Journal,112</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1827-28 BOULTBEE; grave accent on "i" of "kikk"]

### kekerengu
 
 <i>n.</i> a beetle [<i>Platyzosteria novaeseelandiae</i>]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|eke r|ehu; A beetle. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,162</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The most disgusting insects in nature exist among the spear-grass, called toi toi, of the swamps and plains; it is called the kikararu [a's-acute]. its odour is disgustingly offensive, and it is often found in rush or other dwelling-houses. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/320</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>Kika</i>[acute]<i>raru. Polack</i>. inhabits New Zealand (Spear-grass). <i>Polack</i> 1., p.329. `The most disgusting insect in nature.' <i>Polack. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/281</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Adam and White) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/281]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> one kind of Black Beetle is worthy of particular notice on account of its troublesome nature, a known by the Qpellation of Kekerere, by the natives. if molested throws off the most disgusting smell imaginable, but not a venomous insect. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,111,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Raupo cottages always with Kekeriru, a large black wood bug, larger than the English black beetle and which when crushed emits a most horrid stench. <i>(May 29, 1855,Auckland Journals,157,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The large nocturnal beetle (<i>Prionoplus reticularis</i> \{Huhu\}, ... the Kekereru or fetid bug, the large bush <i>Cicada</i> ... all contribute to the support of this bird; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,75,1888 2nd edn 1/130 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The centipede (<i>were</i>), the caterpillar (<i>whe</i>), the Maori-bug (<i>kekerengu</i>), the birds <i>torea</i> ... and the ground lark (<i>hioi</i>: Anthus N.Z.), a sacred bird, were all supposed to have been brought in the canoes. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,181</i>)

>  <b>GASCOYNE</b> The ground on which our camp was situated had been bush, and had plenty of logs and stumps on it, and the camp swarmed with `Maori bugs,' as the men called them. The Maori's name for these horrible insects is <i>kekerengu</i>. They are about one inch and a half long and resemble a black cockroach in appearance. <i>(1916,Soldiering in NZ,115</i>) [NOTES: [reference 1880's Parihaka]]

>  Kekerengu, he says, means "black beetle". Kekerengu is at once a tearoom, petrol station and post office - closed, of course. <i>(Dec 18, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### kekerewai
 
 <i>n.</i> manuka beetle

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A certain larva (<i>huhu</i>) found in rotten timber was much relished by the Maoris; and they esteemed as food the small green beetle (<i>kekerewai</i>) found on the tea-tree (<i>manuka</i>) shrub. it was called as a compliment Te Manu a Rehua, `the bird of Rehua' - Rehua was a god. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,109</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The small green manuka beetle, known as <i>kekerewai</i>, was mashed up with raupo pollen and steamed in small baskets as a type of bread. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,10</i>)

### keketerehe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Olearia chathamica, T.Kirk.</i>] CHATHAM iSLANDS: in swampy places on higher parts of the island ... <i>Keketerehe. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,281</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>Olearia chathamica</i>. ... Chatham Tree-daisy. KEKETEREHE. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,67</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>OLEARiA CHATHAMiCA, keketerehe.</i> A few species of <i>Olearia</i> have purple disc florets - <i>O. angustifolia</i>, the teteaweka of southern Southland and Stewart island, and, in the Chathams, ... <i>O. chathamica</i> on drier ridges. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,130</i>)

### kelly
 
 <i>n.</i> a (Kelly) axe

>  <b>CARR</b> ... for many years to come the `whoof' of the bushman, as he sinks the `Kelly', or the `Plumb' (two favourite brands of American axes, the latter especially) into ... rimu ...will be heard. <i>(1913,Country Work,29</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ...here again this has been a story of the axe and the Condon boys were literally brought up with a `Kelly' in their hands. cf P: 2. An axe: Aust: B. `on the kelly' engaged in axework ex the famous axeman. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,71</i>)

>  <i>n. (slang). </i>an axe (from maker's name) whence to swing <i>kelly|</i> to wield an axe. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kelly n. (Sl.) axe;on the kelly,</i> engaged in axe work. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

### Kelly axe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>iRELAND</b> Along the walls of his room he would store his Kelly axes and his shovels and spades. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,55</i>)

### Kelly Gang
 
 <i>n.</i> [also ca1926 (Ruth Mason) A group of Auckland businessmen]

>  <b>GUSTAFSON</b> The major protagonists at the 1935 election, however, were Labour and the United-Reform coalition, which had merged formally in May 1935 into a National Political Federation, becoming in 1936, after the election, the New Zealand National Party. The `Kelly Gang' of Eliot and Ernest Davis and Oliver Nicholson in Auckland worked hard to prevent conservative notables from defecting from National to the Democrats, even to the extent of offering jobs and directorships. <i>(1986,Cradle to Grave,168</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The notorious Kelly gang developed a fearsome reputation, holding up the Duntroon hotel and great western hotel at Kurow in the 1860s. At the Great Western, the audacious Kelly held the entire pub at gunpoint while ordering the cook to prepare his gang a meal. <i>(May 2001,NZ Geographic,51/46</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Of the 66 meat workers employed [at Petone] ... only 17 will stay ... six in the freezing chambers, two in the kelly gang, one in the rendering department, and eight watchmen. <i>(Nov 17, 1981,Press, The,1/5</i>)

>  Savings...had been made by a drop in overtime pay and putting 22 men on a `Kelly gang' late last year. They were taken off the killing chain and given other jobs around the [Wellington City] abattoir and they lost all overtime. <i>(Feb 10, 1983,Evening Post,26</i>)

### Kelly's eye
 
 <i>n.</i> a tramcar Numbered 111 (from the Housie or Bingo call).

### Kelly-gangish
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> The Otago fields were not so Kelly-gangish [as the Victorian], although a few rough necks did get the rope in the finish. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,22</i>)

### kelp
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Durvillaea utilis.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

### kelp bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  in his newly published history of surfing in New Zealand, Luke Williamson tells how early European visitors discovered coastal Maori tribes riding waves on canoes, kelp bags and logs. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  [Caption] A scene in connection with the kelp-bag industry at Kaka Point. <i>(Sep 28, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,36</i>)

>  <b>HULME & MORRiSON</b> Rimurapa is probably best known, among Maori, for its use in making poha-rimu, the big kelpbags that helped store muttonbirds between seasons. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,61</i>)

>  Those to land leapt overboard with an inflated kelp bag lashed beneath the chin. <i>(Sep 7, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> The <i>titi</i> is so fat, that the native mode of preserving it is to boil it down, and then to tie it up in its own oil, in kelp bags, formed of the large air vessels of common sea weed. <i>(Jan 10, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 10 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part iii (Church in the Colonies Viii) 10 ']

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The [Moriori] men to do the [bird] killing, killing waddy - a short club 18 inches long and 2 inches in diameter - strapped to the waist, and an inflated kelp bag lashed beneath the chin, leap into the sea ... <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 364</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 364]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> The kelp bag, the totara sheath and the flax wrapping [for mutton-birds] challenge our perfected technique of preserving and canning foods. <i>(1940,Rakiura,210</i>)

>  Out on line by door bull-kelp bags blown up and drying. Under the table finished pohas stacked. Birds in kelp bags with totara bark wrapping, looking fine in their new flax baskets. <i>(Apr 20, 1952,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1952 Apr 20 diary in 1982 Sansom in the Grip of an island 153]

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> We found them ... sitting on the steps inflating kelp bags. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,34</i>)

### kelp cod
 
 <i>n.</i> = rock cod

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, ROCK</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Simon made the acquaintance of barracouta, ling, trumpeter, and rig, and red cod, kelp cod, and rock cod. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,251</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] kelpcod 812 <i>(1987,Bibliography of NZ Fishes</i>)

### kelp fly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> Unfortunately he had forgotten to shut the windows and the floors in some rooms were strewn with kelp flies. (The normally inhabit the seaweed on the beaches, but when lights shine at night they fly noisily at the windows like hordes of moths. ...) <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,72</i>)

### kelp gull
 
 <i>n.</i> = black-backed gull

>  Dominican Gull <i>Larus dominicanus </i> LiCHTENSTEiN, 1823 ...The New Zealand population has increased this century. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karoro, southern black-backed gull, kelp gull.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,221</i>)

>  <i>kelp gull n</i> : a black-backed gull (<i>Larus dominicanus</i>) of the southern hemisphere <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1236</i>)

### kelp hen
 
 <i>n.</i> the weka, <i>Ocydromus fuscus.</i>

>  NO. A.89. - OCYDROMUS FUSCUS, <i>Du Bus</i>. Kelp-hen, Blackwood hen. it abounds in the many inlets and sounds of the south-west coast of this island. The only place where we noticed that it seemed shy was in Milford Sound. As soon as the tide begins to recede these dusky Rails come out on the shore to feed amongst the kelp. <i>(1873,TrNZi,6/152</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  OCYDROMUS FUSCUS, <i>Du Bus</i>. - Kelp-hen. in young birds the primaries are obscurely banded with rufous. These markings disappear after the first or second moult, and the rufous streaks on the upper surface appear to diminish as the bird gets older. <i>(1875,TrNZi,7/206</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  OCYDROMUS FUSCUS, <i>Du Bus</i>. - Kelp-hen. in young birds the primaries are obscurely banded with rufous. These markings disappear after the first or second moult, and the rufous streaks on the upper surface appear to diminish as the bird gets older. <i>(1875,TrNZi,7/206</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  <b>EVANS</b> [OED] These Rails are semi-nocturnal ... <i>Ocydromus fuscus</i> [of N.Z.] obtaining the name of Kelp-Hen from the stretches of sea-weed that it frequents. <i>(1889,Birds of New Zealand,247</i>)

>  <i>kelp hen n </i>: a weka of South island of New Zealand that feeds on marine animals and prob. represents a dark phase of a species (<i>Gallirallus australis</i>) though sometimes considered a separate species (<i>G. brachypterus</i>). <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1236</i>)

### kelp pepper
 
 <i>n.</i> condiment made from died algae

>  Kelp pepper is sold in elegant glass shakers as well as in refill packs... Needing soemthing to differentiate their product from imported kelp powders, the Beatties struck upon their name one evening at the dinner table when one of their children asked to be handed the kelp pepper. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

### kelp salmon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. The butter-fish, kelp-fish or kelp-salmon, as it is variously called, is very common in Otago Harbour. it is wrongly identified in my previous list as <i>Odax vittatus</i> Solander. <i>(1913,TrNZi,45/231</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  CORiDODAX PULLUS (Forster). <i>Butterfish; Marari.</i> Sold as `greenbone' or `kelp-salmon' in Dunedin. An outside fish secured in the vicinity of kelp around the northern coasts during most months of the year. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/117</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Forster). Greenbone (butterfish, kelp-fish, kelp-salmon). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/412</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GREENBONE, BUTTERFiSH, KELP SALMON, or KELPiE (MARARi) <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... The name kelp-fish is given on account of its habit of feeding on kelp or seaweed, and also because it likes to make its home among kelp. in such a habitat it has both food and a favourite home. Kelp-salmon is given for two reasons. The first part, kelp, is explained in the last paragraph, while the second half, salmon, is because the fish is not unlike a Salmon in shape, so much so that new chums who caught this fish have been under the impression they had caught the protected introduced Salmon, but of that later in this chapter. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,261</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> it is unfortunate that the Butterfish [<i>Coridodax pullus</i> ] has been given a variety of local names which have originated either from their habit of associating with kelp, their body form, or from the colour of their bones, as for example, Kelpfish, Kelp-salmon, Greenbone, and several other names not so easy to explain. Butterfish or Greenbone are possibly the more generally used names for this fish... <i>ibid.</i> 169 Butterfish Coridodax pullus Greenbone; Kelp Salmon; Kelpi; Kelpfish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,130</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> Once a highly-sought fish in southern waters where it [butterfish, <i>Coridodax</i>) was known as Kelp-salmon because a) it lived in the kelp and b) it bore at first glance a remarkable resemblance to a salmon. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] kelp salmon 1754 ... [index to common names 283-293] salmon, kelp 1754 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### kelpfish
 
 <i>n.</i> applied to various fish in various localities; see also kelpie.

>  (Black Stewart island) [=butterfish] We caught ... a larger number of Butter-fish and Kelp-fish; the former with hook and line in Kaipipi bay; the latter in our nets ... <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> in New Zealand, also called <i>Butter-fish</i>(q.v.), <i>Coridodax pullus</i>, Forst. in Tasmania, <i>Odax baleatus, </i>Cuv. and Val.; ... in Victoria, <i>Chironemus marmoratus</i> ... ...The name is also given in New Zealand to another fish, the <i>spotty</i> (q.v.). These fishes are all different from the Californian food fishes of the same name. [Aust quot. 1841]. <i>(1898,Austral English,246</i>)

>  <i>kelpfish n b</i> : any of several brilliantly colored percoid fishes of the family Odacidae found along the coasts of Australia and New Zealand - called also<i> rock whiting </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1236</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>BUTTERFiSH</i> (Greenbone, marari) <i>Odax pullus (Coridodax pullus, Odax vittatus) </i>... Small individuals have a markedly different colour pattern from that of the adults and until recently were thought to belong to a separate species named the kelpfish, [266] <i>Odax vittatus.</i> These young fish are yellow-brown with a line of white dashes running down each side ... [Butterfish] are an excellent eating fish, with delicate tasty flesh that prompted the name butterfish ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,265,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kelpfish n.</i> an olive-brown fish found in NZ coastal waters. OED <i>kelp</i> 4 the name given to several fishes on the Pacific coast of the U.S. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,410</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Butterfish</i> A small family (Odacidae) of Australasian shallow- water, weed dwelling and eating fishes, sometimes called kelpfishes or weed whitings. internationally the name butterfish is used for the family to which our warehou belong, but in New Zealand the name is so firmly associated with the species described below that a change is unlikely. <i>ibid.</i> 111 The name kelpfish is loosely used. in New Zealand it is used for one of the species described below [Chironemus marmoratus] and describes the other [Aplodactylus arctidens, Marblefish] in terms of its habitat. it is also used for some greenish wrasses. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,110</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] kelpfish 1175, 1321, 1552, 1732, 1751-61 KELPFiSHES 1546-53, 1749-63 <i>(1987,Bibliography of NZ Fishes</i>)

>  There is a lovely little kelp fish, most exquisitely coloured. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (<i>Odax pullus =Coridodax pullus</i>, Otago, ? obsolete) =butterfish, parrotfish, spotty; also called southern kelpfish

>  Kelp-fish ... (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>) [check] <i>(1871,TrNZi,3/130</i>) [NOTES: (Knox)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 71. BUTTER FiSH. ... The Marare of the natives, known as the Kelp Fish among the [Dunedin] fishermen and the Butter Fish in the market <i>(Coridodax pullus),</i> is the fish most commonly sold in Wellington during the winter ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,114</i>)

>  <b>GUNTHER</b> (Morris) The `Butter-fish' or `kelp-fish' of the colonists of New Zealand <i>(Coridodax pullus) </i>... it feeds on zoophytes scraping them from the surface of the kelp. <i>(1880,Fishes,533</i>)

>  <b>SENiOR</b> There is a dark-coloured, ugly-looking fish called the butter-fish, known by the fishermen as the kelp-fish. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,297</i>)

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> BUTTER FiSH <i>(Coridodax pullus)</i>. ... [14] Known by many as the kelp-fish, and by the fishermen in some places as the butter-fish. Dr. Knox, in the <i>Transactions of the New Zealand institute</i>, gives the following interesting details of the marare of the Natives : `On the 2nd August, 1870, a large quantity of the kelp-fish were offered for sale in and about Wellington, and though by no means prepossessing in external appearance, being of a dingy-black colour, and covered with a slimy mucus, i found that the fish frequent Cook Strait, more especially off and around the Mana.' <i>ibid.</i> 56 KELP FiSH (see BUTTER FiSH). <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,13</i>)

>  8. <i>Pencarrow Heads.</i> - Butter-fish (kelp-fish) <i>ibid.</i> 212 Butter-fish or Kelp-fish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>). NUMBER OF FiSH RECORDED - 365. LOCALiTiES - Pencarrow Head, the Brothers, Moeraki, Taiaroa Head, Cape Saunders, Catlin's River, Dog island, Centre island, Puysegur Point (where it is almost the only fish recorded), Bligh Sound, Milford Sound. <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. The butter-fish, kelp-fish or kelp-salmon, as it is variously called, is very common in Otago Harbour. it is wrongly identified in my previous list as <i>Odax vittatus </i>Solander. Notes (Thomson, Otago) <i>(1913,TrNZi,45/231</i>)

>  At the Portobello Marine Fish - hatchery the swimming form has been taken from the stomachs of the following fishes: Smooth-hound <i>(Mustelus antarcticus)</i>, Spined dogfish<i> (Squalus fernandinus), ...</i> Kelp-fish <i>(Coridodax pullus)</i>, Blue cod <i>(Parapercis colias)</i>, Spotty <i>(Pseudolabrus celidotus)</i>, parrot-fish <i>(Pseudolabrus miles)</i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/111</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> A similar net ... was used ... in pools where conger eel and <i>marari</i> (kelp-fish) abound, hence its name kupeng tchitok (pole net). <i>ibid.</i> 378 Plainly ... i see old Kirapu gather his <i>kupeng' tchitok'</i> (pole net) ... to net <i>mararii </i>(kelp-fish) and <i>koiro</i> (conger eel) and i go with him. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. iX No. 5 p. 360</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 378 (Chatham islands)]

>  35. <i>Coridodax pullus</i> Forster. Kelpfish, Butterfish, or Greenbone. Maori: Marari <i>(1929,TrNZi,61/145</i>)

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> (Forster). Greenbone (butterfish, kelp-fish, kelp-salmon). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/412</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <i>Coridodax pullus</i> : Butter-fish (Wellington and Marlborough), green-bone or kelp-fish (Otago). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Marari (or marare in other provinces) - the butter-fish, or kelp fish. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,61</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> it is unfortunate that the Butterfish [<i>Coridodax pullus</i> ] has been given a variety of local names which have originated either from their habit of associating with kelp, their body form, or from the colour of their bones, as for example, Kelpfish, Kelp-salmon, Greenbone, and several other names not so easy to explain. Butterfish or Greenbone are possibly the more generally used names for this fish but it should be remembered that in the North island the Spotty (<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>), a very different fish, is related to the Butterfish [?though] there is superficially little resemblance between the two species. <i>ibid.</i> 169 Kelpi Coridodax pullus Greenbone; Kelp Salmon; Kelpi; Kelpfish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,130</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> Mainly a vegetarian the Butterfish will take small sea animals and shellfish on occasions. Kelp-fish and Kelpie are names sometimes used as are the Maori names `mairari' and `rari'. No relation to his Australia namesake. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)

### kelpie
 
 <i>n.</i> a small moth =?kelp fly

 <i>n.</i> an Aust. sheep and cattle dog

>  <i>Kelpie ... n. </i>Australian breed of smooth-haired, prick-eared dog used to handle sheep [From name of original bitch.] <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> The coin could be hidden ... - but still the shrewd kelpie would find it and put it at Bob's feet. <i>(1970,Nor'west Arch,90</i>)

>  <i>Kelpie</i>: Australian sheep and cattle dog. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

The Kelpie is a `handy' dog capable of working both sheep and cattle. <i>(1985,Working Dogs,13</i>) [NOTES: [derives from a Mr Gleeson's bitch pup called `Kelpie']]

>  <b>CARR</b> And there was Tiger, a big black dog - part cattle dog, part boxer and part kelpie - but wholly pig dog. He earned his tucker this particular day as we were soon to find out. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the butterfish, <i>Coridodax pullus</i>

The name Kelpie, of course, is derived from its habit of living amidst kelp and it is easily understood how the `ie' was tacked on by fishermen. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,261</i>)

### Kelso
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a composite New Zealand-bred sheep, developed by farmer Roger Marshall.

>  "Quite literally, the Kelsos have changed our lives," he says. i need something hardier ... <i>(Feb 16, 2006,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  Outside of a university professor or two, not many New Zealanders can say they have created a new breed of sheep but Roger Marshall can. The Rangitikei farmer's Kelso breed is firmly established as a producer of meaty, high-performance sheep. The Kelsos are a composite of Finn and Texel on a Coopworth-Romney base, with a dash of East Friesian. Starting by mating Finns to his Coopworth-Romneys, and then adding Texels and a small number of East Friesians, it took him five years to develop the Kelso. in all, 11000 Kelso ewes are spread across six farms. <i>(Aug 30, 2002,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### kep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Chamber sets had to be well anchored in the country and heavy bearers inserted beneath them to carry the `keps' or chairs on which the cages were placed when winding trucks of ore from the level. The legs supporting the first set above the level would be about ten feet high. <i>ibid. </i>316 <i>`Clips", "keps", or landing chairs</i>: A solid, dead flush, landing platform for the cages was required at each level and at the surface for getting full trucks in and out of cages. This platform was provided by a steel frame mounted on heavy bearers two or three feet below the level of the chamber floor so that it could be tilted into the shaft by a lever and make a strong support beneath the cages. it was balanced by a counterweight to make it easy to operate. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,183</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> To ensure that the two cages in the shaft worked exactly opposite each other when the chamberman `changed gear' to wind ore from a different level, he rang the change signal of five bells on the steel-wire knocker-line suspended in the shaft compartment, and the engine-driver placed the `free' cage on the surface `clips' (or `keps") and disengaged it on the engine drive-shaft. The chamberman then signalled to lift the `fast' cage on the level where he was working just clear of the clips, and pinned these back clear of the shaft. He entered the `fast' cage and rang the number of the level to which he wished to change, and was taken there by the driver. On arrival at the level he placed the clips in position in the shaft and rang the change signal, whereupon the driver placed the `fast' cage on the clips and put the `free' cage (which was sitting on the surface clips) in gear on the engine drive-shaft. Thus, when the cage was raised from the level to the surface, the cage from the surface would be exactly at the level from which the winding was taking place. Great care had always to be taken to ensure that the clips were not left protruding into the shaft as a serious accident could result if the cage crashed into them. <i>(1878,Gold Mining at Waihi,316(cont.)</i>)

### kereru
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) the wood-pigeon, (<i>Hemiphaga novaseelandiae</i>), kukupa. [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kerer&#363;</b> pigeon (<i>Hemiphaga novaseelandiae</i>)] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 94.]

>  The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 42.<i> Carpophaga Novae Seelandiae</i> - .. Hagareroo [e-grave] of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst. </i>Koukoupa of the natives. Kukupa of the natives. <i>Yate</i>. Kuku and Kukupa of the natives. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/194</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/194]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Wild Pigeon of New Zealand reigns the undisputed king of beauty amongst this feathery tribe, this handsome bird known by the name of Ruckupa [Kereru, <i>Hemiphaga novaseelandiae</i>] ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,112,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CARPOPHAGA NOVAE ZEALANDiAE. (NEW-ZEALAND PiGEON.) .. <i>Native names</i> u, Kukupa, and Kereru. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,157,1888 2nd edn 1/229 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kereru - the beautiful native pigeon .. good to eat. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 64]

>  <i>Carpophaga novae-zealandiae</i>. - Kereru, Kuku, Pigeon. Both islands, and Chatham islands. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The fine New Zealand pigeon (<i>kukupa </i>or<i> kereru</i>: Carpophaga novae-zealandiae) was snared or speared in great quantity ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,105</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> First in his [sc Maori] catalogue, for flavour [19] and bulk, the plentiful kereru, or kuku (pigeon) headed the list. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,18</i>)

>  The [Maori] food has changed: no longer can poaka, kereru, and hurepo be caught in the bush ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,250,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kereru[u]</i>, a bird (Hemiphaga novae zealandiae), pigeon; the echoic ku[u]ku[u] and ku[u]kupa less commonly used; rupe the name more common in Pacific, and this the Maori uses as Rupe in the personification of the bird. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>kereru</i>: pigeon. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> So very fat were the kereru at this stage that if they struck a root in falling the bird burst at the side of the breast. <i>(1952,Faring South,104</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND PiGEON <i>Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae Maori names</i>: Kereru, kuku, kukupa <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,168</i>)

>  <i>211.a. Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae</i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>New Zealand Pigeon (Kereru) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,57,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BERRY</b> He saw a kereru - the wood pigeon - blunder .. from the nearby bush <i>(1980,First Offender,34</i>)

>  <i>New Zealand Pigeon Hemiphaga novaeseelandiae </i>GMELiN, 1789) .. [239]<i> Chatham island Pigeon H.n. chathamensis </i>(ROTHSCHiLD, 1891) OTHER NAMES: <i>Kereru, kuku, kukupa. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,238</i>)

>  <i>kereru n.</i> NZ variety of wood-pigeon. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,411</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-BXE `KERERU' Wood Pigeon) <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

>  One can watch fat kereru in the birdbath, generously proportioned like its habitues, set in brick paving under the trees. <i>(1992,North & South,119</i>) [NOTES: Month needs checking]

>  The compact Glaswegian Kiwi battler, the well-known, long-term soccer administrator and golf aficionado Mr Charles Dempsey has unleashed a whole flock of felines among the kererus. <i>(Jul 13, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Kereru are eating monkey apple seeds and spreading this species far and wide. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,No. 13/113</i>)

>  Among the species that get DOC critics hottest under the tagged collar is the kereru. Our native wood pigeon is by all accounts very nice to eat. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Kereru, our wood pigeon, loves to feast on these berries ... <i>(Jan 12, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  A glass-shattering experience gave a kereru and a Lower Hutt man the fright of their lives. <i>(Feb 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  As New Zealand's only bird capable of eating and dispersing the large fruits of trees such as karaka, tawa, and taraire, kereru are important to maintaining native forest ecosystems. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 13</i>)

### kero

>  <b>FARRELL</b> The new chap was a bit slow on account of the gammy leg but he was a good worker, and seemed right enough, if a bit of a loner who preferred a quiet smoke in a hut beached on some kero tins up the backof the property to sinking a few convivial beers in the farm kitchen. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,65</i>)

 <i>n.</i> kerosine tin

>  There was the risk of ... having the breath thumped from one by a carelessly swung kero tin. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,121</i>) [NOTES: 1974 Hulme `King Bait' in 1977 Listener Short Stories 121; also 1986 Hulme Te Kaihau (King Bait) 40]

### kerosene
 
 <i>n.</i> The usual name in NZ, (spelled chiefly now <i>-ene</i>, though earlier <i>- ine</i> frequent) for what seems called mainly in Britian <i>paraffin (oil)</i>. (Paraffin oil in NZ is usually the colourless, tasteless oil (Concise O.D `liquid paraffin") used formerly as a laxative.) Charles Ransom: US for liquid paraffin is mineral oil. in NZ, <i>kerosene</i> is often shortened coll. to <i>kero</i>.

>  in some sheds the managers insist on the cuts made in shearing being tarred, to prevent them being fly-blown; others use kerosene. <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Trade has given currency to such words as <i>kerosene</i>: an English lady surprised a New Zealand chemist by asking for four gallons of `paraffin' and he surprised her by supplying four gallons of `liquid paraffin'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,22</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to put kerosene on (an animal) (? to kill lice, ticks etc) (See [cf. turp v](tot-tyr.md#turp))

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,167</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> i'll kerosene him. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost</i>)

### kerosene can
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One of [Len Lye's] first memories was violently kicking a kerosene can when he heard that his father was ill. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the wooden case in which 2 4-gallon kerosene tins were packed; used as furniture

For a mirror there is a kerosene-tin filled with clean water. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,29,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Oct in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 29]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Or, maybe, a slatternly unpainted board house; ... its furniture a single kerosene case nailed to the wall for a cupboard. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,278</i>)

>  Our friend decided on a haircut, and sat on the kerosene-case "chair" while the storekeeper performed the kind office. <i>(Jan 16, 1904,Free Lance,12</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> As soon as the alarm rang, i sat up and reached for my pants and shirt, which were hanging on a kerosene case alongside the bed... <i>(1936,Follow the Call,50</i>)

### kerosene tin
 
 <i>n.</i> R.Mason notes `This might deserve mention if only to compare with the early stages of the billy. Any 4-gallon tin of the standard size and shape [ ] is called a `kerosene tin', even if it had held benzine (as often in the twenties before the days of bowsers), oil or meths. A standard utensil'. Kerosene tins were put to innumerable domestic, farming, trade and private uses, as buckets, utensils, containers, or (flattened out) building material up to the 1939-45 War, since when they have been gradually replaced by other containers etc. (in some pronunciations almost a special compound [kero / si:ntin], as its frequent hyphenation would indicate.)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> Two kerosene-cases nailed together, with a bag for a coverlet and door, accommodate my wearing apparel. For a mirror there is a kerosene-tin filled with clean water. ... kerosine and sardine tins <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,29,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Oct in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 29]

>  ... the remains of ruined whares, fragments of cast-off clothing, broken bottles, kerosine and sardine tins ... <i>(1877,TrNZi,9/5</i>) [NOTES: (W.T.L Travers)]

>  ... each of the men carried home several [bats], one man in particular having a kerosine-tin filled with them ... <i>(1894,TrNZi,26/221</i>)

>  (Patents) 6 Buckley, J.W., Wellington, N.Z. Making use of disused kerosine tins as boilers .. 8223 29 Jan. <i>(1897,AJHR,H-10</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> ... a hut roofed [42] with kerosene tin and a small enclosure mainly choked with weeds. <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,41,1985 ed.</i>)

>  Generally [water] has to be baled from an adjoining creek, or carried in kerosine-tins from the sea. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (NS): 25</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan-Mikonui)]

>  Mr Jellicoe: We all know what inspectors are. They grow like mushrooms and kerosene tins, and sardine tins in the front garden. (Laughter.) <i>(Jun 10, 1911,NZ Truth,311/7/3</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> Two spoon fulls of this will be enough ... in a kerosene tin of water to kill thousands of fleas. <i>(1916,Aloe, The,109,1982 O'Sullivan ed.</i>)

>  The Tuhoe folk preserve them now in kerosene-tins. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/203</i>) [NOTES: (Best)]

>  The Nolan amphora, or vessel for storing oil (how much more satisfying aesthetically than a kerosene-tin!) may be described as a theme in root-two <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/37</i>) [NOTES: (Jenkinson)]

>  The soft drinks were mixed in two kerosine tins. <i>(1987,in Fifteen Minutes,167</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Texidor in 1987 in Fifteen Minutes 167]

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> She lugs in a kerosene-tin full of newly gathered kauri-gum nuts ... The Hoppy Crummers are supposed to live on the sale of kauri-gum nuts - the dirty little nobs of kauri-gum that the gum-diggers wouldn't bother with. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,55,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> Dad had a kerosene-tin of boiling water slung over a good fire ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,44</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Among country terms universally known are <i>sliprails</i> ... and the use of <i>kerosene tin</i> (containing four gallons) as a universal measure. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,144</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The laundry was simply one or two kerosene tins over a fire. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,62</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> You can cook a feed/ Or do your washing in/ A four-gallon tin,/ A kerosine tin./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,31</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> A lone black-backed gull ... came to investigate, but Leaping Lena's back tray contained only a kerosine tin and a broken fishing grapnel. <i>(1986,Weaver's Apprentice, The,17</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> The water for making the tea was always boiled in kerosene tins over an open fire. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,31</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> i climbed down the bank with a clanking kerosene-tin bucket and now the mingled voices of the river were loud. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,81</i>)

>  He [sc. Mouth Organ Jack] was an expert on the Kerosene tin cooker ... a method of cooking corned beef, potatoes and steamed pudding, all in one boiling in a kerosene tin full of water on the open fire. <i>(1955,Authentic NZ Ballads</i>) [NOTES: Sleeve Notes]

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> Clothing was washed by being boiled in a kerosine tin over an open fire. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,89</i>)

>  And this, too, to colonials who had known most of them what it was to hunger and thirst after "damper" or "johnnycake" warranted to stay by a man for three days at a stretch! - to long vehemently for Maori hen only three removes from a watertight boot soaked in hair oil! - who knew most of them what it had been to partake of '"Sunday duff " boiled in a kerosine tin. <i>(Oct 16, 1890,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  <b>GiBBONS</b> Kerosene tins cut lengthwise made excellent roasting dishes. <i>(1988,Recollections (various authors/titles),14</i>)

>  Tje addition of a water tank and the inevitable outhouse with the four-gallon kerosene tin under the hinged seat completed the holiday compound. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/22</i>)

 <i>n.</i> broadleaf

 <i>n.</i> variant of kete

### kete
 
 <i>n.</i> a woven flax basket, a kit [ORIGIN: Maori, -] (See [kit](kee-kiw.md#kit))

>  Last year all the senior girls made ketes and tatuas. <i>(Sep 21, 1927,NZ Tablet,39</i>)

>  ...being on the DPB and being a lawyer and being a corporate lawyer all folds into this large kete of taonga that i've got to offer. <i>(Aug 2, 2002,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  Kete bags, selling in London street markets at present for GBP20, are highly sought after. <i>(Sep 23, 1999,Dominion,17</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> A basket Ke[acute]tta <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2 / 332</i>)

>  Friday night saw Wellington's biggest hip-hop event of theyear, and every young gangsta worth a flax kete attended. <i>(Dec 10, 2001,Dominion,14</i>)

>  Economic freedom has done more for the envirnment than any alfalfa-munching, sandal-wearing, kete-carrying greenie could ever achieve. <i>(Oct 1995,Metro,132</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|ete, s. A basket. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,2/332</i>)

>  basket - ke'tta [kete] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,109,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 109]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> [ketes] ... he presented 5 <i>ketes</i> of potatoes which i received with much pleasure ... <i>(Dec 28, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,94,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> They might have said, as an old Maori woman long afterwards said to me, `Mother, my heart is like an old kete [e1-circumflex] (i.e., a coarsely-woven basket). The words go in, but they fall through.' <i>(1884,Our Maoris,44</i>)

>  The <i>kete</i> was a large one, laced up with a long attached cord ... <i>(1897,TrNZi,29/174</i>)

>  <i>kete</i>, flat basket made of strips of flax: a basket of like shape but made of prepared flax dyed with cross-pattern in black called Maori-kit: the first very commonly used for potatoes or kumara and shell-fish in earlier days of settlement; the latter commonly used in later days up till now. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> My work was to watch as the wounded creatures came flapping down ... and put them into a <i>kete. </i> <i>(1948,Taina,121</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Mirinia will open up her huge flax <i>kete</i> and waddle off to my kitchen. <i>(1969,Awatea,114,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Their next venture, after the muttonbird season was over, was to run <i>ketes</i> of birds, the <i>titis</i>, up to the Otago heads ... <i>(1970,Stewart islanders,158</i>)

>  Then he pouted, picked up the kete, and headed for the stream. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,189</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Hulme `Hooks and Feelers' in 1984 Some Other Country 189]

>  <b>FULLER</b> Place in a coarse kete (flax basket) and stand in fresh water ... for two or three days. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,51</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> She giggles over the full kete [of pipis], and he comes back dumb with rage, and glowering, and hits at the bag. <i>ibid.</i> 268 He piles all the rest of his collection into a kete and staggers to the door with it. <i>ibid.</i> 466 132 Kete = basket, generally made of plaited or woven flax <i>(1983,Bone People, The,132</i>)

>  <i>kete n. NZ</i> basket (<i>flax kete</i>). [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,411</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  To Kiwi readers the transformation is not just dramatic but also hilarious. A Maori kit, or kete, is actually a string shopping bag. <i>(Jan 1989,Pacific Way,10</i>) [NOTES: (Sharp New Zildish)]

>  We left Wellington for Waikanae..; little Willie in a large potato kete (basket) affixed to poles and carried by two men. <i>(1961,Reminiscences,27</i>) [NOTES: 1844 in 1961]

>  <b>H WiLLiAMS</b> He presented 5 <i>ketes</i> of potatoes which i received with much pleasure [Note: Williams in 1826-28 uses mainly `baskets' of potatoes, etc.] <i>(Dec 28, 1827,Journal,94,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> i mounted Mr Hadfield's nice horse accustomed to Bush journeys; the two maids with one between them ride and tie; little Willie in a large potato kete (basket) affixed to poles and carried by two men. <i>(1960,Married and Gone to NZ,114</i>) [NOTES: 1845 Jan. 23 Sarah Selwyn in 1960 Drummond Married and Gone to NZ. 114.]

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> An old Maori woman long afterwards said to me, `Mother, my heart is like an old kete (i.e. coarsely-woven basket). the words go in, but they fall through.' [?ke:t] <i>(1884,Our Maoris</i>) [NOTES: 1884 Lady Martin Our Maoris in 1960 Drummond Married and Gone to NZ 158.]

>  Mr Davis.. will today lay a rock, contained in a flax kete, as a memorial to Jody at the place he died... The aim is the kete will gradually dissolve and go back into the land and the rock will be there. <i>(Apr 28, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  if you thought colourful plastic kete were avante garde, have a squizz at the Dowse's latest exhibition where you can find kete made from sea kelp, corrugated iron, bone and fairy lights... Artist Christina Wirihana, who has been weaving all her life, made three kete woven from fluorescent ribbon and copper strands... She says the kete are designed specially for a Wellington market because the flamboyant ends of the kete are left exposed so they can swing and play in the wind. <i>(Dec 11, 2002,Capital Times,13</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The rows of potato-filled ketes were measured out in yards of calico or print. <i>(1977,Hell & High Water,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

>  Why is this kete woven like a fabric and that other one over there woven loosely like a lattice? <i>(Aug 14, 2000,Salient,No. 17 : 38</i>)

>  <b>Trish Fong</b> He wore flax sandals and a cloak of feathers made by his mother and carried all his provisions in a kete woven by his younger sister. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,28</i>)

>  indeed, graffiti is part of our shared cultural "kete". <i>(Aug 20, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

### kete of knowledge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  MORE than 20 kohanga reo in the Bay of Plenty are receiving three "kete of knowledge" this week .. The "kete of knowledge" concept looks at tinana (nourishment food for the body), hinengaro (knowledge resources to feed the mind) and wairua (wellbeing to take ownership responsibility). <i>(Aug 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a basket case; a wreck or a lost cause. [basket case]

 <i>n.</i> [[Ma. <b>kete</b> basket, kit, bag, womb</b>] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 94.]

### kettlehole
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>PRATIA PERPUSILLA.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>PRATiA PERPUSiLLA.</i> A smaller-leaved plant of muddy lowland sites, common also at higher altitudes on the peaty floor of `kettleholes' which become ponds in winter. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,150</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [tin-kettler](ten-tot.md#tin-kettler))

### kewa
 
 <i>n.</i>

### key
 
 <i>v.</i> ? to scrape (a car's paintwork) with a key

>  i still live here and i don't want my Toyota keyed. <i>(Jul 5, 1998,Sunday Star Times,F11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATON</b> GiNNY: She stinks rotten through....What's the key, Boss? Di: She's a junkie. GiNNY: i know that! She a nark? <i>(1984,Outside in,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They were returning [in a bullock dray] ... when a carelessly tied `key' that held the `bow' into the `yoke' dropped out and let one bullock free. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,151,1988 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> it was quite a common practice for experienced bushmen, to have a `drive'. if you are chopping up a `face' and there is a large tree behind you further up the face, you select that as the `key'. To save time ... you do not waste time by chopping down each tree individually, but just `scarf' and partly chop each tree. When all is ready and you have worked back to the key, you scarf it very carefully. ... if everything goes according to plan it will `drive' the one below, which will in turn drive the next, and so on ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACKENZiE</b> in 1953 i made a study ... of New Zealand's habitual criminals. These 520 people ... had received the accolade of New Zealand criminality ... in prison language they had been given the `collar', the `key', the Kathleen Mavourneen sentence - `it may be for years and it may be for ever'. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,9</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a dentist's implement [[Ref. c1869.].]

>  <b>VEiTCH</b> `The key' took its name from its resemblance to the type of key used in the early locks. it consisted of a shaft to which a claw was attached by hinge at the side. A handle of varying design was provided to apply a turning movement to the shaft, and the tooth, held firmly by the claw, could then be dislocated from its socket. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,26</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> (coarse slang) kid fucker, a paedophile [ORIGIN: acronym]

### khaki cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [?first ref] <i>KHAKi CAKE</i> butter ... sugar ... egg ... flour ... dessicated cocoanut ... vanilla ... cream of tartar ... carbonate of soda . <i>(1915,Our Boys Cookery Book,107</i>)

>  <b>MiLLER</b> <i>KHAKi CAKES</i> ... Butter ... Sugar ... Eggs ... Milk ... Flour ... Soda ... Cream of Tartar ... powdered Cinnamon ... Bake in well-greased, floured patty tins, cover tops with white icing, and ornament with red and blue sugar. <i>(1917,Economic Technical Cookery Book,281</i>)

>  <i>KHAKi CAKE</i> ... butter ... sugar ... flour ... cocoa ... almonds ... cocoanut ... milk ... eggs ... baking powder ... ice the cake, almond flavouring, sprinkle dessicated cocoanut and chopped almonds on top. <i>(1924,Help the Babies Cookery Book,111</i>)

>  <i>KHAKi CAKE</i> ... butter ... sugar ... flour ... eggs ... chopped almonds ... dessicated cocoanut ... cocoa ... baking powder ... milk ... Bake in sandwich tins ... [filling] ... butter ... icing sugar ... brandy. Creamed. <i>(1929,ideal Cookery Book,103</i>)

>  <i>KHAKi CAKE</i> butter ... sugar ... flour ... eggs ... milk, coconut, chopped almonds ... cocoa ... baking powder ... Mix ... ice with chopped almonds on top <i>ibid.</i> 168 <i>KHAKi CAKE</i> when cold spread with raspberry jam and icing sugar mixed with butter and flavouring to make a thick filling, dust top with icing sugar. <i>(1929,Practical Home Cookery</i>)

>  <i>KHAKi CAKES</i> ... butter ... sugar ... beaten eggs ... milk ... flour ... soda ... cream of tartar ... cinnamon ... use cocoa to colour ... cover tops with white icing. <i>(1932,St Andrew's Cookery Book,175</i>)

>  <i>KHAKi CAKE</i> ... sugar ... butter ... eggs ... flour ... cocoa ... baking powder ... vanilla ... put together with butter icing and ice the top. <i>(1936,Home of Compassion Book of Recipes,81</i>)

### ki-o-rahi
 
 <i>n.</i> the traditional game of rahi, a full contact tackling game played on a circular pitch. [ORIGIN: Maori, Each team has 10 or more players, or often played seven-a-side. Players generally run with the ball in hand, but kicking is also permitted. This game preceded rugby.]

>  Traditional game ki-o-rahi, using handwoven flax balls, predates European colonisation and is proving a huge success at Whakatupuranga school in Otaki,where Sport Wellington is reintroducing it to Maori children. .. Describing ki-o-rangi as a cross between touch rugby, softball and dodgeball, Mr Woodman-Aldridge said it was originally used to train for battle. <i>(May 11, 2010,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  ironically, in 2005, ki-o-rahi was added to the curriculum of 31,000 American primary schools, and seven million children were introduced to it ... <i>(Jul 19, 2008,Dominion Post,E4</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [kea](k-kea.md#kea))

>  These emotions are just part of the mana and the kia kaha we have within New Zealand. <i>(Aug 26, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A1</i>)

>  Be proud and soon you will realise that knowing where you belong is knowing yourself. No reira kia kaha, kia maia, kia manawanui. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,issue 18 summer 2005.2006. p7</i>)

### kia mataara
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, kia mataara = look alive; wide awake http://www.learningmedia.co.nz/nz/online/ngata/m2edictionary]
