### kia ora
 
 <i>v.</i> to greet with kia ora

>  i might have kia oraed, but it wasn't Hone in the sense of the talkback show, no. <i>(May 13, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> as a greeting or farewell: good day, hello; goodbye.

>  in the evening His Lordship will be greeted with further "Kia-Oras" in the shape of a banquet. <i>(Feb 20, 1904,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kia ora, interj.</i> Maori phrase used by English in the North island of New Zealand, and meaning `Health to you!'. A private letter (1896) say - `You will hear any day at a Melbourne bar the first man say <i>Keora ta-u</i>, while the other says <i>Keora tatu,</i> so replacing `Here's to you!'. These expressions are corruptions of the Maori, <i>Kai ora taua</i>,' `Health to us too!' and <i>Kia ora tatou</i>,' Health to all of us!' <i>(1898,Austral English,247</i>) [NOTES: [Morris is in part wrong:<i> taua</i> is the dual pronoun `we two, us two' (not `us too"); <i>tatou</i> is the plural personal pronoun `you all, and myself; all of us (inclusive)!]]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Some of these [borrowings from Maori] seem to have been commoner in the past than they are now, e.g. <i>Kia ora</i> `good health', more often seen in England (as a brand name for soft drinks) than in New Zealand now. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Some of these [borrowings from Maori] seem to have been commoner in the past than they are now, e.g. <i>Kia ora</i> `good health', more often seen in England (as a brand name for soft drinks) than in New Zealand now. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,169</i>)

>  <i>kia ora n. </i>Maori expression meaning Good luck! health to you. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kia ora .... interj</i>. Maori expression meaning <i>Good luck! health to you.</i> Now also means<i> hello</i>. [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> MiRi, <i>approaching:</i> Tena korua! PERA. Kia ora, Miri! <i>(1969,Awatea,23,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> `And they smiled sweetly and said, `Kia ora"'. Literally the phrase means `Be well', but to these pakehas it is one of the few phrases of Maori they know and they probably think it means `Good luck' or `Hullo'. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,137</i>) [NOTES: 1973 Pearson (ed) in Finlayson Brown Man's Burden 137 [note 17 to p.53]]

>  On Monday the `Post' was first to report the plight of Mrs Naida Povey, an Auckland Maori tolls operator who was ordered to stop greeting callers with `kia ora.' `Now she can,' Mr Talbot said. <i>(May 25, 1984,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> `Kia ora', she said. `What does that mean?' Celia asked, letting her in. `it's a Maori greeting, but <i>pakehas</i> use it too.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,48</i>)

>  <i>kia ora NZ</i> exclamation of goodwill used as a greeting (<i>telephone operators sometimes greet callers with 'kia ora'</i>). [M, = good health] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,411</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 270]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>kia ora!</i> hello; from Maori `good health!'; Reed/Karetu <i>Concise Maori Dictionary</i>, 1984. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,66</i>)

>  At least everyone else looked professional beside Bert, whose idea of a Kiwi joke is to say "Kia Ora, Heinz and Worcestershire to you all!" <i>(Jul 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> And why should Miss Muffet be afraid of a spider? My grandmother would say, 'What a silly girl! Why doesn't she say hello to it, kia ora!' <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,288</i>)

>  The words "Kia Ora" also beam forth from the lofty heights. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> And, rising to take his leave, he continued: `idleness and tribal jealousy. i think you are right. Kia ora.' <i>ibid.</i> 273 That soulless braggarts might pose as almoners, and call from the highway, `Kia ora oh coloured brother!' and go on their way to inhale fresh air for further boasting. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,188</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Your replies must be non-committal and diplomatic. Kia Ora, R.J.Seddon.' <i>(Jan 18, 1906,Seddons, The,162,1968 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PiCARD</b> i could look back on in far off N.Z. and call friend. -Kia ora! <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,4</i>)

>  in the name of the readers of the `Maoriland Worker,' [sic] of the New Zealand Federation of Labour, and of the New Zealand Socialist Party, we extend to him the hand of fellowship and wish him Kia Ora. <i>(Mar 20, 1911,Maoriland Worker,7/1/4</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> The digger put his pint to his hairy lips and said `Kia Ora. Here's fun.' <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,32</i>)

>  All right, Kia Ora <i>(Sep 5, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,35</i>)

>  KiA-ORA - beautiful expression, given with a heart throb. What better wish could the brain of man conceive or his heart feel than Kia-Ora. <i>(Mar 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,1/1,1981 ed.</i>)

>  [end of letter] Kia ora Alec <i>(Jul 4, 1918,Great Adventure,243,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton)]

>  <i>kiaora interj</i>. your health! [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  [Piet] dived into his sack and produced a bottle of `dop'. `What is it you say when you drink in your country?' `Kia Ora!' explained a Maoriland trooper. Piet reverentially said `Kia Ora!' and sucked the mouth of the bottle. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,145</i>) [NOTES: 1930 Claude Jewell in NZ Short Stories 145]

>  <b>LUSH</b> Goodbyee and kia-ora. <i>(1934,Waimana,82</i>)

>  <b>ADAiR</b> A crowd with their paper ribbons and `Kia Oras'. <i>(1935,Wanted A Son,247</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Kia ora!</i> Good health to you!; good luck!: New Zealand (and occ.Australian): from ca. 1870. Ex Maori <i>keora ta-u</i> and <i>k. tatu</i>. See esp. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,452</i>)

>  Now that our political leaders are less lavish with Maori greetings, spoken and cabled, `tenakoe' and `kia ora' are less assured of a long life. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Toasts were not in his line, but part of a verse from a Psalm, `We wish you good luck,' flashed into his mind, so he gave them `Kia Ora' (Good luck). This was said to be the first occasion the words were spoken in that sense, and they were received with acclamation - caught on - and have become a national phrase. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,211</i>) [NOTES: [c1860]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>Kia ora</i> as the equivalent of cheerio! or good luck! especially in a toast, was speedily anglicized <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Taihoa!</i> `wait a bit', and <i>kia ora</i> `good luck' (a disguised form of <i>keora tau</i>) are well-established adoptions [from Maori]. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> <i>`Kia Ora,' said the italian [P.O.W.] guards ... </i>Politeness indeed, ... the Maori Battalion must have passed this way. ... `Kia Ora.' This didn't seem to satisfy the guards, however. ... We soon found out they were saying <i>`Che ora?</i>, (What is the time?) <i>(1945,Gunner inglorious,52</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>kia ora:</i> cheerio, good luck <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  kia ora : form of greeting. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  i would include among New Zealand English words: <i>kia-ora</i>: goodbye, good luck <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  kia ora A Maori expression meaning `good health to you'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),230</i>)

>  <i> kia ora interj </i>[Maori <i>kia ora</i>, lit., be well] - used as a salutation or toast in Australia and New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1240</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> `Kia ora,' we said [drinking up]. <i>(1964,Watersiders,123</i>)

 <i>exclam.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### kick
 
 <i>n.</i> pocket; trousers [also UK and Australia]

>  As a general rule gentle shepherds don't put their hands into their own `kick'. [express = `don't pay their own way"] <i>(May 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1-6</i>)

>  One [Christchurch assailant] said, `Let's go through his kick,' a classic remark which has some direct reference to the recesses of one's pants. <i>(Aug 18, 1906,NZ Truth,61/8/1</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Anyhow, he said they had captured a sugar boat, so i put a few handfuls in my kick. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> And the money in the kick earned as well <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,147</i>)

>  <b>FAiRBURN</b> (i believe you generously dipped your hand in your kick once again) <i>(Jun 27, 1950,Letters,1981,197 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to demand (money; etc) from (a person)

>  <b>TRAMP</b> He will be `kicking' us for a rise in screw <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> kitty; bundle of personal belongings; also ?bank, till [ca1950 Wellington eg I'm going to draw some money out of the kick. I've got twenty pounds in the kick.]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Once he and i set off with thirty bob in our kick and toured the whole country. <i>(1962,One of Us,104</i>)

>  <b>McKENZiE</b> <i>kick</i> bag of personal belongings <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,97</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> KiCK (n) Pocket. USA, from 1845. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,250</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> to have a complaint to make, or to receive (ie to be due for a turn to kick somebody [ca1935 eg I've got a kick coming to me now if everybody's finished.]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a sixpence [Part. from 1700 - mainly in compound sums]

>  nice 'namel paint - five an' a kick (5s 6d) the tin. <i>(Mar 1919,Quick March,51</i>)

>  <b>T. WALKER</b> Havelock. "Four and a kick' =4/6 (?half rh. on six of <i>Zack</i>) <i>(Mar 3, 1952,Personal Communication</i>)

>  For the shilling<i> bob</i> was the most common nickname in use, but also recorded is <i>deener</i>. This latter name was used more widely in New Zealand and Australia than in Britain. The sixpence attracted many slang terms - <i>tizzy, tanner, sprat, bender</i>, and a <i>kick. </> <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)

>  He told how the wool growers could get four and a "kick" for their wool. <i>(Oct 6, 1917,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### kick like a run cow, a
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  b. kick like a run cow The depth [of furrow of a swing plough] was graduated by the bridle adjustment but was corrected in uneven ground by leverage from the long handles, or stilts as they were called. if the the share struck anything these [swing ploughs] had a kick like a run cow. it is said that a good man could land on his feet six yards in front of the horses ... <i>(Oct 30, 1963,Weekly News,[farming page]</i>)

### kick off
 
 <i>v.</i> to start; to get started.

>  To kick off with we'd fool about in the water <i>(1943,NZ New Writing,5</i>) [NOTES: 1943 NZNWi Sargeson Episode 5]

>  <b>DAViN</b> Well, it's time you blokes kicked off [on your journey]. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,88</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To begin the operation, involving more than one person. .in 4;`We kicked off early and had travelled half the distance by the time the sun was up.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS]</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To begin the operation, involving more than one person. .in 4;`We kicked off early and had travelled half the distance by the time the sun was up.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],635</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63) 1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust. and NZ English [TS] 635]

>  <b>HUTCHiNS</b> Of course several players realised that the representative programme was about to kick off and they were painfully aware that the selector would be watching the proceedings. <i>(1983,Rugby Rabbits,38</i>)

>  it was kicked off by Prof. Willi Ziegler, Director of the Senckenberg institute in Frankfurt, tracing the history of the institute and its association with sedimentological research, and by Grand-Old-Man H.-E. [sic] Reineck, a former director at Wilhelmshaven, giving a 'a personal view of tidal clastics', with full benefit of the double entendre. <i>(Mar 1993,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,99/58</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> start, to give something a start

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> i've two `kick offs' in the writing game. <i>One </i>is joy - real joy -the thing that made me write when we lived at Pauline, ... The other `kick off' is my old original one, ... <i>(Feb 3, 1918,Letters</i>) [NOTES: 1918 feb 3 mansfield letters (1951 murry) 149]

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> He knew that Butler liked to be the first with the news, and usually gave it a kick-off at the post office <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,76</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> cf quote [crook =dishonest] <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,145</i>)

>  [First editorial title] KiCK-OFF <i>(an editorial) </i> <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,7</i>)

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> The handling by the New Zealand Labourites of the whole conchie business, right from the kick-off, had been an outstanding case of bungling by men with guilty consciences. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,74</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> They put in a miserable order for a kick-off ... <i>(1960,Word for Word,209</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A beginning containing its own impetus. The moment when the operation begins. .in 4;`We had a good start, with no holdups, and each man knew exactly what to do. Right from the kick-off the job went well.' `For a kick-off i'll put in $10, just to get the project started.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],637</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63) 1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust. and NZ English [TS] 637 -]

### kick out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The result is a foregone conclusion, as the Thorndon Club must `cake walk' it at the 100 yards distance, just the same as the Wellington Club would `kick' it out at the mile or half-mile distance. <i>(Dec 17, 1910,NZ Truth,286/7/4</i>)

### kick the block
 
 <i>n.</i> a game (See [also kick the tin](kee-kiw.md#kick-the-tin))

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Further, because He was an awesome person, he had to be very carefully chosen, and so the mysterious counting-out rhymes were used. Despite his powers, however, his potency could be minimized in many games by the fact that the players helped one another fight against him (e.g., Bedlam, Relievo, Kick the Block). <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,52</i>)

### kick the tin
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Of the Help-He games, Kick the Tin, is the most widely known. A tin is placed in the centre of a circle. One He is in charge of the tin. He can only tag the other players when his tin is in the centre of the circle. Any players he catches must stay in the circle. Any non-caught player can help and free the other caught players by running through the circle and kicking the tin out of it. Other names for this game are: Kick the Boot (Gisborne [18]90), Knock the Block or Homaiacky (Nelson [18]95), Kick the football or Piacky (Mangatainoka). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/513</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> in a few tagging games the players joined hands with the He when they were caught and helped him catch the other players. These games were known as <i>The Bellahonie</i> (E-21) (Wellington, 1910), Kick Post One (Hampden, Otago, 1880), and Ballyhooley (Wellington, 1920). More popular were those games in which the players helped one another to fight the He, and of these the most widespread was the game known as Kick the Tin (e-3), Kick the Boot, Kick the Block, or Homiacky. in this game the He was put on guard of a jam tin placed inside a small circle marked on the ground. [Other players hid: those He could see he named and tapped the tin three times and `captured': other hiders waited till they had chance to kick tin out of circle: then all were free again.] <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,80</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Other games are Humpty Dumpty (known as Dunk and Davey in the 1920s), Kick the Tin, and Hot Rice. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,264</i>)

>  Kicker: A sheep that struggles and kicks during shearing uncommon with gun shearers. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> There are names for types of sheep, the <i>cot</i> with matted wool; the<i> cobbler</i>,a difficult one; the <i>flyer</i>,a fast one; the <i>kicker</i>; the <i>sandyback</i> and the gasper, which gasps apparently because grass comes into its throat. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,147</i>)

>  KiCKER: A sheep that struggles and kicks during shearing. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: [Shearer's language]]

>  <i>Kicker</i>: Sheep that struggles and kicks while being shorn. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,56</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> clincher

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>v.</i>

### kidney fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cardiomanes</i> (formerly <i>Trichomanes) reniforme</i>, with kidney-shaped fronds.

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The singular form of the Kidney-fern (<i>Trichomanes reniforme</i>), the round, kidney-shaped leaves on the edges of which are bordered with seed pods ... <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),133</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> (And) <i>Trichomanes reniforme</i> kidney-fern <i>(1882,Ferns & Fern Allies of NZ</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> Let us suppose we wish to look on the 'kidney fern' (<i>T. reniforme</i>) <i>(1882,Out in the Open,66</i>)

>  <b>FiELD</b> (And) <i>(1890,Ferns of NZ</i>)

>  <b>HARRiS</b> (And) <i>(1890,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The badge of mourning was a wreath of green leaves or of lycopodium, or of the kidney fern (<i>Trichomanes reniforme</i>) twisted in the hair. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,389</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> (And) <i>(1906,NZ Flora</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And). <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>TURNER</b> (And.) <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... the rata ... its base covered with sheets of translucent kidney-ferns <i>(1910,NZ Plants,43</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kopakopa, ... Trichomanes reniforme, kidney fern. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,158</i>)

>  (And) <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>DOBBiE</b> (And) <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Kidney and maidenhair ferns were to be found in abundance, and everyone had been satisfied to collect them. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,61,1975 ed.</i>)

>  (And) <i>Trichomanes reniforme</i> kidney fern <i>(1925,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The kidney-fern was everywhere, sprawling over ground and trees. <i>(1926,Lenore Divine,185</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> There were carpets of delicate kidney ferns, so called because of their shape; maidenhair, Prince of Wales's feathers, umbrella and many other varieties ... <i>(1930,NZ Memories,171</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> There is kidney fern, sprayed dark pads, and Augusta steals a root, ... <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,118,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> My Picton grandmother ... could cut supple jack and find kidney fern and make a track through the thickest part of the bush. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,7</i>) [NOTES: [`Lagoon']]

>  kidney-fern A beautiful fern with kidney-shaped leaves or fronds which grows only in New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),230,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The Anomalous KiDNEY FERN has features linking it both with the filmy ferns and the bristle ferns ... The botanical [name] <i>Cardiomanes reniforme</i> draws attention to the kidney-shaped lamina of the frond. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,102,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> The remains of the stumps [38] protected by a mantle of kidney-fern, may still be seen on the point. <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,37</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>TRiCHOMANES (CARDiOMANES) RENiFORME</i> ... Kidney fern is common in damp forests but fronds are able to curl up and survive dry periods. A very peculiar fern, confined to New Zealand. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,218</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Cardiomanes reniforme</i> (Forst.f.) Presl. Common name:<i> Kidney fern</i> Maori name: <i>Kopakopa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,27</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Cardiomanes reniforme</i> KiDNEY FERN ... Raurenga ... Fronds unmistakable, roundly kidney-shaped ... <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,456</i>)

>  <i>kidney fern</i> NZ fern with kidney-shaped fronds. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,412</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The kidney fern (<i>Trichomanes reniforme</i>) is perhaps our most unusual filmy fern. <i>ibid.</i> 127 The forest floor has ... soft cushions of the pale green milk moss (<i>Leucobryum candidum</i>) and masses of the translucent fans of the kidney fern (<i>Trichomanes reniforme</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,51</i>)

>  The kidney fern (<i>Trichomanes reniforme</i>) with its extraordinarily shaped frond, projecting clusters of sporangia on the margin and yellowish green colour is a plant of international repute that never ceases to attract attention from visitng botanists. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,71</i>)

>  Further in the bush you will find the rare kidney fern, and the plumed fronds of the double crape, and the delicate leaflets of the maiden hair. <i>(Dec 16, 1897,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> in shine and shade alike the kidney fern hung out its glossy lobes of satiny-emerald, shaped exactly as its name implies... [Note] <i>Trichomanes reniforme </i> <i>(1911,Through South Westland,57</i>)

>  it is the common kidney fern, found in most damp forests except on the West Coast of the South island where it is rare. <i>(May 11, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Filmy ferns look almost too fragile to be outside and kidney ferns crown into the crooks of branches. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> in shine and shade alike the kidney fern hung out its glossy lobes of satiny-emerald, shaped exactly as its name implies... [Note] ,<i>Trichomanes reniforme<i\> <i>(1911,Through South Westland,57</i>)

### kidney pie
 
 <i>n.</i> insincere praise

>  <b>MiTCHELL & STRONG</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>kidney pie,</i> insincere praise or what the Americans call <i>bull</i>, and Australians <i>bullsh</i>, and both, in less hurried moments, <i>bull-sh-t; </i> <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> His offsider said `Give a man a fair go', so i gave him a bit of kidney pie and there was nothing lousy about him, but he said he had to be careful because the other fellow was the trump of the dump. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Kidney-pie</i>. insincere praise: New Zealanders': from ca. 1912. Cf. <i>kid</i>, n., 5. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,455</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Twentieth century expressions include] <i>kidney pie </i>humbug or deceit, closely related to the Australian <i>kidstakes</i> with a similar meaning. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,57</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>kidney pie</i> insincere praise; WWi. NZA. . <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,66</i>)

>  <i>kidstakes n. (Sl.)</i> misleading chatter, leg-pulling, often used as an interjection implying disbelief. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <i>kidstakes n. NZ & Austral. sl.</i> (also <i>kidsteaks</i>) humbug, pretence. [<i>kid</i> humbug, 'gammon'] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,412</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

 <i>n.</i>

### kidney-buster
 
 <i>n.</i> a tramper's pack
