>  <b>SARGESON</b> kidsteaks: <i>all kidsteaks,</i> full of blarney. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>)

### kiekie
 
 <i>n.</i> An epiphyte with edible flower bracts [Maori]; <i>Freycinetia banksii</i>, the plant, and its leaves, and (loosely) the flower-bracts, and fruit; NZ pineapple, also called tawhara [Many various spgs, dividing roughly into a. <i>North Island: kia-kia, kiakia, kea-kea, kie(-)kie,kihikihi, kiki, kee-kee</i>, and occas.,<i> giegie</i>; b. South Island <i>gaga</i> (? Nelson, West Coast), <i>ge-ge, gee-gee, gei-gei; gigi, giggy; kihi-ki</i> but cf Brunners' spelling, <i>kiakia. </i> 1890s (1957) Pascoe MED 14 [MS. I.T.G. West Coast.] geigei]

>  The boy.. went out with his mates to look for kie-kie, or gi-gis.. of which they were very fond. [Note] A climbing plant, Freycinetia banksii, considered edible by the very young. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,155</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  <b>REES</b> The kie-kie (Freycinetia Banksii), the fruit about the size of a small pineapple, is of luscious sweetness. <i>(1851,GB Parliamentary Papers (irish Univ. series),1779/28</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The kihi-ki (<i>Freycinetia Banksii</i>) which grows on the lower hills near the sea, wild parsley (<i>Angelica geniculata</i>), spinach (<i>Tetragonia expansa</i>), ... are also eatable. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kie-kie, n.</i> Maori name for a climbing plant, <i>Freycinetia banksii, </i>N.O. <i>Pandanaceae;</i> frequently pronounced <i>ghi-ghi</i> in the North island of New Zealand, and <i>gay-gie </i>in the South island. <i>(1898,Austral English,247</i>)

>  <b>GOLDER</b> `The trees were ... covered with a kind of parasite plant, called a kee-kee, having a thick cabbage-like stock.' <i>(1854,Pigeon's Parliament,77</i>)

>  <b>GOLDER</b> `The trees were ... covered with a kind of parasite plant, called a kee-kee, having a thick cabbage-like stock.' <i>(1854,Pigeon's Parliament,77</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> (And) [GiGi] <i>Freycinetia Banskii </i> <i>(1900,Our Stolen Summer</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Giggies is the bush boy corruption of `Kie-kie', a sweet luscious fruit, or more strictly, flower. [West Coast] <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,179</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Aye, and kie kie also, with its two kinds of sweetmeats, both on the same vine - the first at, and before Christmas, when the plant distills its nectar juices, and collects them in that bunch of tender succulence of fleshly leaflets the Maori called tawhara - the very name is maddening to think of! - and later, in winter, when its fruits, shaped like a corncob studded with the heads of translucent amber! Ah me! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,16</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Freycinetia Banksii, A.Cunn.] ... Kiekie; Tawhara </i>(the edible bracts); <i>Ureure</i> (the fruit). ... ripe fruit in May. ... The white fleshy bracts surrounding the spadices are often eaten; the fruit less commonly so. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,742</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>[Freycinetia Banksii]</i> Maori name <i>Kie-kie</i>. The flower is usually called <i>Tawhara</i>. ... [Freycinetia is called after the French navigator, Freycinet.] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,80,1840, p. 83 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> `kie-kie (Kyay-kyay), the gege of the settler.' A climbing shrub. Maori <i>kiekie</i> [kiekie] (the plant); and ureure (q.v.) the fruit (a reduplication of <i>ure</i> `penis'); and <i>taawhara</i> for the flower bracts. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,65 fn.</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> (And) <i>Freycinetia Banksii</i> GiEGiE <i>(1914,Wilds of Maoriland</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> We have climbed above the pines [on the West Coast]; ... and up here on the mountain side the vegetation is the flowering <i>rata</i>, the <i>akeake</i>, the native alpine broom, the white blossomed ribbonwood, ... the flax-like <i>kiekie</i>, and the semi-tropical <i>neinei,</i> half-shrub, half-palm, and whole forests of fern trees. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol ii South island) 95</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> (W.C) A ga-ga hunt he was going to take her on [Children, West Coast] 1928 <i>ibid.</i> 46 The juicy ga-ga or kie-kie. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,22</i>)

>  <i>kie-kie n.</i> a climbing shrub. [maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,254</i>)

>  One day the boy ... went ...to look for kie-kie, or gi-gis, as they called them [at Karamea] ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,155,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The name kiekie is often corrupted to `giegie' or `gigi' by the pakeha. .... from its appearance probably as well as from its taste it has been called the `New Zealand Pineapple' [quoting Allan] <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,272</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>kiekie</i>, a scrambling plant (Freycinetia Banksii); flower-bracts (ta[a]whara) eaten as a sweetmeat; also banana-like fruit (ureure); often corrupted to geegee (not jeejee). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>)

>  <i>kie-kie</i>: large climbing shrub. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> One source of food supply was derived from the all pervading kihikihi, or as the settlers called it, the gigi. <i>(1952,Faring South,104</i>)

>  <i>kie kie</i> formerly also <i>`gigi'.</i> The Maori name of a gigantic climbing plant of New Zealand; the <i>bracts</i> (small leaves near the flowers) are edible. (Pronounce <i>key key</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),230</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Passing next to root climbers, we find ... the KiE-KiE <i>(Freycinetia banksii)</i> more commonly referred to as the `giggi'. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,146,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>kiekie: </i>climbing plant with spear-like leaves; its fruit is about 7 in. long and is pineapple-shaped. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <i>kie-kie n.</i> New Zealand climbing plant [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i> kiekie ... n.</i> N.Z. plant which grows on trees, leaves used for weaving and banana-like fruit eaten. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> ... gullies which often contained heavy growths of gigi and supplejacks. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,48</i>)

>  <b>CHAR;ES</b> Lawyer and geigei and blackberry root,/ Biddy-bid burrs, matagouri and toot! <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,25</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Other berries popular as delicacies were the fleshy, white, pineapple-shaped bracts and berries of the kiekie (<i>Freycinetia banksii</i>), the ripe, black berries of the makomako or wineberry (<i>Aristotelia serrata</i>) and the small, sweet berries (known as <i>konini</i>) of the kotukutuku or tree fuchsia (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>). <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,7</i>)

>  <i>kie kie n.</i> a New Zealand climbing plant with edible bracts. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,618</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 412]]

>  in the last few years interesting finds have included Freycinetia banksii, the kiekie, or New Zealand screwpine, which has edible fruit. <i>(Dec 1982,NZ Gardener,23</i>)

>  The fleshy flower bracts from the kiekie can be eaten in summer, while their ripe fruits can be eaten in winter. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Capital Times,25</i>)

>  Kiekie is common in the forests of the North island and at the top and west of the South island. <i>(Nov 12, 2005,Dominion Post,indulgence 23</i>)

>  Some of the nikau are loaded with kiekie and other epiphytes, clinging like memories of the ancestral forest. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 65/86</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Kiekie, Uriuri, Ori, Tiore, Patangatanga... it is called New Zealand's pine-apple. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),158</i>)

>  <b>von HAAST</b> There is a plant that grows in the bush to be found on mountain ridges called Kie-Kie it has a leaf resembling the flax and in the months of February and March it produced a cobb-like fruit, of the pineapple family, it has from three to four cobbs a year. <i>(1948,Life & Times of Sir Julius von Haast,550</i>) [NOTES: Quote from DOHERTY 1870]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> These characters generally completed their outfit by wearing a hat, and this was generally broad brimmed and white in colour, and made out of bleached and finely woven kiekie, a bush climber with flax-like leaves. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,155</i>)

>  in the last few years interesting finds have included <i>Freycinetia banksii,</i>, the kiekie, or New Zealand screwpine, which has edible fruit. <i>(Dec 1982,NZ Gardener,23</i>)

>  The pulp from the fruit of the kiekie (freycinetia banksii), on the other hand was used by Maori as well as settlers to make a jelly... <i>(Apr 30, 2005,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

>  Sprays of vivid purple berries, of <i>Dianella nigra</i>, peep out at the paths and fruiting kiekie - hardly as spectacular .. but a solid sign of healthy native bush. <i>(Jul 2006,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,33</i>)

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> Climbing uphill against kiekie was like attacking the bank-and-ditch defence of a pa where the defenders are all pushing harsh green mops in your face. <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,125</i>)

>  Kiekie smothered many a trunk with sword-like leaves bared in defiance of interlopers. <i>(Mar 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

>  The white fleshy bracts surrounding the spadices of the kei-kei are edible and have a sweet aromatic flavour. <i>(Aug 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> We ate, or tried to, the Maori delicacy called kie-kie and pronounced 'giggy', which grew as an epiphyte, like a fern, high up the mossy trunks of trees in such patches of the original Taranaki rain-forest as survived. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,146</i>) [NOTES: 1991 edition]

>  Kiekie is a tousle-headed native that sticks close to watercourses. its tough, pliable stems are held up from the ground by aerial roots, then they bend and snake any which way before rising finally to a burst of long leaves. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  The use of the jute bale.. does not seem merely inadvisable. <i>(Oct 28, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  Next day i spent hours trying to get out of one steep valley, through supplejack nets and thickets of kiekie that forced me to crawl on my knees. <i>(Aug 15, 1998,Dominion,21</i>)

>  The sketch.. shows him [sc. James Carroll] crowned with a hat of kie-kie woven by a native woman. <i>(Jan 2, 1926,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  The bush is composed of pukatae and tawa, and is overgrown with kicke. <i>(May 1, 1880,NZ Mail,00</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|ie k|ie; Name of a certain plant. Polack applies this to KiEKiE blades: is this descriprive merely of KiEKiE blades, and applied as a nonce-use by Pol.: but check whether such plants may be generically called spear-grass in English] <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,163</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> As a preservative against rain, many large garments are worn ... made of the <i>kiakia</i>, spear-grass, which is impervious to the element. <i>ibid.</i> 2/23 The sail is made of raupo flags or kiakia [a's-acute], grass, etc., of triangular shape; it is fastened to two small rickers or poles, which serve for both mast and yards, and is fixed upright between the gunwales. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/392</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The first of the indigenous esculent fruits is the <i>Kia-kia.</i> it grows on a parasitical plant of the same name; and in form much resembles the pineapple. its taste is very agreeable and cool, and could, no doubt, be still much improved by cultivation. [Mentions <i>tawara</i> separately p45] <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),44</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... they [kumera] are spread out equally by individuals draped in mats made of the green kiakia to protect them from the heat ... <i>(Nov 28, 1844,Journal,3/40</i>)

>  [;earliest cit. in this spg form] ... a wet wood of supple-jack and kie-kie. <i>(1847,NZ Journal,7/106,1840 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ... ekiakia <i>(1847,Great Journey,43</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> There is scarcely a bare stem to be seen, even the tallest are embraced by a variety of twining plants, parasites and creepers, amongst which the <i>kia-kia</i>, decked at every joint with bunches of iris-shaped leaves, ... <i>(1847,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 121]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The only indigenous fruit worthy of mention is a parasitical or epiphyte plant (<i>Freycinetia Banksii</i>) called<i> kiekie</i> by the natives, ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,148</i>)

>  <b>ALLOM</b> [kia-kia] <i>Kia-kia</i>. This is a ... very delicious fruit, springing from the base of a number of leaves, ... <i>(1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 129]

>  ... one of them [Maoris] got a little way up the tree and another handed him a supple jack about 5ft long with a running noose of kie-kie at the end - this he insinuated over the neck of the pigeon and so brought it down. <i>(Jan 6, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/141,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>GOLDER</b> The trees were ..... covered with a kind of parasite plant, called a kee-kee, having a thick cabbage-like stock. <i>(1854,Pigeon's Parliament,77</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Kie-kie (New Zealand's pine-apple) ... is a vegetable impostor, like a sweet artichoke flavoured with turpentine. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/136</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> After half an hour's walk through bush, consisting of kie-kie, fern-trees, and nikau palms, we again took to the beach. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,45</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAM MARTiN</b> a hill ... covered with bush supple jacks Lawyers and Kea-Kea with innumerable land slips ... <i>(Sep 26, 1863,Diary</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kiekie, <i>Col[enso]. Freycinetia Banksii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Kie kie. Freycinetia Banksii, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/49</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> (Notes) Kie-kie (parasite) .... a lofty climber; the bracts and young spikes make a very sweet preserve. <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia,505</i>)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> We went out into the bush after <i>tawharas</i> this week. This is the fruit of the <i>kia kia</i>, a parasite which climbs and adheres to the dead trees. The Maoris make excellent hats resembling the celebrated `cabbage-tree' hats, from the fibre of the leaves. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,135,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Kiekie ..... O. Pandaneae; G. Freycinetia; S Freycinetia Banksii. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  <b>PAULiN</b> ... gee-gee and supple jack. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast,100</i>)

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> (W.C.) The bush was very dense, grown over with gigi and cutty-grass. [West Coast] <i>(1889,Wonderful Dog,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> in early and chiefly Si use (usu. as gi-gi, ge-ge) in the sense of `made of the leaves of the kiekie', and applied specifically to baskets and hats; see 1940 Laing and Blackwell Plants 83 `The long fibrous leaves are used in the manufacture of baskets'. [attrib.]

>  <b>POTTS</b> The unused food .... of our little camp, together with the empty kiekie baskets. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,20</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The kiekie thong looked fresh and green. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,214</i>)

### kierakiki
 
 <i>n.</i> ?kiekie

>  <b>POLACK</b> Supper was announced, which was served up in green baskets made of the undressed flax and kierakiki [e-,a-acute] plant. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/86</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> A valuable garment for the stormy [178] climate of the southward is called <i>neri</i>[e-acute], and is made of undressed flax or of the <i>kierakiki</i>, a species of rush, impervious to rain. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/177</i>)

### kihi
 
 <i>n.</i> kiss [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <B>AUKUSO</B>.-Lui, D + Aqua welcomed - Ojihan on 26/12/05. she [sic] arrived at 9:10am (9.8lbs). Kihis to Ngarangi & Penina. <i>(Feb 1, 2006,Dominion Post,D9</i>)

### kihi-kihi
 
 <i>v.</i> ?

>  Locusts kihi-kihi'd. <i>(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54</i>)

### kihihi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Kihii, <i>Geolog. Surv. Pittosporum crassifolium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <i>Pittosporum crassifolium,</i> sea-side tarata, or kihihi. - A fine shrub or small tree, sometimes attaining the height of twenty-five feet; common on sandy or rocky coasts, from the North Cape to Poverty Bay; produces seed freely; a most valuable plant. <i>(1873,TrNZi,6/48</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

### kihikihi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kohekohe (1851)](kiw-kwi.md#kohekohe)))

>  Our last zizag breaks the spider threads spun from tree to tree and tenacious kihikihi provide the processional as we stride out along the paper road which takes us back to Fairfield. <i>(Jul 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

### kikararu
 
 <i>n.</i> insect which lives in toi toi [kekerengu, Maori bug http://www.jps.auckland.ac.nz/document/Volume_61_<i>Platyzosteria novae-seelandiae</i> 1952/Volume_61,_No._1_%2B_2/The_insect_people_of_the_Maori,_by_David_Miller,_p_1-61/p1] (See [kekerengu](kee-kiw.md#kekerengu),[Maori bug](m-mar.md#maori-bug))

>  This Blatta is doubtless the same as the insect named by the natives Kikararu, which was erroneously taken for a bug. it is a new species, described by Mr. Brunner of Vienna, as <i>Polyzosteria Novae Zelandiae</i>. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),171</i>)

>  The most disgusting insects in nature exist among the spear-grass, called toi toi, of the swamps and plains; it is called the kikararu [a's-acute]. its odour is disgustingly offensive, and it is often found in rush or other dwelling-houses. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/320</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> <i>Kika</i>[acute]<i>raru. Polack.</i> inhabits New Zealand (Spear-grass). Polack 1., p.329. `The most disgusting insect in nature.' <i>Polack. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/281</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Adam and White) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/281]

### kike
 
 <i>n.</i> kahikatea (See [kahikatea](k-kea.md#kahikatea))

>  <b>MOORE</b> This meant that on a fairly large log of kahikatea - always referred to as `kike' - ... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,43</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> ... there in the bush at Turama ... He'll be able to handle all them kike trees [185] himself. <i>ibid.</i> 259 it would be worth it just for those kike-trees he's got at the side. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,184</i>)

### kikeri
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> One good example of a variation of this game [of Blindman's Buff] is Kikeri (Ratipiko). `Kikeri is a sort of mixture betwen Blindman's Buff and Tag only instead of your eyes being bandaged you all play in the dark. One of the players stays outside in a lighted room while the others go into a dark room. When all are hidden, they call out `Kikeri Kikeri' and the catcher comes in. The others try to get passes [sic] into the other room and if they do they call out `Kikeri'. Whoever is caught has to go the next catcher.' <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/205</i>)

### kiki
 
 <i>n.</i> faeces [? children's word, not Maori]

>  <b>FRAME</b> Along the path from the lean-to, the 'dunny' beside the cypress tree and soon known as 'the cypress', was covered with the small fragrant roses known to us as 'dunny roses', and was without a door, while the dunny seat, long and wide like a beach dunny, was set over a deep hole half-filled with old 'kiki' (our word for faeces) in varying shades of brown, topped with faded pieces of newspaper, the <i>Oamaru Mail</i> and the <i>Otago Daily Times</i>. <i>(1984,Angel at My Table, An,51</i>)

### kikimutu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ACANTHiSiTTA CHLORiS \{ACANTHiDOSiTTA CHLORiS\}. (RiFLEMAN.) ... <i>Native names. </i>Titipounamu, \{Kikimutu,\} Kikirimutu, Pihipihi, Piripiri, Tokepiripiri, and Moutuutu. [(1873) 113] The Rifleman is the smallest of our New-Zealand birds; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,112</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 1/113, 114}]

### kikirimutu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ACANTHiSiTTA CHLORiS \{ACANTHiDOSiTTA CHLORiS\}. (RiFLEMAN.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Titipounamu, \{Kikimutu,\} Kikirimutu, Pihipihi, Piripiri, Tokepiripiri, and Moutuutu. [(1873) 113] The Rifleman is the smallest of our New-Zealand birds; .. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,112</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 1/113, 114}]

### Kiko
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred goat, a feral/dairy breed cross.

>  Under typical New Zealand hill country conditions, Kiko growth rates from birth to weaning average 180gms/day. <i>(Jan 1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,23</i>)

>  .. Garrick and Anne Batten have been exporting live Kiko goats to the Pacific islands for three years. <i>(Jan 1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  The Nelson breeder, exporter and author has already created his own Kiko breed. About 10 years ago the Kiko central flock was sold. <i>(Feb 2005,Country-Wide,55</i>)

>  The goats are the rare kiko breed, a composite of ferals and several domesticated breeds created by Garrick Batten in the 1980s. <i>(Sep 10, 2009,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

### kiko-kiko
 
 <i>n.</i> a Moriori evil spirit.

>  <b>HUNT</b> But the everlasting <i>kiko-kiko </i>was a terrible bugbear to old and young; not a person amongst them but had a tale to relate of mysterious visitations, more wild and wonderful than the Cock-lane ghost. <i>(1866,Chatham islands,35</i>)

### kikuyu grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass))

>  Norris led me to a shallow crater lined with kikuyu grass. <i>(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Native dune plants are giving way in many areas to the introduced perennial Kikuyu grass, partly because this is one of the few plants that can withstand (and even flourish) when trampled by people or livestock or when driven over by vehicles... Kikuyu is the name of a large, agricultural, Bantu-speaking, ethnic group of south-central Kenya, the country to which the grass is native. <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,20</i>)

### kilikiti
 
 <i>n.</i> cricket (See [also Samoan cricket](sa-sho.md#samoan-cricket))

>  Kilikiti (Pacific island-style cricket) was adapted from the British game more than 100 years ago... Norman says Kilikiti is the only activity a lot of the younger team members are interested in which still connects them to their heritage. <i>(Feb 21, 2002,Contact (Wgtn),5</i>)

>  The rules of kilkiti are a bit like those of cricket, but the game is something of a cross between cricket and a dance, with 20 lava lava-clad players in each side using a rubber ball and a three-sided bat, and colourful dancing to mark fielding successes. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,NZ Catholic,3</i>)

>  Tofilau Kerupi said kilikiti was not just a game. it was about protocol and strong values, such as respecting people and knowing one's place. <i>(Feb 21, 2005,NZ Herald,2</i>)

>  Laulima Mamea has a talent that makes him especially valuable in kilikiti... You can almost guarantee, says Laulima, that a kilkiti game will be riddled with arguments. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 77</i>)

>  Mangere teleports itself to a golden-sanded beach fringed with gently rustling coconut palms, a turquoise lagoon where turtles tumble, a tiny village with lots of little brown kids playing kilikiti. <i>(May 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

### kill
 
 <i>v.</i> (graffiti term)

>  Graffiti Speak... Kill: To hit or bomb excessively. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> i remember shortly after arrival in New Zealand watching the well-known dealer, Andrew Grant, picking `fats.' When asked by me for his hesitancy and discrimination in regard to several apparently prime blacks, his answer was that they `killed' badly. So many statements, nevertheless, continue to be accepted because it is nobody's interest to disprove them, that this dealer's belief is given for what it was worth. On the other hand, what Sandy Grant did not know of sheep was not worth knowing. <i>(1926,Tutira,362 fn 1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the process, or result, of mass slaughtering animals for meat

>  Another point was that the freezing companies were interested in establishing a continual kill ... in the interests of economy, rather than a seasonal kill just for export. <i>(Mar 23, 1963,Dominion,15</i>)

>  <i> kill ... 16. N.Z.</i> a seasonal tally of the number of stock killed at a meatworks. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,618</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 412; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 272]]

>  Put furse over the yard we are going to kill in, the mud being about two feet deep. <i>(Jul 8, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,47,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `Your turn to kill, you know ...' `You out of meat?' <i>(1947,Big Game,53</i>)

>  <b>CHERRiLL</b> No need to fash about feeding them people. i killed a couple o'days since, so there's plenty o'meat. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a total number of a specific stock type killed at a freezing works.

>  Lower schedules have slowed the cattle kill .. Most of the kill is presently made up of cows, he says, as culling is underway <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,43</i>)

>  Our kill this season is still well above 15 kg,' he says, 'with a lot more P lambs this year than Ys.' <i>(Jun 15, 1994,NZ Farmer,51</i>)

>  There may be more than that killed because the kill in some major North island dairying areas is up on last year ... <i>(Aug 1, 1996,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  He expects the kill to have peaked by the end of next week. <i>(Aug 22, 1996,NZ Farmer,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Here the <i>Crescent</i> made fast and killed but the whale sank and the gunsman was forced to cut. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,23</i>)

### kill out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to weigh at slaughter.

>  Breeders of the long-woolled sheep knew - he went on to point out - that a Lincoln would kill out as well as it felt, if not better. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1339</i>)

>  .. the shorn wether lamb kills out three to four pounds better at the works ... <i>(Dec 26, 1940,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Richmond advised lambs killed out at 31.63, 176 seconds. <i>(Mar 1943,Alexander Mowat Diaries,10</i>)

>  On April 1, 28 of these stores were sent to the works, where they killed out at 39.9lb and on April 22 a further draft of 24 killed out at 38.6 lb. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  .. on this type of country the recently-killed 3 and a half year old Friesian steers did much of their growing, killing out at up to 800 lb. <i>(Apr 25, 1968,NZ Farmer,2</i>)

>  Michael Bell regards hoggets' lambs as a bonus crop so he's not worried if they kill out at light weights in December. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,23</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> These [lambs] went to Fairfield on the hoof and killed out at the incredible average of over 42 pounds. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South island,239</i>)

>  Prime oxen, killing out at 270-295 kg have been fetching as much as $3.83/kg ... <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  The 1/4 EFx male lambs killed out heavier than the other lambs. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

>  People were sitting on their lambs but they are starting to move again. Small numbers of lambs are killing out at good weights ... <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,43</i>)

### kill-me-deads
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1930s R.Mason: Names given to food made up from leftovers; or often given as an answer to children when they ask `What is it?' to food of a type unknown to them.]

### killable
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Killable stock has been sent off to freezing works at Picton, Nelson and points south; some have found grazing in more favoured districts; a few farmers are desperate to quit old ewes. <i>(Jan 13, 1983,Marlborough Express</i>)

### killer
 
 <i>n.</i> ? A person or thing outstanding in its class; which `kills' competitors; ? a character. [ca1952 His old man's new car's a real killer (i.e, a real beauty) -->ca1952 He's a fair killer when he gets a few beers in. -->1983? The pace was a killer]



 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep to be killed for meat (on a farm, or at a freezing works) (See [cf. freezer](fly-fuz.md#freezer))

>  <b>SCOTT</b> You know the killers, don't you? ... i.e., the sheep that the boss used for his own mutton at the house. <i>(1897,How i Stole 10,000 Sheep,9</i>) [NOTES: [also AND: check punct.]]

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> We were to be treated to a portion of valuable stud ram for supper in place of the ordinary `killer' .... <i>(1907,Return of Joe,294</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> .. he had just brought in some sheep for `killers'. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,158</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> A new hand ... is wise investing in a good huntaway or a heading dog. He can practice [sic] on the `killers' i.e. the sheep kept handy for station use, or better still, a good noisy yard dog, as all station hands are liable to be called upon to assist in drafting, etc. <i>(1913,Country Work,33</i>)

>  <b>DAViDSON</b> The bulls ... when fat, were worth an extra [pound]1 per head ..., as they were such good 'killers'. <i>(1930,Sketch of His Life,30</i>)

>  <i>Killers, killing sheep.</i> - Sheep for station mutton. (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> We have seen the `dud' dog on a station that is capable of yarding `killers' in first clas style, because he was always used for this job. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,32</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The killers were fast merino [46] wethers. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> if you show an experienced dog a butcher's knife ... he will ... round up a mob of killers (sheep to be used as mutton for the homestead) without any further word of command. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,68</i>)

>  <i>killer n</i>. a sheep reserved for killing and eating on a sheep station. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>McCASKiLL</b> The livestock on Molesworth are not confined to the thousands of cattle, two dozen horses, a few killer sheep, and Miss Ann's tame pig. <i>(1969,Molesworth,248</i>)

>  <b>McCASKiLL</b> The livestock on Molesworth are not confined to the thousands of cattle, two dozen horses, a few killer sheep, and Miss Ann's tame pig. <i>(1969,Molesworth,248</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> `Young Charlie [a station hand] didn't close the gate properly ... and all the killers got out yesterday.' <i>(1970,Tat,98</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Andrew was hard pressed to find enough killers for butchering. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep (rarely a cattle beast) to be killed for consumption for the farm occupiers.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Got in some killers in morning. <i>(Oct 14, 1907,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  it might be inferred from the above that the sheep are there as followers in an intensive grazing system, but this is not so; they are killers, bought at a low price. <i>(Apr 29, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,36</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We wanted a mutton one night, and against my better judgment i took Sam out to run in the killers. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,74</i>)

>  .. no matter what meal it was, the killers would have contributed. Breakfast: hockey sticks and cackleberries ... <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

>  Wintering 2000 ewes, 1100 hoggets, 50 rams and killers, 120 breeding cows. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Press, The,59</i>)

>  <b>TAiT</b> with the exception of 50 lambs kept back as killers, all lambs from the flock of 1800 ewes are sent away as fats. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol iii,135</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> Our sheep were far too precious to turn into killers. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,28</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a pigdog which kills

>  Pig-dog types are many ... The writer's [J.P.Lewin's] considered classification is straight finders, straight bailers, straight holders and straight killers. ... inside it we have finder-bailers; bailer-holders; finder-bailer-holders; bailer-holder-killers and holder-killers. <i>(Dec 1951,Here & Now,15</i>)

### killer board
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a body set up to eradicate noxious plants or animals.

>  <b>LOVELL-SMiTH</b> Employing rabbiters who worked with guns, dogs, traps, gas, poison and night shooting, the killer boards made rapid progress. The Motunau Rabbit Board for example, which became a killer board in 1943, had by 1949 only a 'sprinking' of rabbits in its area ... <i>(2000,Hurunui Heritage,82</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Mackenzie Country has recently come under the control of the Killer Board and yet it remains to be seen how effective their [rabbit control] measures will be. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,38</i>)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMiTH</b> There were fears that the situation would return to what had existed before the advent of the killer boards. <i>(2000,Hurunui Heritage,84</i>)

### killer gas
 
 <i>n.</i> thermal hydrogen sulphide gas

>  ... the tests taken by the Rotorua Borough Council employees strongly suggested that hydrogen sulphine [sic] - known locally as `killer gas' because of its lethal features - caused the deaths. <i>(Feb 16, 1962,Evening Post,10</i>)

### killer kea
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native mountain-parrot Nestor notabilis that will hunt and kill sheep in the high country [by eating the flesh and fat around kidneys.]

>  <b>MCKENZiE</b> A killer kea is particular about the sheep it kills. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,101</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Lake Coleridge country has always been bad for killer keas. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South island,196</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> For 15 years i found it necessary to spend nights on the hills looking for 'killer keas.' <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,131</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> They [sheep] will collapse and remain on the ground until convinced that no tormentor, such as impatient shepherd (even killer keas will send them piggy) is about. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,61</i>)

### killer paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area where stock to be slaughtered for consumption on the station or farm are kept.

>  Hope all sheep farmers have taken their rams out of the orchard or killer paddock as it's only six weeks or so away from tupping date. <i>(Jan 20, 1982,Manawatu Farmer,5</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> There's only wethers in the killer paddock, and a few broken-down rams for dog-tucker. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,105</i>)

### killer policy
 
 <i>n.</i> Sc in respect of rabbits, opossums and other noxious animals; a policy of extermination (rather than `control").

>  There are few rabbits [around Gisborne]; a vigorous `killer' policy has seen to that. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,46</i>)

>  (Fielding) However, since the national adoption of the `killer policy' by rabbit boards in 1947, whereby the boards took direct action rather than leaving the responsibility for killing with property owners, a greater measure of control has been achieved. <i>(Oct 1963,NZ Geographer,166</i>)

>  <b>MciNNES</b> The struggle to eradicate [rabbits] went on for fifty years until the Government eventually introduced the `Killer Policy' in 1950. / Large sums of money were allocated and strychnine poisoning was carried out on a formidable scale, ... <i>(1969,Castle on the Run,40</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a policy decommercializing rabbits and allowing them to be killed without regard to their economic value.

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> .. the runholders adopted, largely because they had no choice, what today is sknown as the 'killer policy,' that is, the destruction of the rabbits without regard to any commercial value they might have. <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,36</i>)

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> Within comparatively recent years there was a successful revival of the export trade in rabbit, but this is now discouraged by the killer policy which seeks to decommercialise the rabbit and lower him to the status of nothing but a pest ... <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,37</i>)

### killer whale
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka killer]

>  The killer whale usually travels and hunts in 'schools' or packs of from three to a dozen or more individuals. <i>(Apr 21, 1917,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Just at present we are interested in a large school of killer whales, which have been playing along the edge of the ice ever since we arrived, apparently in the hope of picking up a meal. <i>(Jan 2, 1929,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Blackfish and killer whales are frequently seen from the island, three killers being observed as recently as Monday last. <i>(Jan 4, 1933,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  A school of killer whales played round the boat, almost upsetting it on one occasion. <i>(Mar 20, 1942,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The Killer Whales, or Grampuses, as they are often called, are the largest and most ferocious of the Dolphin family, and are often termed the wolves of the ocean. <i>(Jul 28, 1908,Wanganui Chronicle,8</i>)

>  4 boats returned without Seeing anything except a Killer - ... <i>(Apr 28, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Apr 28 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell Log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 435]

>  <b>McNAB</b> 4 boats returned without Seeing anything except a Killer - ... <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,435</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Apr 28 Capt. Joy <i> Mary Mitchell </i> Log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 435]

>  <b>POLACK</b> A more destructive and wholesale species of enemy is found in a species of GRAMPUS, called by the South seamen the `Killer,' which is said to be (man excepted) its [the Razorback whale's] greatest scourge. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/407</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the crew said they were killers a large fish of the cetacean family which however attack the right whale. They portend bad weather as last night we had a school of Black fish sporting around us and the gale followed. <i>(Nov 6, 1847,Journal,5/143</i>)

>  The killer (<i> Orcinus orca </i>) may be defined as follows: Dorsal fin high, erect; snout rounded; flippers large and very broad; teeth 10 to 13 pairs, large, about 1 in. in diameter; colour of animal black above, white below, and with one or more white patches on the upper surface. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/138</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i> Killer whales </i> - Grampus, or <i> Orcinus orca </i>. Attacks other whales and marine animals. Peranos found it was not worthwhile to hunt killer whales because of their low oil productivity. The few the Peranos did kill were killed for experimental purposes. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>) [NOTES: [glossary]]

### killing board
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area in which stock is slaughtered in a freezing works.

>  During the off-season the killing board at the Makarewa works was remodelled. <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,220</i>)

### killing chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dawe worked at the freezing works for years and the ritual slaughter of the killing chain, centre of a South island pastoral landscape given over to the spectre of meat, inform the imagery of Dawe's woodcuts, and his designs for ceramics (a dinner set) and carpets. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

### killing house
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm or station building where animals are killed and hung.

>  <i>Killing House</i>. - Slaughter house on station. (C.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> They ... tell [the newcomer musterer] where the killing house is to get some dog tucker... <i>(1951,High Country,18</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Killing House</i>. Slaughterhouse on the station. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Stables, killing house and cart shed make up the remaining buildings. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,10</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> Shiny had forgotten to bring me the meat, so i walked over to the killing-house to get some. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,70</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A concrete killing house has been erected alongside. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,248</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> killing house: farm or station slaughterhouse. Dogproof, flyproof and cool, it replaces the open air gallows. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,47</i>)

>  Auction..Sherenden Station 2 bedroom cottage, 6 bay haybarn, implement shed, workshop, single man's quarters, stable and killing house. <i>(Mar 18, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  implement shed, haybarn, killing house and skin shed, new 15 bay dog motels, airstrip and bin ... [killing-house] <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

### killing mob
 
 <i>n.</i> a mob of killing sheep (q.v.)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> ... Charlie left a gate open and a hundred wethers - the [station] killing mob - escaped through it. <i>(1970,Tat,95</i>)

### killing paddock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> ... i see'd [the dog] after some sheep in the killing paddock. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,281</i>)

>  <b>WiERZBiCKA</b> The killing paddock was the first fence put up; another was put up ... past the woolshed of scrub and a drafting yard made, but it was a slow process. <i>(1973,Wilson Family,126</i>) [NOTES: 1919 C.J.Wilson The Wilson Family in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 126]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... as soon as he could afford it George spread lime, starting in the killing paddock; ... <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,30</i>)

### killing pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small pen in sheep yards where sheep are drafted and slaughtered.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> My first attempt [to pen killing sheep] was at Molesworth. The killing pen is pretty difficult there. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45</i>)

>  <b>SUTRHERLAND</b> in view of this it has often been suggested that a career awaited Peter at the freezing works where, as a Judas sheep, he could end his days decoying his apprehensive fellows to their deaths in the killing pens. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,196,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> ... the killing pen is difficult there - the wing has broken down. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The shepherds were generally about the place in the evening. At the stables or the killing pen, in the forge. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,67</i>)

### killing season
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the optimum killing period of a freezing works during late spring and summer.

>  They also have a general day motor load of 100 to 200 kilowats during the killing season, falling off very much during the slack season. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/20</i>)

>  The normal killing season lasts from November to June, the heaviest months being from January to April. The South island is normally about one month later than the North island. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,630</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Lance had never had such a holiday job [in the freezing works]. He'd never been part of the Killing Season. <i>(1979,Living in the Maniatoto,52</i>)

>  A loadout ban during an otherwise peaceful-looking killing season would have been a sad affair. <i>(Nov 27, 1980,Nation, The</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> The normal killing season lasts from November to June ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,630</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> if weather failed in the winter, wool went in fadges to the Petrie dryer in the Kaiti Freezing Works fellmongery which lay idle once the killing season ended. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,103</i>)

>  The most striking feature of the killing season to date is the early and surprisingly great advance in the killing of lambs. <i>(Jan 5, 1929,Hawera Star,14</i>)

### killing shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm slaughterhouse.

>  <i>killing-shed NZ & Austral</i>. farm building where stock is killed. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,412</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Usually the killing shed is a single-roomed, timber-framed structure with weatherboards and corrugated iron roof. Kuriheka station in North Otago has a killing shed built in 1901 of limestone, carefully dressed. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,224</i>)

>  Farm buildings include a new four-stand woolshed and covered yards, tractor shed, implement and hay shed, killing shed and dog motels. <i>(Mar 20, 1991,NZ Farmer,30</i>)

>  Buildings include a three-stand woolshed .. killing shed and dog motels. <i>(May 19, 1993,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  Auction on site - Concrete Killing Shed. <i>(Mar 23, 1996,Napier Daily Telegraph,24</i>)

>  Buildings include a three stand woolshed with a 450 night pen capacity, large three bay implement shed, a fertiliser/granary shed, tool shed, killing shed, loose box ... <i>(Mar 30, 2001,Dominion,22</i>)

>  <b>Geary</b> The suit [of armour] arrived in kitset form over a period of weeks. He assembled it in the killing shed on an old chopping block. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,163</i>)

### killing sheep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The most trying ordeal for a new-chum musterer is getting in the killing sheep. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,45</i>)

### killing sheet
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a detailed freezing works schedule of a farmer's slaughtered stock.

>  Payment is made on freezing works killing sheets. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,629</i>)

>  Twenty years' ago the 'fats' had to be driven to the nearest railway siding for loading on to trucks. Then the killing-sheet arrives and all sentiment is forgotten in calculations concerning the amount which will be left after taxes have been paid. <i>(Dec 14, 1953,Free Lance,44</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> A bad killing sheet with a lot of seconds is also the last thing a drafter wants. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,39</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> They watched any changes in the grading of beef; and the killing sheets of their lamb grades were, and are, a puzzle ... <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,350</i>)

>  The AFFCO killing sheet includes the skin value with the carcase price and only shows the assessed net wool pull for the line of lambs over all grades on the kill sheet. <i>(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

>  Have you ever looked at kill sheets and wondered what the Animal Health grading meant? Below is a selection of health issues reported in kill sheets. <i>(Feb 19, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert farmer</i>)

>  The schedule typically penalises underweight and overfat lambs. if using scales to draft up to a limit, you should always analyse last year's kill sheets very closely to more accurately assess the killing out percentage .. if you are using an agent you should check the consistency and accuracy of your drafter against the killing sheets. [kill sheet; killing-sheet] <i>(Dec 21, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert farmer Livestock</i>)

### killing space
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the slaughterman and equipment availability at a freezing works.

>  Lots of pressure on drafters for killing space. <i>(Dec 23, 1976,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  A yarding of 7000, a return of the dry weather and shortage of killing space saw good cutting shorn ewes selling at about $8. <i>(Dec 14, 1988,NZ Farmer,42</i>)

>  A lack of killing space and winter feed have been the two major factors in the fall in sheep numbers. <i>(Jul 13, 1995,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  it's not just a case of price for farmers but also killing space availability. <i>(Aug 15, 1996,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

### killmong
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kirimoko](kee-kiw.md#kirimoko))

### kills
 
 <i>n.</i> marbles term

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### kilt
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to trim belly and leg wool.

>  <i>Kilt, to</i>.-To shear the legs and belly wool of a double fleecer in the autumn. Another term for the process is <i>to trace high. </i> <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Kilt</i>. - To shear the legs and belly wool of a double-fleecer (q.v.) in the autumn. Another verb for the process is <i>to trace high. </i> <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,372</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> kilt, to: to shear off only the belly and leg wool; practised duting the cold of the late autumn for lambs grazing on tall rape or for any double-fleecers that must remain unshorn throughout the winter. Sheep men, following the practice of clipping draught horses and hunters will often do their kilting "trace high." <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,47</i>)

### Kim Hill haircut
 
 <i>n.</i> a close-cut haircut

>  ...a woman with a Kim Hill haircut ... <i>(Apr 19, 2005,Dominion Post,B8?</i>) [NOTES: Jane Bowron]

### kina
 
 <i>n.</i> a sea-urchin, a sea egg.

>  "Talking of kinas," says the talkative bloke who sells them, "an Asian lady who asked one day to try one now buys eight $10 punnets of kinas every week. Must have got the taste." <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,15</i>)

>  Among the rocks are kina, which love eating <i>Ecklonia</i>. They are competent climbers, motoring like miniature army tanks onto a holdfast, then inching up a metre-long stalk to the head of the plant, where they eat the strap-like fronds. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  There's kina and corn, island chow mein (vermicelli and canned corn beef), creme de seaweed. <i>(Jan 1996,North & South,89</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Kina - the sea-urchin or sea-egg. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|ina s. A sea egg: <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,164</i>)

>  <i>KiNA, OR SEA URCHiN</i> ... Cut the shell in half ... leaving only the yellow pulp which may be scooped out ... and taken raw as a relish ... Another method is to ... roast, and serve hot. <i>(1908,Souvenir of All Nations Fair,6</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... 500 large smoked eels, 4 sacks of sea-eggs (<i>kina</i>), 1 sack of <i>pawa</i> (a large shellfish), 22 small bags of<i> karaka</i> berries, 2 sacks of watermelons, ... <i>(1930,Maori, The</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> in two feet of water, fixed to the rocks, were shellfish - paua, kuku, kina. Netta ... dredged up kinas. ... Netta took the tongues from the kinas and put them into a dish with vinegar. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,153</i>)

>  They found good-sized kinas under the rocks. <i>(1977,NZ Short Stories,60</i>) [NOTES: 1961 Hilliard in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories 60-61]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> ... the common green kina, <i>Evechinus chloroticus. </i> <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,178</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> A whare by the seashore/ Where you can look for crabs and kina <i>(1979,Collected Poems,538,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kina </i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>BURTON</b> Kina are a species of greenish-purple sea urchin,10-15cm across and covered with prickly spines. Common on all New Zealand coasts, they can be found at low tide in rock crevices and under rock ledges. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,63</i>)

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> kina <i>sea egg </i> <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kina <i>sea egg </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> A cousin of his in Kaikoura providentially sent over kinas on the bus that day. <i>ibid.</i> 466 Kina = sea-egg or sea urchin, delicious! <i>(1983,Bone People, The,110</i>)

>  <b>BENTLEY & FRASER</b> Ecology freaks at Tryphena/ Say `Taihoa' on mussels and kina;/ <i>(1985,Grand Limerick Tour, A,No.41</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> A dairy [at Thames] was selling hot pies and fish bait, a fishshop was selling dabs (baby flounder) and crab sticks ... and pots of ex-Chatham islands kina roe. <i>(1985,G'day Country,56</i>)

>  <i>kina n.</i> NZ edible sea-urchin. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,412</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 272]]

>  [species name] sea urchin .. [scientific name] <i>Evechinus chloroticus</i> .. [other name(s)] sea egg, kina <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Sea urchin Evechinus chloroticus </i>... Alternatively sea egg or kina. Restricted to New Zealand, but similar sea urchins occur in most seas. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,172</i>)

>  ...there's kina mousse with honeyed ginger... <i>(Oct 19, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),16</i>)

>  Even if you have to close your eyes and hold your nose, you should definitely try kina... As a child, you and your friends smashed a kina against the rocks and didn't like what you found: an oily-looking mass of black and grey goo... Despite kina being prolific breeders and voracious, aggressive eaters (they compete for broken kelp with paua and end up eating the paua out of house and home), our national kina resource, however large, is fragile. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

>  The sweet smell, the dark heavy air, the pale gold beaches above the black water, the piles of pale green kina shells jewelled in white like Faberge eggs are as familiar as if i'd left yesterday. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  We went out dredging for kina. <i>(Aug 1962,Sea Spray,79</i>)

>  i ordered kuku and kina fritters with horopito and lemon creme fraiche (mussel and kina fritters with bush pepper) <i>(Aug 2005,Capital Times,17-23 Aug / 20</i>)

>  Kina (sea-eggs) and paua shellfish.. are very plentiful. <i>(Mar 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> When pohutukawa bleed / their short-lived brilliance / yellow-fat and full / the kina are at Wai-o-Rore <i>(1986,Countless Signs,28</i>) [NOTES: From Hone Tuwhare (1964) : Wai-o-Rore]

>  The festival is a celebration of strictly local taste treats, ranging from Marlborough's renowned Sauvignon Blanc to stuffed mussels, sea eggs (kina),.. and Piedmontese beef. <i>(Jun 1992,Cuisine,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a spiky hairstyle [ORIGIN: Maori, figurative use]

>  <b>Geary</b> By then Gary had cut off his Janola-bleached mohawk and given himself a kina. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,106</i>)

### kina pate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The kina pate recipe was published in Whakatu Marae's monthly panui. <i>(Feb 3, 2009,Nelson Mail,9</i>)

### kina roe
 
 <i>n.</i> sea-egg ova

>  Closer to home, i've enjoyed kina roes draped over nori rolls at Catch sushi bar in Courtenay Place and as part of a seafood platter at Kai in the Bay in island Bay... And if that still seems too much for your eyes to cope with, then try your kina roes a la Francaise, buried deep within an omelette. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

>  Kina roe can be bought either fresh or in 250g pots from the many fish markets around the country. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 52</i>)

>  Tastier kina eggs could soon be a boom industry, thanks to Norwegian research to be trialled in Wellington... However, because New Zealand kina roe was considered inferior to the Asian urchins.. an export market had not developed... He said Norwegian researchers had been studying ways of enhancing kina roe for a decade. <i>(Sep 6, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  He says he can't stomach kina roe, he's not fond of wet fish and he doesn't like crayfish. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 68</i>)

### kina slammer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Then i.. try a bit of stinging-nettle soup, which is only okay, and down an octopus patty with a kina slammer while the Kokatahi Band belts out the Maori Battalion marching song. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

### kinaki
 
 <i>n.</i> relish

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|inaki,<i>s</i>. Victuals added for variety's sake. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,164</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> ... as Tarawhata had caught a fat young `titi', ... my natives had a relish, or as they called it a `kinaki', for their fern-root and damper. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,239</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 26 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 239]

>  <b>MACMORRAN</b> i send you by Henry a pot of English blackcurrant jam - as a <i>kinaki. </i> <i>(1969,Octavius Hadfield,77</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Rev. Cotton letter in 1969 Macmorran Octavius Hadfield 77]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Rowing up rivers you see little deposits of shells: ... i found they were the selections of the Norway rats, who, by diving for these freshwater pipis, provide a <i>kinaki</i> (relish) for their vegetable suppers. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,93fn {1888 1/88}</i>) [NOTES: a1873 Rev. T. Chapman, Rotorua in 1873 Buller Birds of NZ 93fn {1888 1/88}]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kinaki, n.</i> a Maori word for food eaten with another kind to give it a relish.... <i>(1898,Austral English,248</i>)

>  '`Kinaki. Victuals, added for variety's sake. <i>(1820,Grammar and Vocabulary of Language of NZ,164</i>)

>  `if it be a Maori who is taken by me he will also be made into a kinaki for my cabbage.' -- <i>(1873,AJHR,' vol. iii. G. i, p. 5</i>)

>  `Fifty years ago it would have been a poor hapu that could not afford a slave or two as a kinaki, or relish, on such an occasion.' <i>(1878,TrNZi,9/71</i>) [NOTES: R. C. Barstow]

>  The kumara ... with any food ... as birds, fish, or other <i>kinaki</i> (relish), are piled on the stones ... <i>(1903,TrNZi,35/23</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> if the foods [of the early Maori] were various, so were also the means of procuring them. if his kinaki (relish) were fish, his predatory instincts invented the means to catch them. <i>ibid..</i> 273 in the bygone days we had a kinaki of pork to our potatoes; ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,17</i>)

>  <i>kinaki n</i>. any food used with others as a relish. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  They could [22] have satisfied most of the `kinaki' [ed fn: Relish eaten with other food] wants from the coast, but preferred forest products. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/21,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>REED</b> To the water, fern-root and damper brought with them was added as <i>kinaki</i>, or relish, a fat young<i> titi</i>, or mutton-bird, which Tarawhata had caught in a hole in a cliff <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,67</i>)

>  <i>Kinaki</i>: relish <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The <i>kai</i> was <i>kumara</i> and the <i>kinaki</i> was pork, both out of the <i>hangis. </i> <i>(1948,Taina,81</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The <i>kai</i> was <i>kumara</i> and the <i>kinaki</i> was pork, both out of the <i>hangis. </i> <i>(1948,Taina,81</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> After a time, the [tutu] juice would set and was eaten as a relish <i>(kinaki)</i> or with fernroot or fungus. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,6</i>)

### kind
 
 <i>adj.</i> of country (cf kindly).

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... this country is green all the year round. All lambs go fat, ... This is `kind' country, which has that something about it that takes a stockman's eye. <i>(1973,Big Country,3,1977 ed.</i>)

### kindly
 
 <i>adj.</i> used by goldminers of likely country for gold

>  The favourable or `kindly' country of the miner is a completely propilitized andesite or dacite 1913 <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 15 (N.S.): 63</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser-Waihi)]

>  in the Aroha Subdivision, as in other mining-acres of the Hauraki Peninsula, a distinction between `kindly' and `dead' country is made by miners. ...... `Kindly' country for each locality is country in which the lodes of that locality may carry valuable ore, while `dead' country is country in which the lodes become barren and small, or pinch out altogether. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 16 (NS): 98-9</i>) [NOTES: (Henderson and Bartrum-Hauraki)]

>  The miner, quickly recognizing these characters, uses them in his search for ore-bodies, and calls any rock exhibiting them `favourable' or `kindly' country. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/115</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan)]

>  The decomposed andesitic lavas generally possess a characteristic yellowish-brown or grey colour, and form the "kindly country" of the local miners; while the solid andesites and tuffs possess a greenish or dark-blue or purple colour and are generally known as "hard country." <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/38</i>)

### kindly sandstone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Above this is a belt of so-called "kindly sandstone," and then a band of what is known as "jointy" or "shingly" country occurs, and consists of a fine-grained light-brown rock, which is much shattered and traversed by joints in every direction. <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/21</i>)

### kindy
 
 <i>n.</i> kindergarten [(prob. not used much before the 1950s). (HWO)]

>  They are sitting on the edge of a sand pit which is a part of the history of the kindy. <i>(Sep 22, 1999,Norwester,5</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Two kids at school now and the little joker's at kindy <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,146</i>)

>  [Caption] <i>Kindy Open House</i> : Hataitai Kindergarten .. <i>(Jun 15, 1962,Hataitai Herald</i>)

>  if a child eats his vegies as he should, he is soon ready for <i>kindy. </i> <i>(1966,English as it is spoken in NZ,173</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> New Zealanders are adept at abbreviations. Examples such as `vegy', `hotty' and `kindy' are easy enough to work out ... <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,141</i>)

>  New Kindies To Be Built. Twelve new kindergartens will be built...the Minister of Education, Mr Pickering, said yesterday. <i>(Nov 4, 1972,Dominion,28</i>)

>  <b>SiMONS</b> `You don't have to do a thing except collect her from kindy and keep an eye on her until i get home.' <i>(1979,Harper's Mother,134</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>kindy</i>: KiNDERGARTEN <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,58</i>)

>  [Caption] Kindy Ball. <i>(Jun 8, 1968,Photo News,78</i>) [NOTES: Wanganui Photo News - there may be others]

>  [Hdg] Gun lobby blasts kindy... The National Rifle Association has singled out the kindy for mention in its first annual "Rosie" awards, bestowed on those who challenge its pro-gun stance. <i>(Feb 14, 2001,Evening Post,13</i>)

### kindy kid
 
 <i>n.</i> kindergarten child

>  Many of you have been 'kindy kids,' [sic] as kindergartens have been around for a long time. <i>(Jan 31, 2006,Kapi-Mana news,21</i>)

>  A report of a threat to kindy kids is unfair to a mentally ill patient, writes Ruth Harrison. <i>(Nov 23, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  [headline] Kindy kids at the fire station. <i>(Feb 28, 2006,Wairarapa Midweek,4</i>)

### King
 
 <i>adj.</i> shortened form of Kingite

>  <b>BULLER</b> Here [at Ngaruawahia], in the heart of `Kingism', they say in English our national anthem ... no communication with any white person was permitted, by the `King' natives. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,134</i>)

>  i went to Allexandria en rout to Kopua to see the great King meeting. Allexandria is a small frontier town. 29th [We] got our horses and started for the Kings camp at Hauwa ... 'he;KiNG <i>(Apr 28, 1879,Chudleigh Diary,279,1950 ed.</i>)

### king
 
 <i>n.</i> Often suffixed, either loosely or compounded, to a noun to connote one who is an adept or expert at, chief of or in control of, or a continual practiser of the particular thing (cf -artist).

>  [Of a politician] The `Yes-no' king. <i>(Jul 21, 1906,NZ Truth,57-7-2</i>)

>  <b>HAYDON</b> Voices of the `two-up' and `housie' kings <i>(1914,NZ Soldiers in England,7</i>)

>  .... the greatest `swi-up king'. <i>(Sep 5, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,28</i>)

>  Sergeants known as `C.B. Kings' <i>(Sep 28, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,28</i>)

>  ... the bull ring bayonet kings. <i>(Aug 30, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,65</i>)

>  <b>TREADWELL</b> One of the Crown and Anchor `kings' was reported to have said, `That damned sergeant has skinned all the schools and taken the boards as security for the moneys ... owing to him!' <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,31</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Tank Tarrant, the loot king, cracking open a German paymaster's safe. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,70</i>)

### King Arthur's cakes
 
 <i>n.</i> a fungus

>  Consider the Stinkhorn. The Fairy Club or the the Shaggy Parasol Mushroom. The Slippery Lizard Tuft, King Arthur's Cakes, the brain [sic] Mushroom or the Dead Man's Fingers. <i>(Dec 10, 1988,Dominion,3</i>) [NOTES: (David Hill)]

### king barracouta
 
 <i>n.</i> southern kingfish, gemfish (See [also king couta](kee-kiw.md#king-couta))

>  <i>GEMFiSH Rexea solandri </i>(Silver kingfish, southern kingfish, hake, king barracouta) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,287,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH ... <i>Other names: Jordanidia solandri</i>; kingfish, hake, barraconda; king barracouta, Tasmanian kingfish (Australia); tikati (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,280</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> KiNG BARRACOUTA: <i>See Southern Kingfish and Hake, the two names the King Couta goes under in New Zealand. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 200 SOUTHERN KiNGFiSH J<i>ordanidia solandri</i>. This is the King Barracouta of Australia and the so-called Hake of more southern New Zealand waters. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,30</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] King barracouta 2011-8 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### King Billy
 
 <i>adj.</i> silly [rhyming slang]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> KiNG BiLLY (adj) Silly, stupid, insane. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,250</i>) [NOTES: rhyming slang]

### king bolt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>King bolt:</i> The bolt controlling the horizontal circular movement of the drifter-drill on the arm attached to the column, and most frequently used to line up the drill with hole. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),326</i>)

### King Caesar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> The most widely known and most popular tagging game in all historical periods - in fact, the most popular game in New Zealand - was undoubtedly the game known variously as <i>Bar the Door </i>(E-8); All Over; Barbadoor; Barley; Barley Goats; Bar the Gate; Black Bull Bee; Blackthorn; Broken Barley; By the Door; Caesar; Father Door; Free Pass; Goosey; Goosey Up and Down; Holding; Horney;Julius Caesar; King Caesar; King's Den; King Dick; King O'Seenie; King O'Weenie; King Seenie; King Seize-Her; King-a-Sene; Last man Over; Last Over Call; Pass Over; Punch King; Punch King Seenio; Red Rover; and Running Through the Middle. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,81</i>)

>  Bull in the Ring required strength; King Caesar, a joyous cunning game. <i>(1978,History of Drummond & Gladfield,42</i>)

### king clubmoss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHAPPLE</b> 'King club moss, amazing stuff. Still - it vibrates like wire, and if you picked it, it'd still be green in five month's time.' <i>(2002,Te Araroa: the New Zealand Trail,36</i>)

### king cobber
 
 <i>n.</i> a best friend

>  <b>WiLSON</b> i remember also her look of complicity whenever we met away from the farm, in town or at school. it was as if we were king cobbers, engaged on some secret project together. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,6</i>)

### King Country
 
 <i>n.</i> an area in the central North island roughly between Te Awamutu and Taupo, orig. under the aegis of the Maori King.

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> The `King' Country <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),157</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> i found the King Country - the very place i wanted to go to; arranged with a man to go with me. <i>(Aug 1877,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Aug T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 260]

>  Perhaps in the `King Country' fashions have not changed. <i>(Apr 13, 1878,Field, The</i>) [NOTES: 1878 April 13 The Field in 1879 Ollivant Hinemoa 121]

>  On the Ocurrence of ... Gneissic Rocks in the King-country. <i>(1893,TrNZi,25/353</i>)

>  The Maru-iwi lived contiguous to the east coast, in a district till lately known as `the King-country'. <i>(1900,TrNZi,32/365</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> `What a refutation of the outcry - `Drunken King Country! Debauched King Country! Sly-grog selling King Country!' till one sickens of the of the sound.' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,188</i>)

>  <b>BRADBURY</b> The name ["King Country"] originated in 1856. in December of that year a large meeting of natives was held at Taupo, when they decided to have their own king [Northern Boundary - the Puniu River, a little south of Te Awamutu - to the coast near Aotea Harbour north of Kawhia. Eastern boundary extended to the shores of Lake Taupo; southern boundary stretched in an irregular line from Lake Roto Aira to Paraninipi ..... on the North Taranaki Bight. Area roughly 4,000,000 acres] <i>(1923,Settlement of the King Country,9</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> He had been invalided home from the Great War ... Arriving at his little farm in the King Country, he found it had deteriorated ... <i>(1931,Such is Life,58</i>)

>  The experiment was carried out ... in the King-country ... west of the Maitroa Post-office. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/57</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> `The King Country' ... already its signification has been forgotten by many who use the phrase. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,87</i>)

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> There was not another man like him anywhere in the King Country. <i>(1959,Stone, The,33</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> Until the eighties no settler dare enter the `king and his followers lived in sullen isolation ... <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,142</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> Until the eighties no settler dare enter the `king and his followers lived in sullen isolation ... <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,142</i>)

>  <b>NORRiS</b> These military settlements were formed with the object of stopping the Maoris, who had retreated to what is now called the King Country ... <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,1</i>)

>  The so-called King Country is rapidly undergoing development. <i>(Mar 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Hitherto, the attention of these three places, during the present attack, has been directed towards the Tuhua District, or as it is more vaguely termed, the King Country. <i>(Feb 28, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  if a distinct arrangement can be made in reference to the tract known as the King Country, by which the Government and the King natives will enter into a sort of partnership,.. that would be the best method by far. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The term "King Country Pumice Lands" is largely a misnomer in that the area which can actually be described by the words is much smaller than the title as generally used gives credit for. <i>(Jul 20, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,55</i>)

>  The latter is making me rapidly forget my poor old girl of the king country (sic). <i>(Dec 27, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,i/9 : 201</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The King country (New Zealand) method of breaking in fern land is well worth recording. <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,123</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> That was the notice the other day in a King Country paper. `Ladies, a basket' is a customary dance-night formula in the outback, and a convenient arrangement it is. Those liberal baskets of home-made dainties outdo anything you can buy for the money in your town shops. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,158</i>)

>  <i>King Country n.</i> central part of North island of N.Z., once belonging to Maori king and at that time therefore closed to Europeans. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> By that time one of my sisters had been to stay with another King Country relative, on a farm even more remote than my uncle's. <i>(1973,Once is Enough,13</i>)

>  <i>King Country n.</i> an area in the centre of North island, New Zealand. [<i> The Maori King</i>, name given to Maori chief there] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,619</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 272]]

### King Country spanner
 
 <i>n.</i> [(often with the crest of the town of Taumarunui on it); cf Taranaki spanner.33 [?dateline] Some soft sheilas start huntin' round for a King Country spanner but usin' a bottle opener has always struck me as a sure sign of a joker who probably uses a deodorant or votes Labour. ... ?? Ponga and Hangi now roll off my tongue and my son has taught me that a `King Country spanner' is a bottle of gin in the back seat.]

>  <i>(Jun 18, 1956,Press, The,12</i>) [NOTES: [R. Mason]]

>  There are those who nostalgically look back on the days of no-licence and sly grog and wonder where the move to have the King Country union discard the traditional `King Country spanner' as its emblem originated. For the uninitiated, the King Country spanner was more than just a bottle-opener - it was a symbol of stout-hearted refusal to submit meekly to a `dry' regime that King Country thought was unjustly imposed on it. You could always get `it' if you knew how. <i>(Apr 27, 1958,NZ Truth,273/7/14</i>) [NOTES: (often with the crest of the town of Taumarunui on it); cf Taranaki spanner. 33 [?dateline] Some soft sheilas start huntin' round for a King Country spanner but usin' a bottle opener has always struck me as a sure sign of a joker who probably uses a deodorant or votes Labour. ... ?? Ponga and Hangi now roll off my tongue and my son has taught me that a `King Country spanner' is a bottle of gin in the back seat.]

>  <i>King Country spanner n.</i> bottle opener. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  G. Griffiths (Dunedin) notes: `not recognised by the contestant (from Hamilton); i had never heard of it.' <i>(Oct 2, 1971,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Personality Squares TV programme (Griffiths Collection)]

### king couta
 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of king barracouta.

>  <b>WiLSON</b> KiNG BARRACOUTA: <i>See Southern Kingfish and Hake, the two names the King Couta goes under in New Zealand. </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,30</i>)

### king devil
 
 <i>n.</i>
