### King Dick
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> nickname of Richard John Seddon, Prime Minister 1893-1906

>  in the early days of the Coast, before King Dick took to Colonial politics, he became a member of the local Road Board. <i>(1904,NZ illustrated Magazine,10/179</i>)

>  After listing several big guns of the opposition one ceases to wonder how King Dick has managed to boss the country for a dozen years. ... [ibid 1/1/2] ... King Dick Seddon <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/1/1</i>)

>  She had left ... with the Right Honorable Richard Seddon on board. ... King Dic [sic] and party were on shore and i soon was with them. <i>(Mar 26, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,432,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRYCE</b> RiCHARD SEDDON, or King Dick as he was commonly and affectionately called, was born at St. Helens in Lancashire in 1845. <i>(1921,Modern Democracies,2/300,1020 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PEMBER REEVES</b> To those of us who knew `King Dick' it does not seem possible to imagine him asleep, inert, or passive in any world, however vast and remote. <i>(1950,Long White Cloud, The,305</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> From the late nineties until 1906 New Zealand was ruled by a benevolent despot known as `King Dick' ... . Neither in the cabinet, ... nor in the House was there anyone to rival Seddon's pre-eminence. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,2/300</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> it was a monument to `King Dick', New Zealand Prime Minister Richard John Seddon. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,7</i>)

>  He was a rabid racist .. who likened Chinese people to monkeys. He also opposed votes for women, and on both of those counts his nickname, King Dick, was well earned. <i>(Sep 1, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The Town Council of St Helen's are considering putting an iron fence round the house in which King Dick was born. <i>(Jan 9, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>REEVES295</b> [Hdg] "King Dick". <i>ibid.</i> 297 The old Conservatives did not like subjection to the rule of "King Dick". <i>(1924,Long White Cloud, The</i>) [NOTES: 1924 edition]

>  <b>BROOKiNG</b> Duncan's loss of enthusiasm was partly explained by the fact that "King Dick" Seddon had told a delegation.. that he was not in favour of agricultural colleges. <i>(1977,Massey - its Early Years,18</i>)

>  He was the most active Prime Minister since his idol, King Dick Seddon. <i>(Aug 9, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,9</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> And his nickname, 'King Dick', indicates both the manner in which he bestrode his contemporaries and the extent to which he concentrated power in his own hands. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,233</i>)

>  <b>BiLLiNG</b> He had been named after Richard John Seddon ("King Dick"), the great politician of the days of New Zealand's emergence as a nation and of whom a verdigris-tinged and warrior-like statue stood in the grounds of Parliament House, Wellington. <i>(1965,Forbush and the Penguins,90</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a kingfisher

>  <b>THOMSON</b> King-dick is the colonial boy's name for the native Kingfisher. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,59</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> nickname of Riki Tatahunga

>  Riki Tatahunga, better known as ~King Dick', died recently at Tauranga. ... his illness was ascribed to witchcraft, brought on because he appeared as advocate against his own tribe in a Land Court case. <i>(1905,TrNZi,37/35</i>) [NOTES: Riki Tatahunga, better known as `King Dick', died recently at Tauranga. Also given as TrNZi 35/1904]



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> = kingy-seeny, King Caesar, king's prick

>  a children's game: in which one `catcher' tries to stop selected runners from breaking through to `home'. (He can select one runner at a time) if he catches the runner, the runner stays and helps him; if a runner breaks through, all can run <i>(1935,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1935 Palmerston North Primary School (p.c. N.C.C. Small)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> it was [at Blockhouse Bay school c1950s] Kingaseeny (or Bulldog, King Dick or Bar-the-Door for those outside Auckland) at lunchtime, rugby on Wednesday, the gang after school, .. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,25</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> The most widely known and most popular tagging game in all historical periods - in fact, the most popular game in New Zealand - was undoubtedly the game known variously as <i>Bar the Door </i>(E-8); All Over; Barbadoor; Barley; Barley Goats; Bar the Gate; Black Bull Bee; Blackthorn; Broken Barley; By the Door; Caesar; Father Door; Free Pass; Goosey; Goosey Up and Down; Holding; Horney;Julius Caesar; King Caesar; King's Den; King Dick; King O'Seenie; King O'Weenie; King Seenie; King Seize-Her; King-a-Sene; Last man Over; Last Over Call; Pass Over; Punch King; Punch King Seenio; Red Rover; and Running Through the Middle. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,81</i>)

### King Dick spanner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> ... using my heavy King Dick spanner as a hammer <i>(1994,Pure Gold,134</i>)

### king fern
 
 <i>n.</i> para; a popular name for a variety of large ferns, esp. <i> Marattia</i> spp. [(Early obs. king's fern.)]

>  ...did you know ... that the roasted tuberous roots of the majestic king fern or para, <i>Marattia salicina</i>, were once considered a great delicacy? <i>(May 23, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

>  (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  <b>DOBBiE</b> (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> king-fern <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>)

>  (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1925,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  king-fern A popular name used in New Zealand for a large, handsome fern (<i>marattia</i>) which also grows in Australia and some other countries. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),231,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> i ... obtained specimens of the Para, a fine fern of the <i>Marattia genus</i>. [ed fn] <i>Marattia salicinia</i>, King fern. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,54</i>) [NOTES: 1959 Taylor Early Travs 54 (1844 Colenso Excurs Nthn isd)]

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> <i>MARATTiA SALiCiNA</i> (Marattiaceae). The king fern occurs in many Pacific islands. Big colonies used to grow in gullies in heavy lowland forests, ... but they are rare now. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,216</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Marattia salicina</i> Smith Common names: <i>King fern, horseshoe fern</i> Maori name: <i>Para</i> ... The `horseshoes' cut from the rhizome were baked or boiled and were a good remedy for diarrhoea. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,29</i>)

>  <i>king fern NZ = horseshoe fern. </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,413</i>) [NOTES: [nf. CCON]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> ...titoki ... and king fern (<i>Marattia salicina</i>) have [pulvini] at the bases of the leaflet petioles. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,37</i>)

>  We have our hearts set on purchasing a King Fern or two. <i>(May 1989,NZ Gardener,7</i>)

>  The pith of the trunk and leaf bases of mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) were frequently eaten by Maori, especially when travelling, and the king fern or para (<i>Marattia salicina</i>) was cultivated for its tuberous root, which was condisered a great delicacy when roasted. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

>  Birds can use [forest fragments] as 'stepping stones' to move around between the larger forest areas and, even in small fragments, threatened species like the magnificent king fern have been found. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Cyathea species: ponga, mamaku

>  in 1906 quot, is <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) [cit lost] <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No. 1 (N.S.) 12</i>) [NOTES: (Bell-Hokitika)]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> (Gloss) king fern: A local [Westland] name for the mamaku, <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>, the tall tree-fern with a wide frond-spread. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,420</i>)

>  There were very big kanuka trees nearby, and many small trees and shrubs on our section, including ponga, which Dad called `king ferns'. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: (Sinclair `At the Point')]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> Todea barbara

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Todea barbara </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The true KiNG FERN (<i>Todea barbara </i>) occurs only in the far north ... though it also native to Australia and South Africa... The PARA or HORSESHOE FERN (<i>Marattia salicina</i>) is often spoken of as the king fern, but this name belongs more appropriately to <i>Todea barbara </i>, a close relative of the British royal fern (<i>Osmunda</i>) ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,116,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Leptopteris superba</i> king's fern. <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### king hit
 
 <i>n.</i> a knockout punch [also as verb]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Finally the discussion got on to the definition of a `king hit'. Apparently during the evening someone or other was alleged to have administered a king hit, because he laid his opponent out cold.Then there came the man who said that one could administer a king hit without laying out the opponent. <i>(1975,Men of the Milford Road,101</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MacKENZiE</b> <i>king hit</i> an unexpected and crushing blow usually causing severe injury <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,97</i>)

### King Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> = Kingite

>  <b>NiCHOLL</b> We then went up to the villages ... which we found cram full of King Maoris who have given themselves up. <i>(Jul 19, 1864,Journal,267</i>)

>  <b>GUDGEON</b> The [friendly Maoris] .... as a rule, disliked us quite as much as the King Maories did. <i>(1879,Reminiscences of the War in NZ,18</i>)

### king moss
 
 <i>n.</i> dawsonia, giant moss

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Of all the ground dwellers in the forest none equals in stature the DAWSONiA or KiNG MOSS <i>(Dawsonia superba)</i>, which though native also to Australia and Tasmania, nevertheless reaches its largest dimensions in the forests north of Westland, more particularly the [66] kauri forests. ... The normal height ranges from eight inches to a foot, but many specimens attain twice that height ... Truly it deserves the alternative name of GiANT MOSS. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,66,1961 ed.</i>)

### King Movement
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori attempt from the late 1850s to achieve unity of purpose and action under the guidance of a single leader, the Maori King (See [also Maori King movement](m-mar.md#maori-king-movement))

>  [Taranaki Herald] King Movement 1859. <i>(1971,Documents and Speeches,137,1859 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1859 (1971) Documents and Speeches 137]

>  <b>GRACE</b> The King movement is, however, perhaps less popular in Taupo than in some other districts. <i>(Mar 24, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Mar 24 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 76]

>  This latter tendency [to set up an independent internal organisation] is sometimes spoken of as `the Maori king movement', because one section of the Waikato tribe adopted that title for their old Chief, Te Whero Whero, ... <i>(May 12, 1860,Memoranda</i>) [NOTES: 1860 May 12 W. Martin Memorandum to Governor Browne in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 108j]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> The present King Movement has been initiated in the Waikato district. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,6</i>)

>  ... the most important feature is, the state of destitution to which a variety of causes have reduced most of the unsettled or King tribes in this neighbourhood. ... This state of things may be distinctly traced to the King movement itself, because there is little doubt that the constant excitement produced by the arrangements for this new government ... formed a complete bar to their settling down to any steady or useful work; while on the other hand, their constant feeling of antagonism to the British government, made the cultivation of their lands appear almost a waste of time, when a war might at any moment destroy all the results. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> `i fear Mr. --, that this king movement is unsettling the natives very much?' <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,10</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> if we had educated the natives in civilisation ... as British subjects ... nothing would have been heard of `land-leagues' and `king-movements'. <i>(1864,Maori King, The,39</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Apart from the Ngati Maniapotos, ... are other Waikato tribes ... who have been for years past the principal mover in what is termed the King Movement. <i>(1864,NZ Native Rebellion,4</i>)

>  The latest feature in Native affairs is the change which has come over Tawhiao, the so-called Maori King. ... He has ... quite recently ... accepted a pension from the present Native Minister. ... it is hoped that it will lead to the end of the `King movement'. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,275</i>)

>  <b>BRADBURY</b> <i>(1923,Settlement of the King Country</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Long before any King-Movement noise among them had roused your drowsy Colonial-Office Officials at Auckland, close observers of the Maori had seen that a `Whim,' a `Caprice,' had seized him ... <i>(1865,England's NZ War,37</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> in the eyes of supporters of the King movement, or Kingitanga, however, the mana of the two monarchs would be complementary. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,191</i>)

### King Native
 
 <i>n.</i> = Kingite

>  The King Native was intended now to act as a bar to colonization. He [Mr. Bell] could not say it was a blameable feature, it was a desire to save their remaining patrimony. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,2</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> ... there were tribes in that neighbourhood, as the Arawas for instance who hated the King natives worse than the whites. <i>(1864,Memoranda,127</i>)

>  if a distinct arrangement can be made in reference to the tract known as the King Country, by which the Government and the King natives will enter into a sort of partnership,.. that would be the best method by far. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>Aptenodytes longirostris</i> (King Penguin.)... The specimen of this noble penguin from which my description is taken was obtained on Stewart's island, where this bird is extremely rare. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,ii/306</i>)

### king shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a shag found only in the Marlborough Sounds

>  Six kinds of shag fish in Wellington harbour: the big black shag, or common cormorant, which is about the size of a goose, the little black shag, the pied shag, the spotted shag and, recently, a long shag was seen here. <i>(May 18, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

The only shag with pink feet in the Cook Strait and Marlborough Sounds area. Prominent caruncles at base of bill. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,118</i>)

### king tarakihi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>TARAKiHi (Jackass fish) Nemadactylus macropterus (Cheilodactylus macropterus)</i> ... in far northern waters, on rock pinnacles rising from deepwater on the continental slope, fishermen often catch fish they call king tarakihi that are old large tarakihi that may be over 60cm long and weigh ... 6kg. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,242,1984 ed.</i>)

### king tide
 
 <i>n.</i> [also used in Aust., Kiribati]

>  <b>SCOTT</b> [caption] The tide rules. <i>Tui</i> caught on the Otamatea, and Pahi Motel marooned in 1938 by a king tide and strong south-westerlies. <i>(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,53</i>)

>  A combination of big surf driven by the recent rough weather and a king tide saw large waves crashing into the coatline. <i>(Jan 4, 2006,Otago Daily Times,4</i>)

### king tree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> in Tom's early years at Puketiti, anything up to forty axemen were employed. They usually worked in gangs of four, and a gang would sometimes scarf for a full week before they let the `King' tree go. The procedure was to work `drives'. Depending on various factors, a certain defined area would be `scarfed', i.e. each tree in that area would have a scarf cut in it to the point where it was about to fall, and when the whole of that particular area was completed the `King' tree would be felled. This tree, specially selected for the purpose, would be so situated that, as it fell, it would take two or three of the scarfed trees with it. They in turn would do the same, and so on, and the whole area would fall almost as one. This is known as working a drive, and saves a tremendous amount of axe work. <i>(1972,Wake of The Axe,155</i>)

### kingfish
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> Seriola lalandi, a large school fish with a distinctive yellow tail, an excellent game fish; also called kahu, yellowtail (kingfish).

NUMBER OF FiSH RECORDED - 7 LOCALiTiES - Cape Maria van Diemen, Bean Rock, Ponui Passage, Portland island, Manukau Heads. <i>(1892,TrNZi,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> King-fish, n. in New Zealand a sea-fish, <i>Seriola lalandii</i> Maori, <i>Haku</i>), sometimes called the <i>Yellow Tail</i>; in Victoria, <i>Sciaena antarctica,</i> Castln. Called <i>Jew-fish</i> (q.v.) in New South Wales. Tenison Woods says the King-fish of Port Jackson must not be confounded with the King-fish of Victoria or the King-fish of Tasmania (<i>Thyrsites micropus</i>, McCoy). The Port Jackson king-fish belongs to a genus called `Yellow-tails' in Europe. This is <i>Seriola lalandii</i>, Cuv. and Val. <i>Seriola</i> belongs to the family <i>Carangidae</i>, or Horse-Mackerels.<i>Thyrsites</i> belongs to the family <i>Trichiuridae</i>. The `Barracouta' of Australasia is another species of <i>Thyrsites,</i> and the `Frost-fish' belongs to the same family. The <i>King-fish</i> of America is a different fish; the name is also applied to other fishes in Europe. <i>(1898,Austral English,248</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The other name, `Southern Kingfish' [for <i>Rexea solandri</i>] is more appropriate, although it suggests affinities with another family of fishes, which are associated in New Zealand with the name of `Kingfish', namely the Carangidae or Trevally (occasionally spelt Trevalli) family, which includes the `Yellowtail' or `Kingfish' (<i>Regificola grandis</i>). <i>ibid.</i> 89 The Yellowtail or Kingfish, as it is called in Auckland, has the upper parts of the body blue, and the underparts are whitish. <i>ibid.</i> 170 Yellowtail Regificola grandis Kingfish; Yellow-tail Kingfish; Northern Kingfish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>SPEiGHT</b> The only true mackerels recorded from Canterbury are the Pacific Bonito (<i>Sarda chiliensis</i>) and the large-scaled Tunny (<i>Gasterochisma melampus</i>) which may reach a length of 6 feet; but a related family includes the well-known Barracouta (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), the King Barracouta, <i>Jordanidia solandrii</i> (often known locally as Kingfish, but not to be confused with the North island Kingfish which is a form or Yellowtail) and the elongated, silvery-hued frost fish (<i>Lepidopus caudatus</i>) whose curious stranding habits can hardly yet be said to be satisfactorily explained. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 199]

 <i>n.</i> a brightly coloured, often crested bird with a strong beak which catches fish and insects; also called kotare.

Big cheeky birds, starlings and mynahs, whistled on the lawns, the little birds, the goldfinches and linnets and fantails flicked from bough to bough. A lovely kingfisher perched on the paddock fence preening his rich beauty, and a <i>tui</i> sang his three notes and laughed and sang them again. <i>(1920,Stories,233,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> There is much confusion in the common names applied to New Zealand fishes, and the application of the name `Hake' to several distinct species of our fishes, is a good example of that confusion. in Blenheim, Nelson, Picton and Wellington, a very different fish, scientifically known as <i>Rexea solandri</i>, and closely related to the Barracouta, with obviously no affinities to the Cod family, is called `Hake' or `Southern Kingfish'. The name Hake applied to this fish is entirely wrong, as it belongs to the Snake Mackerel family, the <i>Gempylidae</i>. The other name, `Southern Kingfish', is more appropriate, although it suggests affinities with another family of fishes, which are associated in New Zealand with the name of `Kingfish', namely the Carangidae or Trevally (occasionally spelt Trevalli) family, which includes the `Yellowtail' or `Kingfish' (<i>Regificola grandis</i>). <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,43</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The Alcedinidae family has only one species, the Kotaretare or kingfisher. it ... possesses the habits of the English bird, although its plumage is not so bright. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/24</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15</i>)

>  Halcyon vagans. King-fisher. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <i>[Halcyon vagans] </i>our common kingfisher, has an extended range ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/7</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  HALCYON VAGANS, Gray. Kotare. Kingfisher. One of our burrowing species. <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/52</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> HALCYON SANCTUS. Vig. and Horsf. Kingfisher. Kotare. <i>H. vagans, </i>Less. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,36</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kootare</i>, n. <i>halcyon vagans; kingfisher. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,65</i>) [NOTES: (3rd edn)]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Let but a grass-green<i> parrakeet</i> alight/ To pluck from some wild <i>coffee</i>-bush in sight,/ And nibble .../ The scarlet berries; let some <i>kingfisher</i> / Slip darting from the post .../ <i>ibid.</i> 1/305 Kingfisher ..... Alcedinidae; Halcyon vagans. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/154</i>)

>  i noticed the following:- Pigeon, tui, korimako, kaka, ruru, piwakawaka, toutouwai, kingfisher, duck and teal in the lakes, and pukeko in the swamp, and various sea birds. <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/419</i>) [NOTES: (Gold-Smith)]

>  HALCYON VAGANS, <i>Less</i>. - Kingfisher. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/124</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> <i>Kotare</i>, n. <i>halcyon vagans</i>; king-fisher. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,75</i>)

 <i>n.</i> fishing for kingfish

>  King-fishing in the Bay of islands isn't in it with the sport at Tauranga. <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

### kingi
 
 <i>n.</i> kingfish

>  "it was stink fishing in a pond," he laughed. "There are no snapper in the ponds here and the guy who arranged it told me there was little in there. Some went and they caught one goldfish. i flagged it 'cos there is nothing that beats going for the snapper or kingis in the sea." <i>(Oct 11, 1999,NZ Herald,B11</i>)

### Kingie
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig [ thought to be of a stock imported in late 1700s from Lieutenant-Governor King of Norfolk island.]

>  <b>SALE</b> So maybe our boar is a Kingie not a Captain Cooker. <i>(1987,Country Diary,64</i>)

### kingie
 
 <i>n.</i> kingfish [also kingey] (See [also kingi](kee-kiw.md#kingi))

>  i knew right from the first moment it was a kingie. it took about 150m of 25 pound.. nylon off my reel. <i>(Jan 3, 2002,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  The island received its name from the myriads of kingfish there ... To fish there requires ... a few hundred fathoms of spare line if you want to haul in the biggest old `kingeys' of them all <i>(Feb 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> Dad's got plently of lines. He doesn't care when we use them. Specially if we catch a 'couta or a kingie. <i>(1968,Sydney Bridge,148</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> `Won't it be terrific,' Martha said. `Mayor island's supposed to be fantastic for kingies and bronzies and black coral and all that.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,44</i>)

>  He hooked a big kingie and heaved it out of the water... Finally that kingie won. it skipped sideways over the water in a little dance. <i>(Dec 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  So prolific were the kingfish then that the fishermen could lean over the side and, using gaffs, haul the 'kingies' on board. <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No. 36/25</i>)

>  Only three Kingies were landed and this was a group of experienced and dedicated anglers, suggesting how hard the fishing was. <i>(Jul 8, 2005,NZ Herald,Super Sport, D19</i>)

### kinging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> The best example of a crowning ceremony is one that was called Kinging. in this ceremony the captive was patted on the head while he lay flat on the ground with other players astride him. if he managed to move during the ceremony, then the rhyme must begin again. `Kingy-seeny, one two three, You're the very man for me ...' <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/508</i>)

### Kingism
 
 <i>n.</i> kingitanga

>  With the natives the idea of kingism is of a very vague and indefinite character. <i>(1862,AJHR,E/7/9</i>) [NOTES: (Turton)]

>  <b>WiLLiAM MORGAN</b> Moketu has been taken, ... it was a hot bed of kingism - the pa where plans were concocted and bloody resolves made. <i>(Jul 1863,Journal,49,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> Taranaki, the hotbed of Kingism and rebellion <i>(1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Meade Ride Through Disturbed Dists in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 480]

>  <b>BULLER</b> Here [at Ngaruawahia], in the heart of `Kingism', they say in English our national anthem ... no communication with any white person was permitted, by the `King' natives. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,134</i>)

>  Rewi and the Ngatimaniopotos were the first to give Kingism an aspect of hostility to the British rule. <i>(May 24, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

### kingitanga
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori King movement of the 1860s [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>kingitanga</b> kingship] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 96.]

>  in 1856, the first Maori king, Potatau Te Wherowhero, raised the kingitanga flag over his capital at Ngaruawahia. <i>(Dec 12, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  So Te Kingitanga or the Maori King movement was set up to prevent the loss of further land, as well as to unify Maori interests and resolve disputes, and King Tawhiao decreed that no land south of the Mangatawhiri stream was to be sold. <i>(Feb 2002,NZ Memories,No. 34/5</i>)

>  Ta [sic] Kerei publicly acknowledged his adhesion to the Kingitanga. <i>(1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/540,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H. Halse)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> But the Kingitanga was the stumbling-block. Grey, for all his kindly feelings towards his native friends, would have nothing to do with an alien flag, ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/233</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Kingitanga - this word refers to the King movement and all it stands for. Belief in the kingitanga rests strongly with the Tainui people and the Waikato Maniopoto, and is being increasingly accepted as a positive force for all Maoridom by other tribes and regions. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,20</i>)

>  <b>iHiMAERA</b> he wanted him to be an emissary between her people and the Kingitanga movement. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,212</i>)

>  "He was a great man for Kingitanga" <i>(Dec 14, 2005,NZ Herald,A15</i>)

### Kingite
 
 <i>n.</i> a supporter of the Maori King and his faction (See [cf. Queenite](que-quo.md#queenite))

>  Every Kingite, and just about every neutral, and, on the quiet, many Queenites were on his [sc. Te Kooti's] side. <i>(1976,Tall & Short Stories,32</i>) [NOTES: 2nd ed.]

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> The other party, the Kingites, anxious for the entire independence of their country. [refce to Maori Wars] <i>(1857,Journal and Letters (Walker),2/290</i>)

>  i believe he has wriiten to the Kingites in Waikato <i>(Jun 21, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/471</i>) [NOTES: (R. Parris)]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> The great body of the Waikatos returned home, and the Kingites proceeded to Rangiaohia to complete the installation of their sovereign ... <i>ibid.</i> 60 Second - The land Question. To prevent further alienation of Native lands is the great object of the league, and on this point the kingites carry with them the sympathies of the majority. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,14</i>)

>  ... a certain native of the Kingite party .. .[W. King of Umuroa, the first or second chief of the tribe] is said to be highly incensed, and to have already commenced a war pah. The bitterness of his feeling is partly due to the exaction of toll, at the gate erected by the Kingites, on all men, live stock, and carts, which pass it. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> Both Kingites and Queenites were present, and all agreed to pray for peace. May God answer our unworthy petitions. <i>(Oct 10, 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Oct 10 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928)112]

>  Near Wellington, Wi Tako, an influential chief, known as a strong Kingite, ... , gave in his adhesion to the queen ... <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,168</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLL</b> He said that he was a King Maori. ... another old man came to us ... he was also a Kingite and lived in the same village. <i>(Jul 21, 1864,Journal,269</i>)

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> Wherever we go now as Missionaries, whether amongst the Friendlies or Kingites, ... <i>(Nov 28, 1868,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Nov 28 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 204]

>  <b>FEATON</b> The Kingites, as the Waikatos styled themselves, were so called on account of their having set up for themselves a King... 6 (Griffiths Collection) The Kingites, as the Waikatos styled themselves, were so called on account of their having set up for themselves a king. Their reigning potentate was Tawhiao, son of their first king, old Potatau, who died a short time before this period. The idea of having a king, or the king movement, was started by Wiremu Thompson, one of the leading Waikato chiefs, who was afterwards called `the King-maker'. <i>ibid.</i> 9 The Kingites, as the Waikatos styled themselves, were so called on account of their having set up for themselves a king. <i>(1879,Waikato War,6</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> This Te Kooti ... maintained a guerilla resistance ... for many years after our `Kingite' neighbours had accepted British authority. <i>(1908,My Simple Life in NZ,84</i>)

>  He typified the irreconcilable element among the Kingites. <i>(Dec 1, 1919,Quick March,35</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The following is a statement by a Kingite survivor of the wars:- <i>ibid.</i> 1/233 The new institutions were first introduced in the Ngapuhi country ... but it was too late to entice the Kingites into the Government fold with such devices. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/157</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> One of the Waikato Kingite Flags at Waahi. <i>(1930,Maori, The,85</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Sometimes the farthest-out farmers went across the Aukati line to trade with Maoris ... and they were on friendly terms with many of the Kingites. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,158</i>)

>  <b>BABBAGE</b> Hauhauism was the result of several different features, which became welded in 1863 in the form of a fanatical religion. ... The progess of Hauhauism corresponded largely with the progess of the Maori King Movement; and with the defeat of the Kingite tribes the religion became a ... negligible force. <i>(1937,Hauhauism,9</i>)

>  <b>WiLY & MAUNSELL</b> The Kingites resolved to thoroughly investigate the matter, and for that purpose a large party of them set out for Patumahoe. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,130</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... he turned his energies to the conciliation of the Kingites ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,97</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> The Kingites were not merely copying the settlers' political organization. As with most similar movements, there was a dark and primitive strain in Maori nationalism. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,112</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> How did they regard the Queen's Maori soldiers? They had a name for them - Kupapa, a derisive term used by Kingite and Hau Hau to identify those they believe "to be crawling on their bellies to the pakeha. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,44</i>)

>  When. the "Free Lance called to see the young Kingite leader.. he found him in a very different garb. <i>(Feb 27, 1935,NZ Free Lance,17</i>)

>  This was a Pa which had sheltered some 3,000 Kingite Maoris. <i>(1965,They Came by Ship,166</i>)

>  Let anyone go to Hikurangi and try to argue with some intelligent Kingite. <i>(Mar 15, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  it was the Kingites who were now the beggars, and got supplies from the Government. The Queenites did not. <i>(May 17, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Te Waka.. paddled three miles up the river.. where the Kingite runanga, a Council of the district chiefs, was sitting. <i>(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,18</i>)

>  Amongst the party was Puru or Neri, the kingite "surveyor-general," who was lately expelled from Government House. <i>(Jun 1863,Southern Monthly Magazine,1(5) : 246</i>)

>  You have seen that the Kingites hopped about, and asked to have the Waikato back. <i>(May 15, 1873,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MEADE</b> Amongst the whole of the `Queenites' (in contradistinction to the `Kingite' followers of the so-styled Maori king) Poihipi is probably the most influential chief. <i>(1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Meade Ride Through Disturbed Dists in 1959 Taylor Early Travs 427]

>  (Dr. Maui Pomare) Progress has not been so rapid among the Waikatos, and in the King Country ... The Kingite influence has retarded progress there. <i>(Feb 14, 1907,Auckland Star,5</i>) [NOTES: 1907 Auckland Star 14 Feb, 5 in 1971 Speeches and Documents 175]

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... we used to see the chiefs whose names were writ large in the story of the Kingite struggle <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,2</i>)

>  Taite was too confident of the influence of the Kingite support .. <i>(Jan 6, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/207</i>) [NOTES: 1936 Jan 6 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/207 (Buck)]

>  <b>WiLY & MAUNSELL</b> Like most intelligent men who had come closely in contact with the natives, he knew that a trial of force was the inevitable corollary of the Kingite movement, ... <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,22</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> But they enforced the `Aukati' order of the Kingite chiefs against the <i>pakeha. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 123 Thence they travelled to the main Kingite village, Tokangamutu, close to where ... Te Kuiti stands. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,113</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> ... two Kingite chiefs visited Vienna. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> To the old-time rebel or kingite Ngati Raukawa they are petrified figures ... <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,27</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> However, with the military road pointing right at the doorstep of Kingite territory, it is not surprising that the Maoris viewed it with misgivings . <i>(1963,Journal of William Morgan,27</i>)

>  Te Rauparaha retaliated when the people east and west of the Waikato cared not to join his kingite movement by a campaign of slaughter in these areas. <i>(Sep 2, 1965,Dominion,2</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK3/648</b> in 1907 a great kingite convention assembled at Waahi. There ... the `King' movement was revitalised and Mahuta withdrew from politics. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ling

### kingmaker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  NZ First is consistently polling as the third biggest party, which could enable Mr Peters to play kingmaker and determine whether National or Labour leads the Government after the election. <i>(Jul 6, 2005,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

>  Peters obviously relishes his role as kingmaker (or, as Helen Clark supporters call it, "queenmaker"). <i>(1996,North & South,76</i>)

>  The NZ First leader could easily return to centre-stage as queenmaker after the November elections. ibid. 8 [Hdg] Queenmaker in waiting... Last time he was the kingmaker. This time he stands the chance of being a queenmaker. Winston Peters is back centre-stage... This November he could conceivably be in a position to be queenmaker to either Helen Clark or Jenny Shipley. <i>(Aug 23, 1999,Dominion Post,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) Wiremu Tamihana (William Thompson)

>  <b>FEATON</b> The idea of having a king, or the king movement, was started by Wiremu Thompson one of the leading Waikato chiefs, who was afterwards called `The King-maker'. <i>(1879,Waikato War,6</i>)

>  <i>(1894,Speeches & Documents,1971 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Speeches and Documents 165 quoting an Evening Post May 30 1894 translation from Maori Son of the King-maker, William Thompson.]

>  <b>GRACE</b> ... the national movement under Wiremu Tamihana, the King-maker, and the wave of religious enthusiasm called [149] `Hau-hauism' which followed it. <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,148</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The kingmaker's appeals to the <i>pakeha </i>Administration read pathetically. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/154</i>)

>  <b>WiLEY & MANSELL</b> At Te Akau, between Putataka and Raglan, lived Wiremu Tamehana (William Thompson), who must not be confused with the much greater personage ... who, a few years later, came to be known as `The Kingmaker.' <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,46</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Finally, the Totara [Otaki] people have never been very favourably inclined towards the concept or the fact of the Maori king. it is true that two of the early king-makers were associated with local Maoris so that there are connections between Totara and Waikato genealogies. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,290</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> The man who did most to mould it [the kotahitanga movement] was a remarkable chief of the Ngatihaua tribe, Wiremu Tamihana, who earned the name of the `king-maker'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,112</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Kevin [a teacher] was rambling on about a television quiz he had seen the previous evening and when a contestant was asked who was the Kingmaker he had promptly replied Warwick. Kevin held that any patriotic New Zealander should have said Wiremu Tamihana. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,108</i>)

>  <b>BELiCH</b> The contemporary misnomers `moderates' and `extremists' have stuck to the two major parties [of the King Movement], represented by Wiremu Tamehana (`The Kingmaker') and Rewi Maniapoto respectively. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,76</i>)

>  The defection of William Thompson, the King-maker, was the most serious blow that the Maori King could have received. <i>(May 24, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a lion

>  The lion at Wellington Zoo, known as King Dick, is suffering from paralysis of the legs. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,1</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [Maori kingship](m-mar.md#maori-kingship))

### Kingston Flyer
 
 <i>n.</i> a tourist train (no longer operating 2009)

>  They came.. on a sizzling iron plate, sprinkled with water before delivery, hissing and puffing out more steam than the Kingston Flyer. <i>(Jan 13, 2001,Dominion,21</i>)

>  <b>LORETZ</b> Three bus loads of tourists inspected the Kingston Flyer - the original steam train that used to run from Gore. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,94</i>)

>  On this day in 1971 - just two months after the last scheduled steam train service in the country - the vintage steam train the Kingston Flyer began a tourist run from Lake Wakatipu to Lumsden. After several owners and a changed run - it now plies between Kingston and Fairlight [sic] three times a day from September until mid-May - the Flyer is still charming tourists... The operators cannot guess how many people have travelled on the Kingston Flyer since 1971, but say it must run into "the hundreds of thousands". <i>(Dec 21, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  The two old locos belonged to Tranz Rail, but the track on which it ran was run by Kingston Flyer Ltd. <i>(Feb 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  <b>CHURCHMAN & HURST</b> in May 1971.. the Minister of Railways announced that two steam locomotives.. would be retained to work a summer tourist service on the Lumsden-Kingston route... The service was called the 'Kingston Flyer', after a popular name for the Gore-Kingston trains of 50 or more years earlier. <i>(1990,Railways of NZ,208</i>)

>  Unfortunately, we don't get time to linger because we've a train to catch - the Kingston Flyer. Based on the southern shores of Lake Wakatipu, the original flyer operated till the 1950s between Kingston and Gore, but today is restricted to a 14-kilometre stretch of track between Kingston and Fairlight. <i>(Jun 11, 2002,Dominion,15</i>)

### Kingy-seeny
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game [1953 15 M A21 Thames DHS 20 KINGISEEN[Y] -] (See [also King Seenie](kee-kiw.md#king-seenie))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Others wanted [to play] `Prisoners' Base' or `Chivvy', `Brewer', "Egg-in-the hat', `Duck-stone', `Red Rover', or `Kingy-seeny'. The boys did not know where this last game came from. They did not know its proper spelling or pronunciation. `Kingy-seeny' it was to them and they did not care whether that had a meaning or not. This rough and tumble game was the most popular of all. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,84</i>) [NOTES: 1953 15 M A21 Thames DHS 20 KINGISEEN[Y] -]

(c) Other common names: Kini; Pioke; Lemons; Lemonfish; Dogfish; Rig; Silver Strip (fillets); Sweet William; Flake. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,44</i>)

>  [species name] rig .. [scientific name] <i>Mustelus lenticulatus </i>.. [other name(s)] spotted dogfish, gummy shark, kini, pioke, smooth hound, deepsea bream, lemonfish, spotted gummy shark, sweet william, dogi <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> curiosity (?from kinky).

### kino
 
 <i>v.</i> at a wedding ceremony, to answer no, reject

[fn The native expression of aversion: literally, <i>bad.</i>] <i>(Feb 7, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Feb 7 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part iii (Church in the Colonies Viii) 30]



 
 <i>n.</i> bad

### kiokio
 
 <i>n.</i> a common hard fern [Maori]

>  <b>COWAN</b> A large body of Hauhaus was in concealment behind a thick bank of the drooping <i>kiokio</i> ferns <i>(Lomaria procera). </i> <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/355</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>B[lechnum] capense</i> ... Long Hard-fern. KiO KiO. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,13</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Much the most abundant species [of <i>Blechnum</i>] is <i>B.procerum</i> known as the COMMON HARDFERN or KiO-KiO. <i>(1969,Flora NZ,105,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kiokio [s] Blechnum capense</i> (L.) Schlecht. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

>  Blechnaceae <i>B[lechnum] capense</i> kio kio <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>) [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Blechnum</i> sp. `black spot' KiOKiO ... Palm-leaf fern ... Fronds arching out from a creeping rhizome, often a metre or even 2 m long ... Frond scales brown with prominent black spot in the centre of the wide base. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,460</i>)

>  Ferns line the way ahead; an easy one to identify is the Kiokio, or palm leaf fern. Tell it apart by the conspicuous reddish-pink tips to the fronds. <i>(Dec 31, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Although kiokio, <i>Blechnum novae-zealandiae</i>, is one of our commonest ferns even becoming a weed at times. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,page 68</i>)

>  Kiokio (<i>Blechnum novae-zealandiae</i>) is probably one of the commonest ferns in New Zealand, and because of its habit of growing abundantly on roadside cuttings, it should be familiar even to those who never step out of their cars for a closer look.<i>ibid.</i> 77 More palatable are the young fiddleheads of many species, especially the hen and chickens fern, kiokio (<i>Blechnum novae-zealandiae</i>), gully fern (<i>Pneumatopteris pennigera</i>, shining spleenwort (<i>Asplenium oblongifolium</i>) and the shield ferns (<i>Polystichum richardii</i> and <i>P/vestitum</i>). <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/72</i>)

>  ...don't overlook ground-cover ferns like shining spleenwort, <i>Asplenium oblongifolium</i>, or kiokio, <i>Blechnum capense</i>,for intriguing texture under tall trees. <i>(Oct 31, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),47</i>)

### kiokio fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These [plants] include coastal flax.. and, in shady spots, the fans of spreading kiokio ferns (Blechnum). <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,9</i>)

### kiore
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori or native rat so-called, introduced to NZ by the early Polynesian migrants

>  New Zealand possesses only two distinct proven genera (of mammals]; the bat (Pekapeka, two species) and a small indigenous rat (Kiore). <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),161</i>)

>  Kiore are predators... Eradication of kiore from Hauturu will eliminate important history and raises other legal and cultural issues. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/3</i>)

>  <b>COLLiNS</b> E-kere[acute], <i>A rat. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>NiCHOLAS</b> A rat Keo[acute]uree <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/331</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> K|i |ore; A mouse, rat &c. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,164</i>)

>  rat - kior|ee [kiore] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 110]

>  <b>WALTON</b> The <i>Kiore</i>, or rat, has long been an inhabitant of New Zealand, into which he was transported by European vessels. <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>STRANGE</b> [The Norway rat] destroys the <i>kiore</i>), or native rat. <i>(Mar 7, 1849,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1849 March 7 Strange's Journal (Naturalist on `Acheron") (in 1851 Canterbury Papers 79)]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the Native Rat (Kiore[e-acute]) not larger than the English Water-Rat, is the only indigenous four-footed creature found in New Zealand. ... the Kiore[e-acute] is now so scarce <i>(1865,England's NZ War,38fn</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The small frugivorous rat (<i>kiore</i>) was an article of food highly esteemed. ... When cooked they were like large juicy sausages. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,106</i>)

>  The native rat, which the Maoris named Kiore, was very different to the Norway rat, and strictly herbivorous. <i>(1906,History of North Otago,4,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.ROBERTS in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 4]

>  <b>SHAND</b> Native Rats, called <i>Kiore</i>, were common to the [Chatham] island: but it is believed they were not eaten by the Morioris ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,6</i>)

>  <i>kiore</i>: native rat. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <i>kiore n</i> -s [Maori] : the native rat (<i>Rattus exulan</i>) of New Zealand now wholly or nearly replaced by the introduced Norwegian and black rats <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1245</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> in camp at White island in the Bay of Plenty, ... we watched kiore scampering around our feet at meal times, ... <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,168</i>)

>  On Great Barrier island, bush rats kiore [sic] and feral cats threaten the small colony [of Cook's petrel]. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,81</i>)

>  ... getting rid of kiore or Polynesian rat from koropuki [e]. Lizards fairly common. Lizard called moko [????] different from Whittaker skink. We put some of these moko skinks in the same enclosure. <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1987 Sep 26 National Programme introduction of Whittaker skinks to Mirokuhi[????] island]

>  <b>MiKAERE</b> in those days the haumata (snow grass) grew over one and half metres high, and abounded with ... succulent kiore (native rats). <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,75</i>)

>  The Pacific rat or kiore <i>Rattus exulans</i> has become the subject of some controversy in New Zealand... in recent years, however, evidence has come to light indicating the supposedly innocent kiore is a predator of lizards, frogs, large invertebrates and some birds' eggs... in protein-depleted New Zealand, kiore was an important seasonal food source for the Maori. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,11</i>)

>  These were kiore, descendants of the original rat Maori voyagers brought as a portable food supply 800 years ago. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,No. 13/83</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> i was much astonished to see a rat at that altitude... Had he been a kiore, or Maori rat, one could have given him a pathetic biography at once. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,159</i>)

>  Undoubtedly, contributing to those early extinctions was the kiore, the Polynesian rat, which came here with the Maori. <i>(Aug 6, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  The kiore trail does not support the romantic notion of the Chatham islands being settled by a pre-Maori community from parts unknown. The genetic trail is short - and it leads back to the Marlborough Sounds kiore. <i>(Sep 21, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  The elimination of Pacific rats or kiore from a group of islands off Whangarei has resulted in a swift increase in the population of tuatara. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,10</i>)

>  THE KiORE, or Pacific rat, arrived in canoes with early Maori. .. Kiore roamed well-trod paths at dusk and through the night. .. Although still widespread throughout the pacific - and the thrid-most populous rodent in the workld - kiore disappeared from most of new Zealand by the 1890's and remain only in Fiordland and on numerous offshore islands. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,24</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The decline of ground-dwelling birds had begun with the introduction of the kiore, the Polynesian rat, which accomapnied the early Maori. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,47</i>)

>  The initiative follows DoC's successful eradication of kiore - the indigenous rat - from Little Barrier island, which has allowed tuatara, giant weta, skinks and cooks petrel to thrive again. <i>(Jun 12, 2006,NZ Herald,A3</i>)

>  Along with the kuri, or Maori dog... the kiore was part of the first wave of introductions to this country. <i>(Mar 28, 1992,Evening Post,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> The <i>kiore maori</i>, or native rat, and the guana, were once favourite dishes, but they have met with the same fate; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/45</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The smaller kind is supposed to have been introduced first, and is called <i>kiore maori</i>, or <i>native rat. </i> <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,167</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> in New Zealand there exists a frugivorous native rat, called `kiore maori' by the aborigines, which they distinguish from the European species, ... called `kiore pakeha,' or the stranger rat <i>(1866,Polynesia,55</i>)

>  i believe this is the true Maori Rat - the <i>Mus maorium</i> of Professor Hutton - the Kiore Maori - the rat with whose presence we now are so largely favoured. <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/206</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

### kip
 
 <i>n.</i> a small, flat piece of wood used for tossing pennies in two-up (See [cf. bat](b-bla.md#bat))

>  Much to `Truth's' deep regret, for it detests having to give the show away, lately, at Miramar, when some road-making or mountain-razing work has been going on, some of them have engaged in the simple yet sinful process of tossing two bright copper pennies, with a bit of wood, popularly known as `the bat' or `the kip' and, therefore, in the eye of the law are hardened criminals. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

>  One James Holt, arrested for drunkenness down at Westport, had on him when arrested two double-headed pennies and three kips, one genuine and two that are known as `swindling kips,' with a hole at the palm into which the good and the bad pennies can,... be worked in a way to easily deceive the unwary. <i>(Jun 8, 1912,NZ Truth,363/1/6</i>)

>  Anybody who has once played the game could smell a `kip' .... [at] 123 yards. <i>(Mar 1912,Magpie, The</i>)

>  it kinder gives a bloke the bloomin' pip to find his efforts all knocked 'umpty dumpty by other blokes what allers rush the kip. <i>(Feb 8, 1913,NZ Bulletin,16</i>)

>  ... very soon he got the `kip' <i>(May 16, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> One man takes the kip and places two pennies on it ... He hands [his bet] to the ring-keeper. .. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,162</i>)

>  <i>kip n.</i> wooden bat used for tossing ? in game of two-up. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>kip n.</i> wooden bat used for tossing pennies in game of two-up. <i>(q.v.) </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> in the centre stood the spinner with his kip and two pennies. <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,75</i>)

>  <i>kip n. NZ & Austral</i>. small piece of wood from which coins are spun in the game of two-up. [orig. unkn.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,413</i>) [NOTES: [Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 620, KiP 4]]

>  Seven packs of cards, six two-up kips, a crown and anchor board, and a number of double-headed pennies were exhibited in the Police Court at Rotorua. <i>(Feb 6, 1934,Feilding Star,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  He, however, aspired to fame a few months back when, at Christchurch, he, with others of a holy unctuous order, `rushed the kip,' as it were, with Sir Joseph Ward, and asked him to suppress gross and licentious books and literature, and invited, indirectly, of course, Sir Joseph Ward to do something, which even sir Joseph Ward, all-powerful as he is, or was at the moment, hesitated to consent to do, viz. suppress of the Liberty of the press. Naturally Sir Joseph Ward could not see his way clear to play with fire, and scientifically side-stepped the awkward proposition <i>(Jul 31, 1909,NZ Truth,214/6/5</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> But the most interesting of all the team games was a small group game variously known as <i>Cunning Joe</i> (K-6), Cunny, Conjure, Conjo, Cunny Joe, and Cronje. in this game the cat [a thin 6-inch stick with pointed ends] was sometimes called the Cunning Joe stick (Waikouaiti, Otago, 1900) or the kip (Dunedin, 1895). it was a game for four players. Two rings were marked on the ground a short distance from each other with a square drawn in the middle of each. A boy armed with a short stick stood at each ring, a bowler behind him. One of the bowlers tossed a short piece of wood at the ring opposite. The striker hit it as hard as he could, then both boys ran as in cricket. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,159</i>)

### kipper
 
 <i>n.</i> a two-up player who holds the kip; a spinner.

>  When the `two-up' school is raided it is a mix-up between "copper' and `kipper' <i>(May 12, 1906,NZ Truth,47/1/4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand kipper

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MULLET, YELLOW-EYED </i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Aldrichetta forsteri</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Sea Mullet; New Zealand Kipper (smoked); Rollmops (pickled). (c) Other common names: Herring (in error and best dropped); Common Mullet; Sprat (in error). (d) Principal localities where taken: Tauranga, kaipara, lake Ellesmere. ... The name `Herring' has been applied widely and for a long time to the New Zealand Yelloweyed Mullet which is not a Clupeid and therefore only very distantly related to the true Herring of European waters. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,39</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] kipper 1641 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an English serviceman

>  <b>PUGH-WiLLiAMS</b> An Englishman as opposed to a `Kiwi' [New Zealander]. i have heard this term mainly in the New Zealand Navy. The NZ Division [? Naval] was formed in 1921 and it may have been used from this year <i>(May 1951,Letter</i>)

### Kipperland
 
 <i>n.</i> England, the UK

 <i>n.</i> kip

### kiss
 
 <i>n.</i> (marbles term) [<i>Halocarpus kirkii</i>, aka Kirk's pine, Manoao]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Action terms related to the movements of the marbles are, Throughs and No Throughs, Tracks and Clears, Stops and Rolls, Lay-Up, Manyies, Kiss, Ons, Gees, Haystacks, Stays, Spiders Can't Cross a River etc. Action Terms related to taking an opponent's marbles are Fires, Double-Hits and Forces. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### kiss in the ring
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>
