### kit
 
 <i>n.</i> a basket woven from flax [Maori kete a bag or basket]; a flat Maori basket made of woven strips of flax; Maori kit, (see 1946 Andersen below). [derivation of Maori kete from English dial. kit would be counter-indicated: (a) NZ recorded kit uses antedate those of English dialect (b) kit would more likely become Maori kiti or kite (c) there is evidence of a development kete to NZ Eng. kit through kete, kite, ket etc. eg., in the following "transitional' forms:]

>  <b>BAKER</b> On the low-lying hills women with bare feet had been hoeing their potato beds, with bright-hued handkerchiefs tied over their dark hair, while their lords and masters, as was customary, squatted about smoking, or engaged themselves making 'kits" from green flax. <i>(1884,Daughter of King,177</i>)

>  <b>MEURANT</b> (Waikato) ... bought one Kete corn one Kete potatoes. 1845 op. cit 10 ... 34 Kites of potatoes [? kitiz] <i>(1845,Diary,29</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> 50 kites of potatoes. <i>(1845,Journal,3/47</i>)

>  <i>kete</i>: flat basket made of strips of flax: a basket of like shape but made of prepared flax dyed with cross-pattern in black called Maori-kit: the first very commonly used for potatoes or kumera and shell-fish in earlier days of settlement; the latter commonly used in later days up till now. in early use, the usual reference is to kits of kumara potatoes, fish, or shellfish; in 20 century use, to a similar basket used for shopping, etc. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: J.C. Andersen (<i>Maori Words</i>]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... <i>kit</i>: shopping bag; from Maori <i>kete</i> (flax basket) <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <i>kit n.</i> NZ a string bag for shopping. [< Maori <i>kete</i>] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,620</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 413; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 273]]

>  Pigs and kits of potatoes were fast landed from their canoes. <i>(Mar 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  in places there were tufts of the pingao grass, whose tough leathery leaves were formerly used by the Maoris for making girdles, and are still used for the manufacture of kits. <i>(Aug 13, 1908,Auckland Weekly News,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. Lyon <i>Faring South</i> for another term]

>  <i>Kit</i>. - A bundle of (usually flax) sticks with a coat or blanket over them, strapped on the pommel of a saddle to help a rider to stick to a bucking horse. (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  in prohibition districts eight bottles of tanglefoot are known as a `kit', and two kits are described as a drunk. <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9-1-4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> [The net] is made of Koraddy [korari] or Flax undrest split green and worked up. They make Baskets or Kits as we call them for Potatoes and a Chief or Head of a Familly has a clean basket made of Koraddy for his plate of potatoes ... every meal. <i>ibid..</i> 75 We took two or three pieces of Pork with us and some seven or eight Kits or Baskets [fn The phrase `or Baskets' is inserted in the margin.] of Potatoes, .... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),46,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> The natives readily consented to pay my passage, accordingly they brought 178 kits of potatoes. <i>(1841,Journal,1/56</i>)

>  <b>BRiGHT</b> ...[potato] baskets, which they call kits. <i>(1841,Handbook for Emigrants,46</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> ..quickly deposited in a `kit' or native basket. <i>(1842,Journal,iV/7</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> <i>Kits</i> of Potatoes a shilling each. <i>(1842,Letters,3/487</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... we noticed an open penknife stuck in the uppermost kit which seemed to be a bad sign, ... <i>(Jan 20, 1845,Journal,3/66</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> You have to pay for each small kit of potatoes. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,2/358</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ...kit of potatoes. <i>(1847,Great Journey,48</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Arrived at the Kaika, Wahinei searched the wata and among a quantity of putrid fish ... found 2 kits of potatoes of which we made a hearty meal. <i>(Oct 24, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Oct 24 Mantell Diary in 1947 Stephenson Maori and Pakeha 128]

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> There are 1,200 kits of <i>kumara</i>, large baskets of <i>taro, papa</i> or bark cases of birds cooked ..., including <i>tui ... </i> <i>(Jul 16, 1849,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1849 July 16 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 48]

>  <b>McKiLLOP</b> ... an old woman coming down from the pah with a kit, or small grass bag, of potatoes, ... <i>(1849,Reminiscences,105</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The woman, having the potatoes in a flax-basket or `kit', with two handles. <i>(1850,Savage Life,1/237</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The woman, having the potatoes in a flax-basket or `kit', with two handles. <i>(1850,Savage Life,1/237</i>)

>  i find it quite impossible to get any kits for Mrs Blundell. The natives here are too profitably occupied in agriculture to give any time to the making of kits. <i>(Aug 16, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/155,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>SWAiNSON</b> ... 50 baskets of Patiki, 20 baskets of fresh eels ... and 8000 kits of potatoes and kumeras. <i>(1859,New Zealand (various authors/titles),24</i>)

>  (The Maori's Choice) (A kit of eels he carried) <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,85</i>) [NOTES: 1860 July in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 85]

>  <b>MOSER</b> Things ... were got ready at last, and served up in baskets made of flax, known as kits, calabashes, pots and kettles; and a roar of `kai-kai! haeremai', created a sudden sensation amongst the outsiders. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,34</i>)

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> They had followed us with the little pig and a small kit of potatoes. <i>(Oct 30, 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Oct 30 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 129]

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> (Griffiths Collection) Maori picking up something and putting it into a kit (basket) ... <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,105</i>) [NOTES: 1864 diary in 1888 Preshaw Banking 105]

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> We are now in the midst of the Peach and Melon Season. Maoris bring in Kits of them very often <i>(Feb 24, 1865,Journal</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The females were all now getting the kapura maoris ready for a grand feed when ... there arrived ... some hundreds of kits of potatoes; ... <i>(Mar 25, 1865,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Mar 25 S.A.Levy in Williams East Coast Historical Records 91]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Kit, n.</i> a flexible Maori basket; not the English <i>kit</i> used by soldiers, but the Maori word <i>kete</i>, a basket. <i>(1898,Austral English,249</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>"Kete </i>(Maori), pa-kete (Anglo-Maori), basket, kit (Eng.).' <i>(1855,Te ika a Maui,199</i>)

>  <b>FiTTON</b> `The natives generally bring their produce to market in neatly made baskets, plaited from flax and known by the name of `Maori kits.'' <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),68</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> `The kit is a large plaited green-flax basket.' <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/180</i>)

>  <b>AN OLD COLONiST</b> `Potatoes were procurable from the Maoris in flax kits, at from one to five shillings the kit'. <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,31</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> `They might have said, as an old Maori woman long afterwards said to me, `Mother, my heart is like an old kete (i.e. a coarsely woven basket). The words go in, but they fall through.'' <i>(1884,Our Maoris,44</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> Then we go into the parlour [of the Maori's whare] - photos - a chiming clock - mats - kits - red table cloth - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,53,(1978 Gordon) ed.</i>)

>  <b>REiSCHEK</b> ... and a piece of uncooked meat in a flax-basket <i>(kit)</i> to take with him. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,204,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> The fat brown man leers broadly ... `How you like, eh?' asks Fatty-face, swinging the full kit forward for Uncle Ted to see. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,40,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> [Maori child speaks] `When i comes to this word `basket' in my book i never says `basket"' `Oh?' `i allays says `kit'.' `Why?' `'Cause `basket' it's too much like swearing so i says `kit' <i>(1958,Spinster,41</i>)

>  On the back of a truck, guarding a kit of soft-drinks between her knees... <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,56</i>) [NOTES: 1961 Hilliard in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories 56]

>  <b>SWAiNSON</b> Peaches ... are very abundant and cheap: during the present summer upwards of 1,200 kits were brought into Auckland by canoes alone. <i>(1853,Auckland,71</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... the [native] oven is a simple contrivance whereby a kit of kumeras or an entire Maori can be cooked with equal convenience - and well cooked too! <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/6/107</i>)

>  On this were piled about half a dozen kits (flax baskets) of kumara. <i>(1903,TrNZi,35/22</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [first use here] i found a kit of apples most uncomfortable to carry ... <i>(Feb 25, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,216,1950 ed.</i>)

>  (Wellington) The accused offered to carry her kit. <i>(Jan 30, 1875,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Our chum .... came ... staggering under the weight of a great kit full of crocks and such-like. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/139</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A few examples will suffice. Maori papa, a father. .. English papa, father. ... kuri, a dog. .. cur, a dog. ... kete, a basket. .. kit (Gr.) kiste. <i>(1885,Aryan Maori,96</i>) [NOTES: [cf KETE Tregear is speaking of his theory that Maori language was originally `Aryan'. He has given examples of transliterated borrowings from English (honi = honey, etc.) and now alludes `only to the vocabulary used before our [sc English] immigration.] -]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We took with us kits - New Zealand bags made of flax. <i>(1886,Glimpses of Maori Land,85</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> `There, mother, let me take him!' said the young woman anxiously, quietly. She tossed the kit away. <i>(1922,Journal,305,1954 Murray ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> `There, mother, let me take him!' said the young woman anxiously, quietly. She tossed the kit away. <i>(1922,Journal,305,1954 Murry ed.</i>)

>  <i> kit n</i>. a large, plaited basket made of green flax. [Maori kete, a basket] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  The arts and crafts section comprised tukutuku, ... plaiting (rourou [fn A small basket used for cooked food.], rough kits and floor mats), taniko work and making poi balls. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/212</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> We have also put into wide use the term kit for a shopping basket. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,55</i>)

>  The nightmarish business of struggling on and off vehicles with a folding push-chair, a heavy baby, a kit of napkins, ... <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,18</i>)

>  <i>kit</i> A Maori basket made of `flax' (see <i>flax</i>); the Maori name is "kete' (ke|-te). [ 14 edn: A Maori basket made of `flax'.] <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),231</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> (New Zealand) (Australia, a Dillybag) A bag [=] for carrying small article; for instance, a shopping bag. .in 4;`Give it to me. i can fit an extra parcel into my kit.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],648,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <i>kit, kite n.</i> flexible Maori basket. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),618</i>)

>  <i>Kit</i> `basket' is in New [360] Zealand use, where its frequency seems to be due to the influence of Maori <i>kete</i> with the same meaning. <i>(1968,Anglia,86/359-360</i>) [NOTES: G.W.Turner review Pitcairnese Language]

>  <i>kit n.</i> plaited basket of N.Z. flax [Maori<i> kete</i>, basket]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),11</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> kit: hand bag or basket made of flax <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <b>BiDWiLL</b> i then gave Moning-'aw a kettle of hashed pig's head and some cold potatoes to carry, and taking a kit in my own hand, set off in the direction of the water-course in order to botanise. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,79,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASKEW</b> The kits are made of native flax. They have two handles or loops to lift them by. They are the same in shape as those used by the carpenters, and some are as well made. <i>(1857,Voyage to Australia & NZ,347</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> `Shut up the whole kit of you.' Every one became mournfully silent ... <i>(1935,Little Country, The,90,1937 ed.</i>)

### kit-bag
 
 <i>n.</i> [Probably from Maori "kete"]

>  The dear old soul had a kit-bag of apples. <i>(Nov 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,73</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> Plump Maori ladies.. sold us kit-bags made of flax. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,30</i>)

### kit-making
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  1936 Mar 28 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/214 With them takiri muka or whitau is pahuhuhu [fn Terms for the preparation of flax.] and they have a slight variation in the technique of kit-making. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/214</i>) [NOTES: 1936 Mar 28 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/214 (Ngata)]

### kitchen
 
 <i>n.</i> ?meat for cooking; ?a cooked meal

>  <b>HEiNZ</b> ... and with the money purchased a piece of pork which is the only `kitchen' i have had for three weeks past. ... <i>2 July</i>. ... kindled the fire, got breakfast, tea and bread - still no money to buy `kitchen' and not enough to pay for the provisions i have gotten from Duncan Clark. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,96</i>) [NOTES: 1863 June 25 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 96]

### kitchen evening
 
 <i>n.</i> a party to which guests bring gifts of kitchen-ware for a bride-to-be (See [also kitchen party](kee-kiw.md#kitchen-party))

>  A kitchen evening was given by Mr. and Mrs. A.R. Gillett at their residence ... in honour of Miss Sabina Gardner, whose marriage ... to Mr. P. Richardson takes place shortly. <i>(Mar 22, 1931,Auckland  Star,7/2</i>)

>  Friends.. rallied around when i returned home just prior to our marriage and gave us a marvellous kitchen evening. <i>(Mar 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter</i>)

>  The parish hall was well filled on Wednesday when a kitchen evening.. was tendered to Miss M. Ansell and Mr. Schou, who are to be married shortly. <i>(Aug 21, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

### kitchen party
 
 <i>n.</i> = kitchen evening

>  A number of the young ladies of the district met.. the object being a "kitchen party" to Miss Lawrence, prior to her marriage. <i>(Jun 18, 1919,Otago Witness,27</i>)

### kitchen tea
 
 <i>n.</i> a party to which guests bring gifts of kitchenware for a bride-to-be; also called a (kitchen) shower or kitchen evening.

>  <b>ELDRED-GRiGG</b> A kitchen tea is where you invite the girls round, and they all bring a present - some fucking nicknack for you to put in your kitchen. Then you drink a bit of sherry. Then you play games! And you giggle! <i>(1995,Mum,254</i>)

>  Every woman over 35 will remember attending countless pantry teas, kitchen teas, laundry teas. <i>(Jan 28, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  We flip back in time to the kitchen tea and the day of the wedding. <i>(Feb 14, 2001,Capital Times,8</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Trixie gave a `linen tea' for the bride-elect, and a `kitchen tea' and a "china tea' followed, each making some contribution to the household equipment ... <i>(1948,Rusty Road,179,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> The Stag Night has no educative role, as does its female counterpart, the Kitchen Tea. <i>(1980,Wedded Bliss in $188</i>)

>  <i>kitchen tea n. Austral. & N.Z</i>. a party held before a wedding to which guests bring items of kitchen equipment as wedding presents. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,620</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 413, KiTCHEN EVENiNG (or PARTY or TEA); 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 273]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>kitchen tea</i> female version of stag party on eve of wedding, usually not especially alcoholic and usually where sensible kitchen or domestic presents are given to the bride-to-be. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,66</i>)

>  Gift parties for the bride were given by bridesmaid Esther (whose party took the form of a kitchen tea). <i>(Apr 12, 1947,Sports Post,9</i>)

>  [They] gave a delightful "kitchen tea" recently in honour of [their guest] whose marriage is to take place shortly. <i>(Feb 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,57</i>)

### kitchen tidy
 
 <i>n.</i> a receptacle for kitchen refuse; a small rubbish tin with a lid, mainly opening with a foot pedal.

### kitchen whizz
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  in the Mackenzie Country, the RCD liberation front blended infected rabbit livers into what would come to be known as the "kitchen whizz" variety of RCD. <i>(Sep 27, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### kite
 
 <i>n.</i> stomach

>  We should have men go forth, with well-filled kites, at the sound of the 8' o'clock bell, joyfully to their appointed tasks. <i>(1964,Saturday Review,2/7</i>) [NOTES: 1864-67 Saturday Review 2/7 (J.G.S. Grant)]



 
 <i>n.</i> variant of kete

>  <b>SMiTH</b> We ... were enlivened by a good hour's <i>tangi </i>between our guide and the woman ... at the end of which our guide ... knocked off, and fell upon a <i>kite</i> of potatoes ... [ed. fn A bag-shaped basket woven of flax, cabbage-tree leaves, &c.; frequently called a `Maori kit'. <i>(Jan 13, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 13 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 360]



 
 <i>n.</i> a sail, a spinnaker [1980 Lynfield College, Auckland (Goldie Brown Collection) to fly a kite: to hoist a spinnaker (yachting); to do something useless <i>go fly a kite</i>: get lost, go away]

### kite high
 
 <i>phr.</i> (be driven/knocked) kite high: sky high, to the end of one's limit, etc.

>  ... he is sure to bang in upon you with some beastly earthly concern which simply knocks you `kite high'. <i>(1897,Tom Bracken's Annual,2/48</i>)

>  He was knocked kite high, however, when the owner of the pocket-book, a man of means, sent along 2s 6d for the recovery of his pocket-book. <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,195/4/8</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> ... he [the boar] tossed this dog kite high as soon as he went near him, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,93,1954 ed.</i>)

>  [Jim the Milker, Taranaki] But i must tell you that the cocky that i work for ain't a bad sort of chap, but he is driven kite-high by a joker that comes around ... to collect his intrest. [sic] <i>(Jun 9, 1906,NZ Truth,51-5-8</i>)

### kite winder
 
 <i>n.</i> (type of stair)

>  <i>Winder, kite-</i> in a stair the central winder of three winders forming a right angle, so called because in plan it looks like a kite. <i>(1979,Sage Tea,130</i>)

### kite-full
 
 <i>n.</i> a good or full meal, a gut-bash (cf kite n1.). [ca 1951 Wellington Teachers' College. (C.D. Orsman)]

### kitey
 
 <i>adj.</i> 1. Angry, esp. get kitey--> (cf kite--> n2.). \2. Stuck-up, ?ikey. [ca 1930 (Havelock) Jane Morison]

### kitful
 
 <i>n.</i> basketful (See [kit](kee-kiw.md#kit))

>  <b>J. WHiTE</b> Went to fish, caught a kit full. <i>(1856,Journal,62</i>)

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> in this hole they [sc Maoris] put some kindling, ... put a kitful of stones about as large as one's fist on top of the faggots, and light the kindling. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,36,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 36]

>  <b>HAY</b> [The Maori] stops and ... examines the kitful of fruit. <i>ibid.</i> 2/175 As proof of the re-establishment of cordial relations, kitsful of peaches, melons, kumera, taro, and other gifts were carried by the party. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/38</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> i will give you a kitful when you go away. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,161</i>) [NOTES: (Northland)]

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> ... you're positively reeking of beer. i can only suspect that Barney, as usual, had a kitful of bottles. <i>(1975,Anna of Strathallan,24</i>)

>  So far the natives have been very successful, one old woman getting a large kitful every day. <i>(Dec 6, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

### kitset
 
 <i>n.</i> the package of components for building an article, model, etc. [(cf. Brit. kit); the components for building an article such as a radio 1963, 1966 (Weekly News).]

>  <i>Transistor Radio Kitsets .... </i> <i>(May 8, 1963,Weekly News,56/5</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> it was indicative of his dedicated approach to modelling that he shunned plastic kitsets as being far too small for the detail he craved ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,22</i>)

>  The kitset of material includes the outline for these four studies - which are similar in style to those [of] ... interView' 69. <i>(Jun 28, 1972,NZ Tablet,6/4</i>)

>  <i>kitset n. NZ</i> set of parts sold together from which whole object can be made. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,414</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>WEDDE</b> The boat was a kitset double-kelson dory <i>(1986,Symme's Hole,79</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> Chester wrapped the kit set Lancaster bomber with a tube of model cement, then pushed the parcel across the counter. <i>(1986,Weaver's Apprentice, The,122</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> Chester wrapped the kit set Lancaster bomber with a tube of model cement, then pushed the parcel across the counter. <i>(1986,Weaver's Apprentice, The,122</i>)

>  the car comes in three collapsible parts like a kitset/ of ego and id and superego ... <i>(Jun 1986,Landfall,158</i>) [NOTES: (M.Edmond)]

>  A kit-set confessional ready for removal - could make a good shed or cupboard for tools and implements. <i>(Nov 15, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>) [NOTES: 1986 October Cathedral Bulletin in 1986 Nov 15 Listener 5]

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> i've a sense of myself, kitset man, assembled in a jiffy <i>(1970,O'Leary's Orchard,137</i>) [NOTES: (Riley's Handbook)]

### kitt
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a contrivance used as an anchor in the saddle of a bucking horse.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> kitt: bundle of sticks, usually willow or korari, wrapped in an old blanket and strapped across the saddle as an aid to staying on a bucking horse. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,47</i>)

### kitten
 
 <i>n.</i> Young of the Australian opossum, <i>Trichosurus vulpecula</i> [ca 1946 R. Mason. 1954 Mar. 2 [Oposs. Trapper's Letter to NZ Forest Service] ...I include all small opossums, kittens & so on.] (See [cf. joey](../J#joey))

### kitty
 
 <i>n.</i> for small amounts of money

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> i suggested he should hang up a `kitty' for the Wanganui Hospital, so that those who felt grateful for the accommodation might have an opportunity of expressing it in a more substantial form than words. <i>(1871,Casual Ramblings,37</i>)

### kittywake gull
 
 <i>n.</i> = black-billed gull

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Black Billed Gull (Kittywake Gull) ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,241,1954 ed.</i>)

### kiwakiwa
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Blechnum fluviatile</i>

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Blechnum fluviatile</i> KiWAKiWA ...Rosettes of olive-green fronds ... Common ... on banks, streamsides and in gullies. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,464</i>)

### kiwano
 
 <i>n.</i> Cucumis metaliferus (fam. Cucurbitaceae) [African] horned melon, jelly melon, African horned cucumber. From South Africa. [Registered trademark name for the African horned melon, Cucumis metaliferus (fam. Cucurbitaceae), a native of the Kalahari Desert introduced to New Zealand around 1920, the name invented by grower Sharyn Morris to combine Kiwi, 'an' (for its delicate banana flavour) and 'o' (as in the endings of other exotics such as pepino, babaco, tamarillo); also known as Mexican fruit salad, spiny cucumber and jelly melon.]

>  Kiwanos even attained a top shelf image in their first major export season - they were on sale in the exclusive Harrods in London... Morris even has an experimental batch of Kiwano wine "fermenting out the back". <i>(Feb 1986,Marketing,26</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Kiwano from Kaukapakapa... For each horned bump on its attractive, football-shaped skin, the kiwano has almost as many names. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Gardener,15</i>)

>  The interesting article on the 'Kiwano' in the September issue.. reminds us of how many similar plants can be grown in New Zealand where the climate is suitable. <i>(Dec 1985,NZ Gardener,25</i>)

>  For the future, keep your eyes open for kiwanos, okra, pepino, bitter melons and asparagus beans. <i>(Dec 1990,Cuisine,98</i>)

>  The family of Cucumis metuliferus is alive and well in Kaukapakapa, North Auckland, but they could be classed as undesirables and possibly prohibited from travelling to the United States. Cucumis metuliferus is the botanical name for a relatively new development in New Zealand horticulture. it is the kiwano, sometimes known as horned melon, African horned cucumber, or jelly melon, and is thought to have originated in tropical Africa. <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Evening Post,30</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of the African horn melon.

>  John says there are about 20 Kiwano growers in New Zealand at present. The Kiwano is susceptible to cold .. a New Zealand bred cultivar of the African Horn Melon. <i>(Oct 1987,Growing Today,33</i>)

### Kiwi
 
 <i>v.</i> polish (with Kiwi brand shoe polish)

>  Wherever Kiwi polish was sold, people started to say they were going to "kiwi their boots" rather than polish them, he says. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Dominion,19</i>)

>  <b>WOLFE</b> Even today, with the arrival of kiwi-inspired patriotism, New Zealanders do not 'Kiwi' their boots, as is the expression in England. <i>(1991,Kiwi,53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a ship

>  A sister ship of the Kiwi, she had been the Kiwi's partner in a glorious fight off Guadalcanal with one of Japan's large submarines, and the shots which had finally sealed the doom of the unfortunate submarine had been hers. <i>(Jun 28, 1943,NZEF Times,iii/105 : 3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand English

>  <b>MARSH</b> it is good honest kiwi to kick the English language into the gutter .. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,202</i>)

>  We generally laughed as he practised his Kiwi or micked me or spoke Cockney <i>(Sep 1983,Landfall,147/277</i>)

>  Names ... are supplied, as is what the author calls `A Crash Course in Kiwi', a linguistic guide for the unwary. Elizabeth Hansen cites the example of the B&B proprietor who inquired, `Shall i knock you up in the morning?' it was some moments before she realised that all he was planning to do was wake her. <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,27</i>)

>  <b>Allen Curnow</b> Those native Accents free and wild, That Well of Kiwi undefiled! <i>(2005,Whim Wham's New Zealand</i>) [NOTES: (poem originally published 1956)]

>  We've now translated it into 'Kiwi'. <i>(Jul 26, 1992,Onslow Parish Newsletter,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ soldier [WW2 usage is rare in this sense and seems applied as a descriptive term by outsiders rather than used by NZ troops themselves: early in WW2 used as distinct from Digger (rather appropriated by Australians) and WW1 Anzac. Possibly first applied by non-New Zealand units, and adopted as appropriate, as a noun, as an attributive and (occas.) as a term of address. Often humorously pronounced [kaiwai], perhaps in imitative guying of American pronunciations.] (See [cf. Digger](dig-dou.md#digger),[Anzac](all-ari.md#anzac))

>  We christened the Adjutant `Kiwi' - the symbol of En-Zed. <i>(Jun 21, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,225</i>)

>  <i>`Aussies' and `Diggers'.</i> [heading] ... we ... were attached to the `Aussies' for a while. ... [in an estaminet a French girl] ran about pouring beer, and calling every New Zealander `Kiwi', and each Australian `Kangaroo', sometimes prefaced by an adjective. <i>(Aug 11, 1919,Quick March,19</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> [in] Lemnos - drill broken by trials of prowess between the New Zealanders and the Second, First, and Fourth Australians. The Kiwis took the [137] Kangaroos at Rugby football ... <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,136</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> Then, recognizing the ... heavy uncertainty of a soldier just out of uniform, his voice changed. `Haven't got all day, Kiwi!' <i>(1949,it Was So Late,43,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> i squatted beside the piece of baize. The Maori did not twitch a muscle of his face. ... i said, `This is a raid. Fold up your board, Kiwi, for half an hour.' [WW2 troopship] <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,138</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> That six-pointed medal is my Pacific Star. ... `Let's get airborne, Kiwi,' my [RZNAF] mate Ted would say to me. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ soldier [(WW1 and 2)]

>  <b>HAYDON</b> Watch the antics of the `Aussies' and `Kiwis' <i>(1941,NZ Soldiers in England,8</i>)

>  [see pron Kaiwai] <i>(Aug 2, 1942,2NZEF Times,6</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The kiwi, like the fernleaf, is now a New Zealand national emblem and provided the name by which Dominion servicemen were popularly known during the war. (The term `Anzac' correctly applies to veterans of World War i.) >  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The [Government Bank Note] Designs Committee [1933] decided to give prominence to the <i>kiwi</i>, ... firstly because it was distinctive of the country, and secondly because of a suggestion that the overworked term `pound' did not accurately describe the New Zealand measure of value in relation to [other pounds]. it was suggested that an appropriate substitute would be the term <i>kiwi</i> which was in line with a current official proposal to use `rand' for the South African unit, and an unofficial proposal to use `roo' for the Australian unit. <i>ibid..</i> 274 in view of the then active advocacy of a decimal system of age, and the fact that the florin was a `decimal ' introduced in Great Britain in 1849 ... the [1933] age designs Committee decided that the kiwi should be associated with this , firstly because the term `kiwi' had been suggested as a possible name for the new decimal unit of value for New Zealand, and secondly because s are frequently described colloquially by the designs they carry. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,181</i>)

 <i>n.</i> proprietary or trade name esp. for a boot polish (? because brown) [[HWO writes 1984 to enquirer: I have an idea that <i>Kiwi</i>, the name of its competitor bootpolish, was given to that product in England before the First World War, because it was originally a brown boot polish. It's amusing that <i>nugget</i> should become a well-known word in New Zealand for bootpolish, and <i>Kiwi</i> merely remain the name of a brand here. One might expect that it would have been the other way around.]]

### kiwi
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a group of flightless native birds. it is a national emblem of New Zealand. See APTERYX. See also ROA. [cf. emu, roa in order of size, tip of bill to tip of tail,(Moncrieff): <i>A. oweni</i> LITTLE GREY KIWI, kiwi pukupuku, kiwi hoihoi; South Island. <i> A. mantelli</i>, NORTH ISLAND KIWI, Kiwi kura, kiwi nui; k. parure; north of Lat. 40 south. <i>A. haasti</i>, (cont.)LARGE GREY KIWI, kiwi karuwai, also ROA, ROAROA (cf EMU, MOUNTAIN KIWI); South Isd forests. <i>A. australis</i> SOUTH ISLAND KIWI, Toko(w)eka; West South Island, Stewart Island.] (See [cf. emu](egg-ens.md#emu),[roa](red-roa.md#roa))

[549 1870] if the Roa-Roa is in the country, mark my words, i will find him. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,548</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Oct 13 William Docherty in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 548; 549]

>  The cry of the kiwi is not heard till nightfall, or, as the digger expressed it technically but truthfully, `not till the night shift comes on.' <i>(1872,TrNZi,5/189</i>) [NOTES: (Potts: West Coast)]

>  <b>HAY</b> Pigeons, nestors, parrots, rail, kiwi, swamp-fowl, water-fowl, owls, parson-birds, all these and more were eaten; while the native dogs and rats were held to be great dainties. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Kiwi, the Apteryx ..... Struthionidae; three species: 1. Apt. Australis; 2. Apt. Mantelli; 3. Apt. Owenii. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/338</i>)

>  Kakapos, kiwis, and roas, are plentiful on the south side of the river, and there are plenty of penguins on the north-east side of it. <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/485</i>) [NOTES: (Sutherland)]

>  <i>A. haasti.</i> - Roaroa, Kiwi, Karuai. South island, in Alpine regions. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/308</i>)

>  <b>MOFFATT</b> [pl kiwi] Next day [near Lake Rotoroa, Nelson] i caught some woodhens and enjoyed the first good meal for a week. i also saw a few ducks and heard some kiwi. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,68,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Kiwi, n. <i>Maori name for a wingless struthious bird of New Zealand, the Apteryx</i> (q.v.), so called from the note of the bird. The species are.Large Grey Kiwi (Roa roa, generally shortened to <i>Roa,</i> q.v.). <i>Apteryx haastii</i>, Potts. Little Grey K.- <i>A oweni</i>, Gould. North island K.- <i>A. bulleri</i>, Sharpe. South island K.- (Tokoeka) <i>A. australis</i>, Shaw and Nodder. <i>(1898,Austral English,250</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> There are three varieties of this singular bird [the Apteryx] in Westland, the roa [F.N. ? A. australis], the kiwi [F.n.? A. Oweni], and another which i have called the giant kiwi [F,.n. or Mountain Kiwi, A haasti]. <i>ibid.</i> 228 <i>The Mountain Kiwi </i> i have very little to say regarding this bird, as i have only seen two of them, and being pushed with hunger, i ate the pair of them, under the circumstances i would have eaten the last of the Dodos. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,225</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Douglas Birds of South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 225]

>  <i>kiwi n</i>. any bird of the genus Apteryx (found in New Zealand). [Maori imit. fr. its cry] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,7</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [Stewart island birds include] the fern-bird (<i>Bowdleria fulva</i>), the bell-bird (<i>Demiegretta sacra</i>), Weka <i>Ocydromus</i>) and Kiwi (<i>Apteryx</i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,323</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kiwifruit

>  <b>BURTON</b> `Kiwifruit' is an embarrassingly contrived name, but as it is now accepted worldwide it seems here to stay. Thankfully, however, the name has been shortened to plain `Kiwi' in our major markets in the United States and Germany, ... <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,138</i>)

>  <b>R. ROBiNSON</b> eg `One kiwi please' or `a New Zealand kiwi, not a Californian'. <i>(Apr 1984,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (occasionally with NZ attached)]

>  ... the kiwis are bursting into flower ... <i>(Oct 26, 1985,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1985 26 Oct National Radio M.A.F. Report from Northland 7.35 a.m. in the farming programme]



 
 <i>n.</i> the bird as national emblem [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BARR</b> [a poem <i>What `Kiwi' Means. Said Leo: </i>`What's this ... thats broke my blessed head?/ it's called out here `The Kiwi' - alluding to N.Z.,/ it serves this small Dominion as an emblem for a crest, ...' <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,54</i>)

>  The kiwi (q.v.) as a national emblem is of comparatively recent date. it was used after 1911 in the badge of the 2nd South Canterbury (Territorial) Regiment and became widely known from the giant kiwi carved on the chalk hill above Sling Camp, England, during the First World War. After 1940 the kiwi became synonymous with New Zealand servicemen overseas. The Kiwi Concert Party, which toured many battle areas during the Second World War, and the Kiwi (New Zealand Army) Football Team, which toured the British isles, France, and Germany in 1945-46, greatly enhanced the emblem's popularity. ... More recently, the kiwi has become the emblem used by New Zealand rugby league representative teams. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/571</i>)

>  <b>GRANT</b> [Lloyd of the New Zealand Herald was the first cartoonist to widely use the kiwi to symbolise New Zealand: cartoons of 1910, 1915 illustrate it here.] <i>(1980,Unauthorised Version,96</i>)

>  One is so frequently exposed to the over-used, hackneyed word `Kiwi' that one is getting sick and tired of hearing it. ... From its legitimate Polynesian use for an endemic bird, it has now been stretched to include not only human beings, but lottery tickets, sportspeople, fruit, and now the dollar itself. <i>(Oct 7, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the New Zealand florin.

>  Kiwi ... has also become the name of the New Zealand florin, because of the kiwi on the obverse. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Names]

>  <i>kiwi</i>. The Supplement records another meaning: `a non-flying member of the air-force,' - which is good. Kiwi is now a common name of the footballing Newzealander: it has also become the name of the Newzealand florin, because of the kiwi on the obverse. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/148</i>)

### Kiwi bear
 
 <i>n.</i> a possum

>  `i believe that the member for Tarawera is referring to the possum (Trichosurus vulpecula), not opossums (genus Didelphis) which are American marsupials,' [Agriculture Minister Moyle] said in his written reply. ... And for the name, you can be forgiven if you call our imported possum (the `brushtail possum,' to be precise) an opossum. The Aussie possum - there are about six types across the Tasman - looked so much like the American counterpart when first discovered by Europeans that it was called an opossum. As recently as 1981, scientists who turned up for the first New Zealand symposium on marsupials ... were sagely talking about possums in 12 of the papers presented while their colleagues talked about opossums in six papers. All were referring to the same nocturnal nuisance. One of the great achievements of that symposium was to finish up talking the same language after taking a vote that favoured for possum. ... The fur trade is pushing for `NZ phalanger' (a generic name for a group of marsupials) while possum stew exporters are trying to win acceptance for `Kiwi bear' in Oriental restaurants. <i>(Jun 28, 1988,Auckland Star,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Chinese hunger for 'Kiwi bear' may earn millions. China has expressed an interest in buying possum meat - once known there as Kiwi bear... Ten months before the 1987 share-market crash, the Kiwi Bear Company was formed to farm and market possum meat. <i>(Apr 25, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Keeley's Bush Farmyard Holiday Park runs night-time bush walks where tourists see wallabies and possums, or Kiwi bears as they tell visitors. <i>(Jun 2, 2001,Dominion,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

### Kiwi polish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAiTE</b> The most ubiquitous person [in Egypt in WW1] was easily the bootblack. ... `Bootsa clean, sir! no good, no money; Kiwi polish, sir!' <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,40</i>)

>  <b>KiWi POLiSH CO.</b> Most of these newly-enlightened individuals first learned about the Kiwi when soldiers of New Zealand, Australia and Britain, carried this show polish with them to all parts of the world. in sharing their polish with allies, they also shared information. This seems to explain how the term `Kiwi' came to be applied so generally not only to New Zealanders, but to other Anzacs as well. <i>ibid..</i> 10 in 1906, John Ramsay's son, William, had developed [in Melbourne] an unusually fine bootpolish to which he gave the name `Kiwi'. The choice of the name `Kiwi' as a trademark was a tribute to William's wife who was a native of New Zealand, home of the Kiwi bird. ... Millions of men under arms [in WW1], meant millions of tan [Nb colour] boots to be polished ... <i>(1950,Story of the Kiwi, The,7</i>)

>  The Kiwi share agreement which was promoted as being "set on stone" to protect consumers' interests in the phone market appears to be nearing the end of its life. <i>(May 24, 2000,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  [Telecom's] obligation under the Kiwi share is to maintain the rural network to voice-quality standards... Telecom says the Kiwi share was introduced in 1990 when its network carried almost solely voice traffic, but today just over half its traffic is to the internet. <i>(May 24, 2000,Dominion,26</i>)

>  At present, residential line charges are protected by the fabled Kiwi share, under which Telecom guarantees not to raise residential charges faster than inflation, unless profits are unreasonably affected. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Options include a Kiwi share binding the company to undertakings; reduced rates.. and a council-funded consumer watchdog. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,9</i>)

>  its provisions need to be able to be updated - it [the Kiwi share] should be regarded as a living document, a bit like the Treaty of Waitangi.... Technology progress meant the Kiwi share needed to be renegotiated. <i>(Aug 4, 2000,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  There was a "Kiwi share", various rights ceded to the New Zealand Government so it would retain some say in the airline's affairs, particularly which foreign airlines could buy in, and how much they could own. <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Telecom says plan to disband its four regional operating companies will not alter its Kiwi Share obligations... The Rocs are at the heart of the Kiwi Share - a statement enshrined in the company's articles of association when Telecom was sold to Bell Atlantic and Ameritech in 1990. it was an attempt by the then Labour government to protect the interests of the New Zealand public. <i>(Oct 5, 1992,Dominion infotech Weekly,1</i>)

>  The Kiwi Share is simply a multi-faceted statement written into the articles of association bought, along with the company, by Telecom's new owners... Telecom has made no secret of its desire to extricate itself from the Kiwi Share restraints... Tampering with the wording of the Kiwi Share may appear cosmetic to some but there are others who will take a dim view of any attempt to do so. <i>(Oct 5, 1992,Dominion infotech Weekly,4</i>)

>  He says Kiwi Share discussion are under way with Telecom at the moment, relating, among other things, to whether the Share should be "contractural, constitutional, or legislative". <i>(Aug 6, 2001,NZ infotech Weekly,No. 501 : 1</i>)

>  Telecom continued to insist that dial-up internet was not a normal use of the telephone - not insofar as it held Telecom to its Kiwi Share obligation to offer free local calling, anyway... So it was that just before Christmas the new Kiwi Share was passed into law, not only recognising internet calls, but also obliging Telecom to guarantee minimum data speeds for all of its lines. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  [The Broadcasting Minister] said a "Kiwi share" would require the new owners of the community stations to offer their assets and frequencies for sale if they closed the stations. <i>(Jul 20, 1995,Dominion,7</i>)

### kiwiana
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka Kiwiana]

>  What about that plastic bag - is that Kiwiana?.. in 1989... [Richard Wolfe] and Stephen Barnett co-authored a book called <i>New Zealand! New Zealand! in Praise of Kiwiana</i>. <i>(Feb 4, 1998,NZ Herald,G10</i>)

>  Kiwiana as a term was coined in the late 1980s, several generations after it had infiltrated the villas and bungalows of New Zealand. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/20</i>)

>  Best of all, though, is an exhibition based on the book <i>New Zealand! New Zealand! in Praise of Kiwiana</i> by Stephen Barnett and Richard Wolfe. <i>(Aug 13, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kiwiana en pointe <i>(Jun 7, 2001,Dominion,19</i>)

>  Selwyn was an icon. He was Kiwiana and it was a real privilege to work with him. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Particularly striking are... David Geary's dynamic and irresistibly incisive piece of Kiwiana, bubbling with one-liners ("like Becket, as she got older she got more succinct"); Lloyd Jones's informative and entertaining reflection on Wellington in the 70s ("... a precious number of BYOs were starting up. These eateries were Wellington's equivalent of the samizdat press inn the Eastern bloc, promoted by word of mouth and subject to police raids.") = From Sport 21 <i>(Dec 5, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  To [ian Wedde\} kiwiana is "a term that's been invented by us to be able to describe a range of cultural items that help to define some kind of sense of national identity"... But he says kiwiana doesn't encompass the "complexity and the kind of gnarliness" of our culture. it's not popular culture. it's minority culture, and a very small minority culture."... As for Wedde's own kiwiana possessions - any paua shell collections, punga vases? "i guess it would be my gas barbecue, or Skilsaw. They're associated with a very Kiwi way of life and have a quality that's, sort of, very familiar." <i>(Aug 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  The numerous little jokes about the sacred cows of Kiwiana are as sharp and telling as the strands of a barbed wire fence. <i>(Aug 7, 1992,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  But some people still prefer pure Kiwiana -- a fibrolite box where a bit of sand doesn't ruin the white walls. <i>(Feb 22, 2006,NZ Herald,Summer Escape (insert) H2</i>)

>  in an increasing dog-eat-dog society, Spot represents the lost values of caring Kiwiana. <i>(Dec 15, 1991,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)

>  it follows that the icons of Kiwiana, so many of them drawn from advertising's rose-coloured world, amount to a fairly one-sided view of New Zealand. <i>(Aug 3, 1990,North & South,12</i>)

>  <b>WOLFE</b> The <i>de rigeur</i> furnishing of Lynn and Gary's lifestyles encompass what is loosely called 'Kiwiana'. <i>(1991,Kiwi,73</i>)

>  The friendliness of the waiting staff, the informal, relaxed ambience and the kiwiana decor lends the feeling of being hosted by friends rather than strangers. <i>(Aug 2005,Capital Times,17-23 Aug / 20</i>)

>  Held together with everything from baling twine to No 8 wire, the iconic and much loved Kiwi bach is pure Kiwiana, with many occupying Conservation Department land - but not for much longer if the department has its way. <i>(May 22, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

They were the palest green she had ever seen, green like thinly sliced kiwi fruit". <i>(Sep 28, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  The `Kiwi clobbering machine' comes down hard on those who stick their heads above the dingy crowd ... Sportsmen like Sir Edmund Hillary and Colin Meads become archetypes of sainted Kiwihood, their virtues deemed to be all-embracing ... <i>(Dec 27, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,6</i>)

### KiwiHost
 
 <i>n.</i> a national customer service training programme

>  He said KiwiHost courses were designed to improve the knowledge base of staff and to help them build long-lasting relationships with business customers. ... KiwiHost provides all resources for the courses including a book which participants retain for future reference. <i>(Sep 7, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6</i>)

>  it's no coincidence that Ray and all his staff have completed KiwiHost - New Zealand's only nationwide customer service training programme. "KiwiHost reinforced my team's commitment to good service," he said. <i>(Apr 29, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

### kiwikiwi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> Natives never ascend this mountain alone, it is consequently pathless, and is the resort of the <i>Kiwi-kiwi</i> bird, lizards, and innumerable birds, ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/235</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>kiwikiwi</i>, or Apteryx Australis, placed under the head of Struthionidae, by Mr Gould ... <i>ibid </i>303 The present kiwikiwi, so named from the note of its voice, is about the size of a large duck, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/301</i>)

>  i was however so lucky as to get ... a specimen of the Kivi-Kivi. <i>(May 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,222,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> `Kievi-kievi' bird ... it is called kiwi-kiwi from its peculiar note... Mats made of dogs' skins sewed together, were displayed in great number, and a small garment of the feathers of the Kievi-kievi bird was sported with great pride and state by the wearer. [fn] Kievi-kievi (<i>Apteris australis</i>) is covered with a hairy feather, similar to the skin and feathers of the Cassowary, and like that family (<i>Rhea</i>) is destitute of the accessary plume. <i>ibid.</i> 1/97 fn it is called Kiwi-kiwi from its peculiar note. it burrows in the ground, the powerful spur on its leg assisting the bird. it is found throughout the country, especially near the East Cape. (<i>Wai appu</i>.) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/96 and fns.</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> The kiwi, or kiwi-kiwi, as it is called by the natives, inhabits the deepest recesses of the forest. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/231</i>)

>  <i>kiwi n</i> -s [Maori, of imit. origin] 1 also <i>kiwi-kiwi : </i> a flightless New Zealand bird of the genus <i>Apteryx</i> that is about the size of a domestic chicken, has very rudimentary wings, stout legs, a long straight or slightly curved bill with nostrils near the tip, and hairlike plumage of various shades of gray and brown, nests in burrows, and lays eggs as large as one fourth its weight which are incubated by the male<i> 2 : usu cap </i>: NEW ZEALANDER - used as a nickname <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1247</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the herb <i>Blechnum fluviatile</i>

>  <b>STARK</b> KiWi KiWi <i>Blechnum fluviatile </i> This herb is found in damp, rather hilly woodlands of New Zealand. <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,38</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Blechnum fluviatile</i> (R.Br.) Salom. Maori name: <i>Kiwikiwi </i> ... Found throughout New Zealand in damp hilly forest. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,26</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  You could.. imagine one circle to be white and the other black, and then of course the overlapping area would be grey, or kiwikiwi. <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,82</i>)

### Kiwiland
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand (See [cf. Kiwi-Country](kee-kiw.md#kiwi-country),[Maoriland](m-mar.md#maoriland))

>  it's in the regions of heartland Kiwiland that 'real lunch' food reigns. <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Just how many names for <i>Kiwiland</i> (including that one) have there been? <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4 : 1</i>)

>  in this colloquial set are the predictable <i>Kiwiland</i> (land of kiwis and / or Kiwis) which surprisingly appears to date only from the 1940s, but now enjoys regular journalistic and colloquial currency. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> i'll be going back to good old Kiwiland when the ship pulls out. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,396</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> This is Kiwiland, see. And we have gentleman's agreement here ... <i>(1964,Watersiders,47</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> `That's 1922,' said Kelvin. `See the dayth above the tunnel.' `Dayth' for `date', just like my brother Michael and Dad saying `fillum' for `film'. it is the irish way, quite common in Kiwiland. <i>(1985,G'day Country,97</i>)

>  At the end of the twentieth century the opportunities for civilised dining in Kiwiland are greater then ever. <i>(Feb 21, 1986,National Business Review,19</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> You were the local boy who'd made good in Kiwiland ... <i>(1989,Frigate Bird,42</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> You were the local boy who'd made good in Kiwiland ... <i>(1989,Frigate Bird,42</i>)

### Kiwiness
 
 <i>n.</i> essential New Zealand quality

>  But if you want one where you're won over by the author's stoic, sturdy stoic, sturdy, stroppy Kiwiness, then hang around. <i>(Dec 4, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  [heading] Clinging to Kiwiness on a wave of Americanisation. <i>(Aug 24, 2005,NZ Herald,A15</i>)

>  And there is an ease about his Kiwiness that allows him to slip in mentions of rapper Savage, veteran rockers Hello Sailor and the Desert Road, without appearing self-conscious. <i>(Oct 16, 2010,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### Kiwiosity
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ziel's upbeat ragga-style approach contrasting with Eel and Earl's laconic Kiwiosity. <i>(Sep 18, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

### KiwiRail
 
 <i>n.</i> a state-owned rail system, re-purchased by the New Zealand Labour Government in July 2008 after it had been sold into private hands by a National Government in 1993.

>  RAiL and ferry services, to rebranded KiwiRail, will return to taxpayer hands today at a ceremony at Wellington railway station that will also unveil the new corporate colours .. The expected name - KiwiRail - continues the Government's branding theme that includes Kiwibank and KiwiSaver. <i>(Jul 1, 2008,Dominion Post,A1</i>)
